The image shows the front cover of a historical book or manuscript. It features a **half-binding** <note>a binding style where the spine and corners are reinforced with leather for durability, while the rest of the cover is made of paper or cloth</note> design with dark brown leather on the spine and at the corners. 

The main surface of the boards is covered in **marbled paper** <note>paper decorated using a technique that floats pigments on water to create stone-like patterns</note> with a mottled pattern of dark grey and purple splotches, interspersed with smaller flecks of cream, red, and blue. The leather is worn and scuffed, especially along the left edge where the spine begins, indicating frequent historical use.

<image-desc>A central rectangular card contains a colored oval emblem. The emblem depicts a pelican in her piety (a mother bird feeding three young chicks with blood from her own breast) resting upon a coat of arms. The shield of the coat of arms is blue (azure) with a white (argent) cross, accompanied by four red roses, one in each quarter. Gold rays shine from above the pelican. The oval image is encircled by a green border and the words "HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY" <note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"</note> in gold capital letters.</image-desc>

->**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**<-
<note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"; This refers to a philosophical and religious tradition based on the writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, which seeks to understand the hidden laws of the universe and the soul.</note>

<term>Hermetic Philosophy</term>: <note>original: "Philosophia Hermetica"</note> The study of ancient esoteric wisdom, often involving alchemy and mysticism.
<term>Pelican in her piety</term>: A symbolic image where a mother pelican wounds her breast to feed her young with her own blood. In this context, it represents self-sacrifice, the "Red Work" of alchemy, and the nurturing of spiritual knowledge.
<term>Rosicrucianism</term>: A mystical movement symbolized by the Rose and the Cross, suggesting a secret brotherhood of sages working for the "universal reformation" of mankind.
<term>Alchemy</term>: The art and science of transformation, both of physical matter (like lead into gold) and the spiritual self.
<term>Coat of arms</term>: A heraldic display; here, the cross and roses identify the work as part of the Rosicrucian tradition.
<term>Roses</term>: In this symbolism, the red roses typically represent the unfolding of the soul or the blood of the heart through spiritual trial.

<meta>This is the title page of a 1613 Dutch edition of Sebastian Castellio’s rebuttal of John Calvin. There is no previous page for continuity.</meta>

## A Short and Clear
# Refutation

Of that which was brought forward by <term>Mr. John Calvin</term><note>The famous Protestant reformer in Geneva (1509–1564).</note> to assert the power of the <term>Civil Authority</term><note>Originally "Overheyt," referring to the state or magistrates.</note> in the punishment of <term>Heretics</term><note>People whose religious beliefs contradict established church doctrine.</note>; in a certain little book by him entitled:

->Refutation of the errors of <term>Michael Servetus</term><note>A Spanish scholar burned at the stake in Geneva in 1553 for his views on the Trinity; his execution sparked an international debate on religious tolerance.</note>, where it is also<-
->taught that one must punish Heretics with the Sword,<-

Formerly written in Latin by the Pious and very learned <term>Sebastian Castellio</term><note>A French theologian and former friend of Calvin who became a leading voice for religious freedom and tolerance.</note>, and now faithfully translated into Dutch: and enriched with a Preface; Useful and profitable to read in these dangerous times.

->Philippians 2, verses 3 and 4.<-

> Do nothing through strife or vain glory: but through humility let each consider the other better than himself. And let each not look to his own, but let each also look to that which is another's, etc.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut printer's device or vignette featuring a central bearded face/mask integrated into a symmetrical design of scrolls, stylized foliage, and flourishes, roughly heart-shaped in overall silhouette.</image-desc>

### In the Year of Christ / 1613.

<summary>This title page introduces a Dutch translation of Sebastian Castellio’s defense of religious tolerance, written to challenge John Calvin's justification for the execution of Michael Servetus.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Michael Servetus, Sebastian Castellio, religious tolerance, heresy, Reformation, civil authority, 1613, Dutch history</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to a work advocating for religious tolerance, focusing on the hypocrisy of early Reformers who, after escaping persecution themselves, turned to persecuting others.</meta>

# PREFACE.

A ii
<meta>catchword: honor</meta>

->Beloved Reader,<-

For new diseases, new remedies are necessary; and when one sees people seized by old illnesses, one likewise brings forth the old medicines. The disease—or more accurately, the deadly sickness—of exercising lordship over another's conscience and faith was ancient and had reigned for a long time, bringing many people to their deaths in these lands and elsewhere. It was expected that with the Reformation of the Christian Church throughout Europe <note>The original Dutch *uytgenomen* here likely means "throughout" or "across" in this context, rather than "except for."</note> things would improve, as indeed happened in part. For those who were oppressed by it resisted with the sword of the Spirit <note>A biblical reference to Ephesians 6:17; here it means using scripture and argument rather than physical weapons.</note>, so powerfully warning the authorities against such persecution that the sickness was forced to clear out of many places, and elsewhere was so restrained that it had to dive for cover and hide itself. 

But what a strange thing! Those who had complained and cried out the most about this when they themselves were suffering under the press <note>original: *onder de Perse*; a metaphor for being under intense pressure or persecution.</note>, having now overcome their distress through the Spirit, have sought to finish in the flesh <note>A reference to Galatians 3:3: "Having begun in the Spirit, are you now being made perfect by the flesh?" This accuses the Reformers of starting with spiritual ideals but resorting to worldly force.</note> what they had well begun in the Spirit. They wished to hunt and drive the aforementioned plague of forced conscience (from which they were now freed by the grace of God) onto the necks of others, with no less bitterness than they themselves had recently experienced. This was to the great astonishment of all sensible people, and a notable warning to all cautious and modest authorities: that those who had fought so excellently for the freedom of faith—against the forcing of conscience and persecution for religious matters—as long as they were still hiding under the Cross <note>original: *onder het cruys*; a common 17th-century term for being a persecuted "underground" church.</note>, as soon as they sat somewhat more securely and had gained the secular sword <note>The power of the state to punish or execute.</note> on their side, they became a little whore of the world. <note>The author uses this harsh language to describe a church that abandons its principles to seek power from the state.</note> They very quickly forgot the sighing they did under the Cross, and having turned their coats, they abused the power of the authorities to execute the damnable doctrine of "killing heretics" <term>Ketterdooden: the practice of executing those deemed to hold false religious beliefs</term> against those who spoke against them, or who could not say "yes and Amen" to all their particular opinions.

<margin>Calvin himself admits this in his book against Servetus, page 695, in the latest edition, in folio.</margin> An example is Master John Calvin, the renowned teacher at Geneva, in his persecution of Michael Servetus, whom he arrested, put on trial, and had burned alive for the sake of his faith—or rather, for the sake of his unbelief. He calls Servetus’s unbelief "blasphemy." That is more than unbelief. I do not speak against that: unbelief is a great sin, and blasphemy even greater, I admit. I wish neither to excuse Servetus in the one nor the other, much less to defend his errors; indeed, it is better not to speak of them and let them be forgotten. This I say: Servetus’s act concerned faith, or more accurately unbelief; it concerned the understanding of Holy Scripture. He dishonored God (if indeed what Calvin writes is true, for we do not have Servetus’s books), though he intended to honor Him. It was a gross un—

<summary>This preface critiques the hypocrisy of Protestant Reformers like John Calvin who, after suffering under Catholic persecution, used state power to execute "heretics" like Michael Servetus. The author argues that religious disagreements and even "unbelief" should not be punished by the state.</summary>
<keywords>Religious Tolerance, Reformation, Persecution, John Calvin, Michael Servetus, Freedom of Conscience, Heresy, Geneva, State Power</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface, specifically discussing the case of Michael Servetus and the intellectual conflict between John Calvin and Sebastian Castellio regarding the execution of heretics. The author frames the struggle for religious freedom as a foundational element of the Dutch Republic's identity.</meta>

<meta>Handwritten "19." in top left corner; text printed in two columns</meta>

# PREFACE.

...understanding and harmful proposal; nonetheless, he knew no better. Otherwise, what madness would it be to allow himself to be put to such a seemingly orderly death for an opinion that he could have renounced if he had known better, thereby saving his life? He would rather die for his perceived truth than live with a gnawing (though poorly instructed) conscience. He believed he was honoring God with his death. 

You will say his intention was deceptive. Correct; nonetheless, he truly believed it and died for it. He cannot be convicted of any crime, rebellion, riot, or resistance against authority—at least not against the authorities in Geneva—to say that he was executed for such reasons. It was, then, purely for his opinion. You call it evil, ungodly, slanderous, and so on. I admit it; yet these were opinions he believed to be good, godly, and honorable to God, and which he sought to prove with Holy Scripture. Indeed, it seemed to him that he ought to give up his life to serve God, just as his opponents killed him to likewise serve God.

<margin>
The title of the book is *Refutation of the errors of Michael Servetus*, where it is also taught that one must punish heretics with the sword. See page 694.
</margin>

<column-break/>

The question is whether this is not an impermissible <term>compulsion of conscience</term> <note>original: "Conscientie-dwangh"; the act of forcing someone to act or believe against their internal moral or religious convictions.</note>, a lordship over faith, an inhuman cruelty—yes, pure tyranny? I cannot see it any other way. Calvin <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the influential French theologian and leader in Geneva.</note> sought to excuse his actions in public print and to defend the killing of heretics, attempting to prove that whoever dares to deny that heretics are rightfully punished by the government knowingly makes themselves guilty of the same crime. 

Beza <note>The text says "Besie," referring to Theodore Beza (1519–1605), Calvin's successor, who wrote "De haereticis a civili magistratu puniendis" in 1554.</note>, treating this matter even more extensively, seeks to prove that the Church is in great danger of total ruin if the magistrate <note>The civil government or local authorities.</note> overlooks the punishment of heretics. But both their tracts have been strongly refuted—or, if you prefer, an attempt was made to refute them—by, among others, the God-fearing and highly learned Sebastian Castellio <note>A French preacher and theologian (1515–1563) and an early proponent of religious toleration.</note>, whose tract against Calvin regarding this matter I provide for you here, so that you may judge with full knowledge. It is an old work and has been in private hands for a long time, but it was never made public through printing until recently, first in Latin and now translated and published by us.

You ask: why dig up the dead and bring this up now? I shall tell you. Is there any land under the sun where <term>heretic-killing</term> <note>original: "ketter-dooden."</note> (this is the proper name for the compulsion of conscience I am speaking of) has been more hated, abhorred, and driven out than this land, these free United Netherlands? If much has been suffered, if blood has been shed, if it has cost infinite treasure to be rid of this same beast, it happened here. For just as the Honorable and Mighty Lords <term>States General</term> <note>The assembly of representatives from the Dutch provinces.</note> (according to the clear declaration made to the Majesty of Great Britain in their resolution of October 1st, 1611) were forced to maintain the freedom, rights, and privileges of the Netherlands against those improper, tyrannical, and bloody procedures that fought against the consciences, bodies, and goods of the...

<summary>The author argues that executing people for their religious opinions is "pure tyranny" and introduces Sebastian Castellio's refutation of John Calvin's defense of heretic-killing. The text highlights the Dutch Republic's historical commitment to religious freedom as a hard-won victory against such persecution.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Servetus, John Calvin, Sebastian Castellio, Theodore Beza, compulsion of conscience, heretic-killing, religious toleration, States General, Dutch Republic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from the specific case of Servetus to the broader historical context of the Dutch Revolt. The author argues that religious freedom—specifically the rejection of the Inquisition and "conscience-forcing"—was the primary motivation for the Dutch people's long war against Spanish rule.</meta>

# PREFACE.

A iii

...of the good inhabitants of all qualities, practiced for many years by the Spaniards and their adherents (after long patience, a great number of <term>Remonstrances</term> <note>Formal statements of grievances or petitions presented to the authorities.</note> and <term>Requests</term>, and other diligence performed in vain, and the matter being beyond all remedy) to take up arms in their necessary defense, primarily also calling upon the Majesty of Great Britain and other Kings, Princes, and Republics for their assistance; and through their favor, they have for many years continuously had to bear burdensome and bloody wars, many great and bloody skirmishes, as well as notable battles by water and by land, unheard-of sieges of many cities, the scattering and devastation of lands and towns, and other hardships arising from war. With great steadfastness and moderation, both in prosperity and in adversity, their <term>Honorable Mightinesses</term> <note>Original: "EE. Mog." (Edel Mogende), a formal title for the Dutch governing officials.</note> always acknowledge that, specifically, the first and principal reason for taking and holding the aforementioned Resolution was the aforementioned tyranny over conscience, bodies, and goods, through the introduction of the <term>Inquisition</term> <note>The ecclesiastical tribunal used by the Spanish crown to suppress heresy through interrogation and often execution.</note> and the compulsion in matters of religion.

Therefore, it is more than reasonable that proper care be taken for this ground and foundation of our flourishing state, so that the same might not be undermined under a holy appearance of Divine zeal and Christian earnestness, or in any way weakened or impaired, nor through any of our neglect or inattentiveness in this laudable and by God so graciously blessed Republic,

<column-break/>

built and erected with such great effort, so much struggle, such excessive costs, and so much sweat and blood of pious heroes—lest too much ground be newly granted to that land-ruining opinion of persecuting for opinions, of ruling and dominating over human <term>Consciences</term>, and investigating that which the Lord has reserved for Himself alone. We, who have carried upright—unharmed and unbowed—that shining <term>Crown of Freedom</term> which God has sent us from high Heaven and placed upon our head, in spite of the Tyrant in the greatest anxieties and most precarious times; having now come to rest and being freed from the foreign noise of war, we ought now to work with united hearts and hands to set it upon a firm and immovable foundation, so that it might be left undiminished to our descendants. It should not be so quickly or so shamefully taken from us by a heap of foreign zealots and vehement drivers of their own opinions, nor should we allow a muzzle to be placed on the mouth of Truth—something that neither the cunning of the <term>Princess of Parma</term> <note>Margaret of Parma, who served as Governor of the Netherlands for Philip II before the war escalated.</note>, nor the cruelty of the <term>Duke of Alba</term> <note>A Spanish general known for his "Council of Blood" and harsh suppression of Dutch Protestants.</note>, nor the treasures and rich mines of the Indies, nor so many powerful allies of <term>King Philip</term> <note>Philip II of Spain, the primary antagonist of the Dutch Revolt.</note> had the power to achieve.

It was only a few years ago that one did not even speak of this monster among us; indeed, the name itself was scarcely—

<meta>catchword: spo- (The catchword in the OCR "ghe-" likely connects to a word like "ghesproken" or "ghelooft" on the next page, though the OCR shows "ghe-" at the bottom and the text ends with "ghe-")</meta>

<summary>The author frames the Dutch Revolt as a struggle primarily fought for "freedom of conscience" against Spanish tyranny and the Inquisition. He warns that this hard-won liberty is now threatened from within by "foreign zealots" who wish to bring back religious persecution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface's argument that the struggle for Dutch independence was rooted in religious freedom. The author warns that some within the Reformed movement are now attempting to reintroduce the same religious coercion they once fought against, using the metaphor of a wolf in sheep's clothing.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...less known to those who had not seen the first troubles, nor tasted the **Alvan cruelty** <note>Refers to the Duke of Alba, the Spanish governor known for his "Council of Blood" which executed thousands during the Dutch Revolt.</note>: One daily slandered the **Papists** <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics.</note> with such earnestness, because they were unwilling to enter into any dispute, nor to constantly take the field with the Word of God in a difficult struggle in the army of the **Militant Church** <note>Original: "strijdende Kercke" — the concept of the church on earth engaged in spiritual warfare against sin and error.</note>, but instead sought to bathe in luxury here in the world, safely under the protection of the steel sword! Not one in a thousand dared suspect that one would so quickly and shamelessly turn the page, seeking to force us back into those **old, worn-out, and pinching shoes** <note>A metaphor for the restrictive and painful religious laws of the Spanish Inquisition.</note> that we had only recently cast off with such great effort—and strive to erect a new, luxurious, and powerful Popedom. This was done so that those who rebuke the creeping errors or wish to investigate the truth would, in time, be placed back on the old footing: standing under the gallows with a noose around their neck, "disputing" with an executioner.

<margin>
Preface of their warning. a iij.
</margin>
But it has happened here just as the **Preachers of Leeuwarden** <note>A reference to local religious leaders in the province of Friesland.</note> say: that these heavy and hideous wolves (knowing well that they would be recognized and feared by the sheep if they immediately showed this gruesome hide, teeth, or the behavior of the wolf upon their arrival) have masterfully put the sheep's clothing over the wolf's skin. The uglier and more gruesome their hair and hide were, the more they covered themselves with beautiful wool; and wherever a small piece had accidentally peeked out, they immediately retracted it, or stretched the sheep's clothing, re-milled, patched, and adjusted it, until

<column-break/>

either God—mercifully and paternally lightening the chastisement of His Church—unexpectedly and against their will (or before their intended time) lifted the sheep’s clothing and exposed the **Wolf** as a warning to His sheep; or they themselves, finding everything ready in their opinion to overpower the sheep, cast off the sheep’s clothing and acted as true wolves in Christ’s sheepfold. And so, they slowly returned to the discarded sheep's clothing, now and then putting a patch of it back on, even pretending entirely as if they had turned from wolves into sheep, and as if they wished henceforth to live in peace with the sheep and be good sheep themselves, until another new opportunity arises. For the **wolf changes his hair, but not his nature.**

Following this, although from the beginning some holy **Theologians** among us—who nevertheless knew how to speak very beautifully like little sheep—considered this **tyrannical** and ruinous opinion of punishing all those they hold to be **heretics** with death (even when they cannot be deterred otherwise) to be good, indeed so necessary that the Church would be in danger of falling and collapsing if the **Magistrates** <note>The civil government and city officials.</note> turned a blind eye in this matter! Nevertheless, they were so ashamed to admit this, even when urged and forced in public disputes, since they well perceived that the common people, still bearing the wounds of such teaching with pain, hated that same doctrine with hostility: That the Magistrates still

<margin>
See the Leiden dispute with **Coornhert**, Year 1578.
</margin>

<summary>The author warns that certain religious leaders are attempting to reintroduce the same religious persecution (the "wolf in sheep's clothing") that the Dutch had originally fought to escape during the revolt against Spain. He highlights a 1578 dispute involving Dirck Volckertszoon Coornhert as evidence of these early tendencies toward "tyrannical" religious laws.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's argument regarding religious factions in the Netherlands, specifically warning against those who advocate for a strict "Genevan Inquisition" under the guise of piety.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...were not yet sufficiently imbued and taken with that **Lutheran-like opinion**: And that the States General <note>The governing body of the United Provinces of the Netherlands.</note> openly declared they felt the opposite / so that it was necessary to make use of the **sheep’s clothing** / and to first approach their **Honorable Mightinesses** with a softer tone. Therefore they secretly nourished this harmful monster within their breasts / in order to eventually release the grievous evil of this coercion of conscience / as a new ruin for the entire Netherlands: and then, seeing their time was right / to introduce this new **Genevan Inquisition** (which is not a hair’s-breadth better than the old Spanish one) into the Netherlands / who would thus exchange this Spanish yoke for a Savoyard one <note>Geneva was historically associated with the Duchy of Savoy; here it refers to the strict Calvinist discipline of Geneva.</note> / but would not cast off the tyranny.

For one must not doubt / that these people / who know how to recite this lesson of the "little sheep" so finely / have also known it by heart for a long time / and have artfully practiced / always having a fair **sheep's garment** ready / and with all fair appearance / secrecy / gentleness / and the likeness of an English purity and Christian earnestness / to prepare a soft entrance for their tyranny and devotion among the <margin>See the counter-challenge at Cologne which relates entirely to this.</margin> simple-minded. This is all the more so because / from the time the **Reformation** began to be maintained among us by the holy Authorities of these lands / they have well perceived / that the walls and gates of these united five <note>Though usually seven, the author may be referring to the core provinces or a specific historical grouping.</note> Netherlands were so firmly closed and well-guarded against them / that they could not cherish the least hope of accomplishing anything with

<column-break/>

open violence: Therefore they have had to devote themselves entirely to undermining / that is, wearing the **sheep’s clothing** / in order to wander here and there under it / to search and sniff through the flock / and to lurk for all opportunities. Practicing therein the cunning lesson of their Master **Justus Lipsius** / who, despite the fact that he was very fond of playing <term>uro, seca</term> <note>original Latin: "uro, seca," meaning "burn and cut," a famous and controversial phrase from Lipsius advocating for the forceful suppression of religious dissent.</note> that is, of burning and executing / <margin>Politics, Book 4, Chapter 3.</margin> nonetheless adds this caution / if the times can endure such things: he also advises his Prince not to undertake that which he could not carry out / as (as he very well says) in a sickness nothing is more dangerous / than an untimely medicine.

Therefore, although the constant and far-sighted among us had seen for a long time / that the intent was to dig up the blood-bath again / which had been stopped and covered by so many dead bodies of the pious heroes / they nevertheless must not say it / or even if they said it out of the pressure of their **conscience** / they could find no belief among the majority: because as soon as one or another lover of the Fatherland’s freedom / who had seen this wolf-skin glimmering in secret / being terrified and heartily grieved by it / set out to warn the sheep against such wolf-shepherds! these people would pull the **sheep’s clothing** tightly closed / in order to certainly not be recognized / and would cry out against such a person openly before all the world and scold him as a false agitator / a rebel / a **Theudas**, or a **Judas the Galilean** <note>Biblical figures who led failed revolts.</note> / most worthy to be <margin>See the answer to the Institution of Leiden.</margin> hanged and punished as a Raging person / a slanderer / a new **Machiavelli** / a sycophant / a flatterer / a dreamer / a Church-owl / and a smith of all heresies.

A iij
<meta>catchword: In order to</meta>

<summary>This page warns of a "Genevan Inquisition" attempting to take root in the Netherlands by masquerading as gentle and pious. The author compares these religious hardliners to wolves in sheep's clothing who wait for the right political moment to impose their "burn and cut" policies.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's argument that certain hardline religious factions are using deceptive tactics to manipulate the Dutch authorities and impose a strict, inquisitorial system of church discipline.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<meta>handwritten: 19.</meta>

...to use such high **protestations** <note>Formal declarations or public protests intended to signal piety.</note> not only to easily blind the good community / but even (O great arrogance!) to so bewitch the high **Authorities** / that they would let themselves be caught like stupid snipes in a net / and bind the generous **Lion** <note>The Dutch Lion, the heraldic symbol of the United Provinces, representing the state's sovereignty.</note> once again.

But those who are not entirely blind / or at least blinkering / have nonetheless been able to tell by the fur what kind of birds these are / who have come over here from elsewhere / where they had fouled their own nests / and have begun to nest in our **Churches** / and to set their throne / if not above / then at least alongside the Gods <note>A common 17th-century reference to civil magistrates and rulers, derived from Psalm 82.</note>. For to pave the way for this over a long period / they have tried to turn **Religion** into mere **speculations** and idle word-quarrels / everywhere scolding / shunning / and avoiding most bitterly those who differed in the understanding of even the smallest points of doctrine / more so than the greatest sinners who lead a scandalous life in public. But they have also sought to bind the Teachers themselves to the very letter of the opinions of certain private Doctors <note>Specifically referring to influential Geneva-aligned theologians like Calvin or Beza.</note> / and to burden them with strange and unbearable extensions of the **Confession** and **Catechism** <note>The Belgic Confession and the Heidelberg Catechism, which the hardliners wanted to interpret as strictly as possible.</note> / even going so far as to despise / and mockingly brand as heretics / the most excellent instruments of God / who yielded to no one in learning / nor in godliness of life / nor in love for the common fatherland / as soon as one began to doubt that they did not agree to the very last grain...

<column-break/>

...with the opinions of those who unjustly take for themselves the name of the Holy Mother Church.

They have caused great turmoil in the Churches and the State over matters which they admitted did not overturn the foundation / nor were of such weight that those with different views could not be tolerated in good conscience. And as soon as any misunderstanding arose regarding some minor points of Religion / which do not even touch upon salvation / let alone contain it / they did not stop from immediately suspecting / speaking / and judging the worst / inventing new Definitions and descriptions never used before / in order to (so they say) either keep the **Ketters** <term>Heretics: those holding beliefs contrary to established church doctrine.</term> in check / or to expose and then banish them.

Those who labored in their Sermons / deliberations / conferences / and other actions toward the healing of poor / torn Christendom / and therefore neither slandered the Sects / nor damned others over small things / here or there in a single word / nor wished to drive all Points to the extreme / but rather advised mutual tolerance / and long-suffering / and spoke against the "heretic-killers" <note>Critics of the death penalty for religious dissenters.</note> / were not only secretly suspected / rejected / and shunned / but were also most bitterly and rudely attacked in Sermons and printed booklets / as if they sought to smother all Religion in one pot / and mix the poison with the healthy substance in one vessel / to the certain ruin of the state / advising them rather to follow the example of **Abraham** / who, when his Shepherds quarreled with the Shepherds of **Lot**...

<meta>catchword: gave</meta>

<summary>This page criticizes hardline religious leaders who arrived from abroad to impose a strict orthodoxy on the Dutch Republic, accusing them of prioritizing minor doctrinal points over peace and civil authority. The author advocates for tolerance and warns that these factions are trying to undermine respected local leaders who favor moderation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of religious factions that use piety as a cover for political maneuvering. It specifically addresses the attempts to undermine the authority of the civil government (the "States") in favor of a rigid, independent church structure.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...gave Lot the choice <note>original: "kuere" — a reference to Genesis 13, where Abraham allows Lot to choose the land to the East or West to maintain peace.</note> to separate Eastward or Westward from him for rest and peace.

But what counsel has not only been very bitterly slandered in all **Ministers** <term>Predikanten: the Dutch term for Reformed clergy, often used here to imply those of the strict Calvinist faction.</term> of the other **Sects** / in which even those / who collectively hate the horrors of **Popery** <note>The Roman Catholic Church.</note> / and have purged out the **Leaven** <note>A biblical metaphor for a pervasive, corrupting influence.</note> of **Idolatry** and superstitions / being divided more perhaps through misunderstanding and ill-will / than through other fair reasons / but also [they have] undertaken and attempted to immediately make some misunderstanding / **Sects** and followings permanent / and to erect a separate **Church** / therein to obtain the promise of salvation / as if it were the life in the **Ark of Noah** / condemning all others outside it as worldly and Godless / and to shut them out from the **Kingdom of Heaven** / even though they were people of pious life / and in many, indeed the most particular / and for salvation most necessary points, were at one with them.

One has not only made many Laws and decrees over the **Conscience** of all the people / or even of the Teachers alone / daily more and more to Regulate each one's understanding according to his own / and to bind all senses to his own Opinions / but also when the **Authority** <note>The civil magistrates or the provincial government of Holland.</note> resisted the introduction of such ordinances / and according to their command some **Ministers** refused to follow the same / one has for many years (so that no one should think that such a thing has only arisen today or yesterday) run outside the city to **Preach** / leading out as many as six hundred / both men and women / administering **Baptism** to their children / and reaching out the **Supper** <note>The Lord's Supper or Communion.</note> as—

<column-break/>

—if otherwise things had been taught falsely in the city / and the **Sacraments** had been defiled.

<margin>See the opinion of the <unclear>Office</unclear> and <unclear>Council</unclear> <note>Likely referring to the 1613 "Colloquy of Delft," a failed attempt to reconcile religious factions.</note> regarding the power of ecclesiastical matters at Delft, 1613.</margin>

One has sought to make the **Authorities** themselves believe / that they should not concern themselves further with matters of **Religion** / than to only support it with their power and authority / and to put the good pleasure of the **Ministers** into work / to open the **Church** / to bar the Altars and images / to set the **Bible** upon the pulpit / not to call <note>To "call" in this context means to formally appoint or hire a minister.</note> the **Ministers** / elders and other **Ecclesiastical** persons / but to have them called by the congregation and **Church** / and to provide for their yearly salary / and maintenance / further not to help vote and decide in the **Ecclesiastical** meetings and gatherings over the occurring matters / but by their presence merely to ensure / that those who have come together / remain together in good peace / and if they should be divided among themselves into any parties / or might conspire together somewhere / to separate the same and bring it to nothing / as also to ward off and prevent all outward hindrances of the **Ecclesiastical** gatherings from violence or nuisance that occur / and may disturb the same: and further to confirm / strengthen / proclaim and authorize within the bounds of their territory by their edicts and commands that which was decided in the **Classes** <term>Classis: a regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders.</term> and **Synods** <term>Synod: a formal council of church leaders to decide on doctrine and administration.</term>:

<margin>Appears from the distinction between the political and **Ecclesiastical** government of the **States of Holland** delivered by some **Ministers** in the year 1579 in the 9th and 10th article.</margin>

And finally to force the despisers and falsifiers of the Divine word (declared as such by the **Classes** and **Synods**) whether they be public **Ministers** or private persons by virtue of their authority to leave the outward peace to the **Church** / and those who disturb this, with imprisonment—

A ii

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the historical friction between civil authorities (the Magistrates) and the Reformed clergy, specifically focusing on the clergy's resistance to state oversight and their attempts to purge political opponents.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<margin>
See the letters of the Prince of Orange to the Magistrates of Flanders regarding the **Anabaptists** <term>Doopfgesinde: Literally "Baptism-minded," referring to the Mennonites or Anabaptists who were often persecuted by both Catholics and strict Calvinists.</term> printed behind the counter-report on the Apology of the Edicts of Groningen.
</margin>
...to punish them in their person or with monetary fines. To this end, [the clergy] have not only frequently and very earnestly petitioned the Authorities to forbid and abolish all other gatherings except those of the Reformed religion, but they have also slandered them as wicked **Libertines** <term>Libértijnen: A derogatory label used by strict Calvinists for those who advocated for religious tolerance or who did not adhere to rigid dogma.</term> and free-spirits when they refused; indeed, in various places, they have sought by force, under a different pretext, to drive out all those of a different mindset, not only ousting the **Magistrates** <term>Magistraten: The civil rulers of a city or province.</term> from their Office, but also forcing most of them out of the city, and pushing in foreigners against the country's **Privileges** <note>These were the traditional legal rights and freedoms granted to Dutch cities and provinces, which the central or church authorities were accused of violating.</note>.

<margin>
Examples: Ghent, Leiden, Utrecht, the dealings with Leicester. And this is openly shown in the defense of Jacob Valmael, etc., printed at Utrecht in the year 1587.
</margin>
They have not wanted to yield in the slightest to the Holy Authorities in Church matters: even in the **Classes** <term>Classen: Regional assemblies of Reformed ministers and elders.</term> and **Synods** <term>Synoden: Larger ecclesiastical assemblies of the church.</term>, they refused to grant them a vote to help decide anything in Church matters, but barely consented that the Authorities might have some deputies in the Church meeting—provided they made **Profession of the Reformed Religion** <note>Meaning the state representatives had to be practicing members of the specific church they were meant to oversee.</note>—to have knowledge of the matters handled there, and also through their Authority and prestige to steer and lead the entire proceeding: but those [deputies] were not allowed to state their opinion on what was being discussed, or be asked for it, except extraordinarily or outside the meeting. Thus, even the introduction of certain church ordinances was opposed for the sole reason that they were drafted by the Authorities and not forged within the synods. Indeed, what is more, when the high Authorities commanded something of certain Classes, basing the fairness of their command even on the Synodical acts themselves, they still respected...

<margin>
See the cases of Baner, Arnoldi, and Corneli regarding this.
</margin>

<column-break/>

...neither command nor regard, but did the flat opposite; of which a remarkable example can be cited: It happened in **Zeeland** that under the **Classis of Schouwen** a village became vacant through the death of a **Minister** <term>Predicant: A Reformed preacher.</term> (about 7 years ago). While they were busy calling another, the Lords [of the States] together with the local law of the aforementioned village called a Student who was an **alumnus** <note>original: "alumnus" — a student whose education was funded by the provincial government (the States), usually on the condition that they serve the state church.</note> of the Lords States of Zeeland. The Church Council, however, went and called a certain wagon-maker who had practiced **Exhorting** <term>Proponeren: The act of delivering a practice sermon or theological discourse by one not yet fully ordained.</term> for some time. From this arose such a great dispute that the Lord Councilors of Zeeland had to involve themselves, and wrote to the Classes, charging them that the Student should be placed and promoted before the wagon-maker. They alleged and brought as a foundation for their command a certain Act of the **Synod of Goes**, which stated that the Zeeland alumni should be appointed to the Church service before others. But after this Letter was read, one man from that Classical meeting, out of a proud desire for **monkish** <note>original: "Mönicsche" — a derogatory Protestant reference to the perceived tyranny or arrogance of Roman Catholic monks, here applied by the author to fellow Protestants who act like "little popes."</note> lordship, burst out, saying: "Because the Lord Councilors come to command us this, therefore we ought to do the flat opposite." This was no sooner said than it was done, notwithstanding it was even contrary to their own Church act, and that simply to defy the Lord Councilors as well as the Lords and the law of the aforementioned village. For it did not fail due to the Student's capability, since shortly thereafter he was called and promoted to the Ministry in the city of **Zierikzee** itself. And they [the clergy] were summoned to court to give an account of this bold deed of theirs. Such things are driven and practiced everywhere.

<summary>This page describes the power struggle between civil magistrates and the Reformed clergy, detailing how ministers resisted state involvement in church governance. It provides a specific example from Zeeland where the clergy defied a government-sponsored candidate for the ministry simply to assert their independence from civil authority.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the Reformed clergy’s attempts to assert spiritual supremacy over civil authorities. The previous page discussed the clergy's use of force and their disregard for local privileges; here, the author satirizes their claim to exclusive, divinely-inspired insight while painting the Magistrates as spiritually "blind."</meta>

# PREFACE.

Just as if the office of the **Preachers** <term>Predikanten: The ordained ministers of the Dutch Reformed Church.</term> were meant to live in constant dispute with the **Authorities** <term>Overhept: The civil government or "Higher Powers."</term> in order to properly preserve the freedom of the Church, and as if the Church could not maintain its freedom unless the proper freedom and power of the Authorities were clipped and shortened: indeed, as if not only all preachers, but even the coarsest peasants and clumsy fishermen—whenever they are merely draped in the mantle of the **Eldership** <term>Ouderlingschap: The office of lay elders who, along with the ministers, governed the local church.</term>—possessed such hawk-eyes and were so sharp of sight that they could see a **Heretic** <term>Ketter: Someone holding beliefs contrary to established church dogma.</term> even through a triple-layered hood <note>The author uses this metaphor to suggest the clergy claim to see into a person's very soul, regardless of outward appearances.</note> right into their bosom, and could judge to a hair who was a heretic or not. And [it is as if] all **Magistrates** <term>Magistraten: City or provincial officials responsible for civil law and order.</term> and Authorities, precisely because they do not wear that mantle, even if they might have sharpened and exercised their senses to distinguish good and evil through diligent hearing, reading, and searching of God’s word, must nevertheless be regarded as blinder than moles, unable to distinguish a cow from a windmill <note>A Dutch idiom meaning to be completely ignorant or unable to see the obvious.</note>. And just as if they [the clergy] alone were graced with such a spirit that would always lead them into all truth, so that they could not fail in the slightest point, and that the Authorities had no part nor portion in such a spirit but must always doubt, waver, and wander, even if they sat for entire years with their noses in the **Bible**; because the aforementioned spirit is promised only to the "presented" church. And as if they [the clergy] were always driven, without ceasing, by such a firm, infallible, and heartfelt devotion and love that they could never deliver anyone to a "Pilate" out of a grudge, hatred, or bitterness; and that, conversely, the Magistrates and Christian Authorities, precisely because they are not elders, were all together so fierce, envious, and bloodthirsty—

<column-break/>

—that they would as easily seek to hang, strangle, drown in sacks, burn, and roast the good as the bad, the right-minded as the heretics. 

For in order to make the common people believe this, the Magistrate has been spoken of everywhere with great contempt, and their actions have been continually traduced; and it has been most masterfully sought to instill in the common people that the same [magistrates] are a heap of **Epicureans** <term>Epicureën: Followers of Epicurus, but used here as a slur for people who live only for pleasure and do not believe in divine providence.</term>, **Atheists**, and blind men, having no understanding at all to judge the current disputes of religion. Indeed, they have been accused in public print, and that to foreign churches before all the world, that, being puffed up with 

<margin>
Sybrandus Lubbertus <note>A prominent and strict Calvinist theologian (c. 1556–1625) who clashed with the more moderate Remonstrants and civil authorities.</note> in his Letter before the book *On the Savior* [Latin: *De Servatore*], against Socinus.
</margin>

worldly honor, they nourish and strengthen those who stir up discord in the Church, and also prevent that same discord from being settled by a lawful judgment. And what is most wretched of all, [the accusation is] that despite having no understanding of the points of Christian doctrine, they nevertheless take upon themselves the supreme judgment thereof, as if the blind could judge colors. 

All of this, and much more that could be brought forward if necessary, has served to gradually pave the way for the country-destroying doctrines: to close the door to the **freedom of Prophecy** <note>The "Freedom of Prophecy" (vryheyt der Prophetië) refers to the right of members to interpret and discuss scripture, a key point of contention for those seeking a more inclusive church.</note>—already granted much too narrowly—even more tightly, and to introduce a new **Popedom** <note>Dutch: "Pausdom." A common Protestant insult used against their own clergy when they acted with what was seen as Catholic-style authoritarianism.</note> and dominion over every man’s faith through the misuse of political power. To this end, the foundations have been laid by those who 

<margin>
3 John verse 9. <note>Refers to the biblical Diotrephes "who loves to be first" and refuses to acknowledge apostolic authority.</note>
</margin>

strike their fellow brothers and, like new **Diotrephes**, are not content to be the greatest, but wish to be everything alone. And also wanting their elbows to be free from the bonds of the Authorities, they seek on all sides to drive out under strange pretexts those who notice and oppose this their intention...

<summary>The author critiques the Reformed clergy's claim to exclusive spiritual insight and their derogatory portrayal of civil magistrates as "blind" and "atheistic" for seeking oversight in church matters. This tension is framed as a struggle against a "new Popedom" where the clergy seeks to dominate both faith and political power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the Reformed clergy’s attempts to assert spiritual supremacy over civil authorities. The previous page discussed the clergy's use of force; here, the author warns that these "zealots" are attempting to reintroduce a form of the Inquisition by slandering magistrates and inciting discord.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...to lock out and cast off. But now that they think they have their bonds tightened, they begin to force this yoke upon our necks more openly than before. Satan, who has been a murderer of man from the beginning, would gladly crawl back up onto **Christ’s** throne under the beautiful cloak of Church-zeal and God's glory, to make himself master of human conscience and faith; and for this purpose, he prepares anew I know not what tools. Some quarrelsome zealots, playing the part of the desperate, act like madmen and throw everything into confusion, so that—although their tongues strike a wondrous discord—they nonetheless deafen the people and make them stubborn with loud screaming and shouting: thinking *aut nunc aut nunquam* <note>Latin: "now or never."</note>, now or never is the time that the **Inquisition** <term>Inquisitie: Historically, the Catholic institution for rooting out heresy; here, the author uses it to describe the Reformed clergy's attempt to control private belief through state power.</term> shall be reintroduced, step-by-step and well-disguised. This opportunity must not be missed, since perhaps in a hundred years such a fine occasion will not appear again; therefore they run and trot, they write and spew day and night, far and near, just as if the Church were in labor and the time for birth had come, but there was no strength to give birth.

<margin>Preface of the *Warning*, folio 3 verso.</margin>
To this end, they exaggerate the need greatly, as if the Church were on fire everywhere, and the sheepfold <note>The original "scharpschope" is likely a printer's error for "schaepshok" (sheepfold).</note> were full of ravening wolves on all sides: as if the matter directly touched the Majesty of God and the crown of our Christendom, which the Dutch churches (through the godly maintenance of the High Authorities) have thus far worn clearly and unstained, and which is entrusted to the High Authorities

<column-break/>

to protect us from robbery and ruin, for His glory and the salvation of the subjects.

They run daily to the ears of **Princes** and **Lords**, both inside and outside the land, and leave hardly a person of status unbothered, filling their ears with contrived and decorated slanders, blowing them as full as possible, and blustering from top to bottom with a boastful boldness, as if a loud cry were half the victory. And they do this most of all against those whom no one has ever in their life rebuked for any misconduct, let alone caught in any.

They work masterfully to blow the fire of discord among the great ones, and do not hesitate for this purpose to depict excellent persons in government—who have done exceptional service to the land in defense of its freedoms, both inside and outside the country—through letters, books, sermons, and proposals, portraying them to everyone as if they were sworn enemies of God and His anointed, sought a new religion, and wanted to change the old one, or were allied with the **Pope** and the **King of Spain** to the ruin of the Church and the lands. This went so far that when a certain missive from the Honorable Mighty Lords the **States of Holland and West Friesland** <term>Staten van Hollandt en West-vrieslandt: The sovereign governing body of the primary Dutch province, often at odds with the more radical clergy.</term> was read in a church assembly—in which their Excellencies exhorted the members of that assembly to work for peace, love, and unity in their sermons, deliberations, conversations, and other actions—the President of the assembly did not hesitate

to close off and to reject. Some bellicose zealots, playing as if "All is Desperate" / act like madmen, and throw everything into confusion / so that / although their tongues strike a strange discord / they nevertheless, with loud screaming and shouting / make the people / as if deafened and stubborn; thinking *aut nunc aut nunquam* <note>Latin: "either now or never."</note> now or never is the time / that the Inquisition, nicely dressed up, shall be reintroduced / that opportunity must not be missed / since possibly in a hundred years such a fine occasion shall not appear again; therefore one runs and trots / one writes and churns out [texts] day and night / far and near / no differently than if the Church were in labor / and that the time of birth had come / and there were no strength to deliver. <note>An allusion to the Biblical verse Isaiah 37:3, expressing a crisis where a great effort is made but the desired result cannot be achieved.</note>

cleverly to say / that he could notice nothing else / but that they sought to lift the cloth all at once / that is / as he himself phrased it / to cast the State and the Church alike into the ground.

They still venture daily / not only to spread the arising misunderstandings regarding the Points of Predestination <note>The theological debate over God's divine decree concerning who is saved, which was the central conflict of the Synod of Dort era.</note> and their related issues as far and wide as possible through all Kingdoms and lands, without correctly presenting the nature of the dispute / in order to better obtain a Condemnation according to their own appetite / and then to pretend to both high and low / that all other Churches have heard of these things / and are greatly troubled by them: But for a long time they have also, through preaching and writing, instilled into the congregation / that a change in Religion is being sought / that it is not just about Predestination, but that all sorts of other strange things are hidden behind it / indeed that the whole Religion is called into question / and the only foundation of all true comfort is denied / slandered, and mocked / in order to thus alienate the good congregation as much as possible from the Teachers / whom God has given them. When they are exhorted and urged by the High Authorities <note>The States of Holland and West Friesland, who were trying to maintain civil peace.</note> to work toward peace and mutual tolerance in the questionable Points / then they very grandly pretend / that the minds are so alienated / and the hearts generally so troubled / by the strange rumors spread throughout the whole land / of something wondrous, something strange / that under this sought-after moderation / and moderate freedom to Prophesy <note>In this context, "prophesying" refers to the interpretation and preaching of Scripture, a common term in Reformed circles.</note> according to the measure of faith / there would

<column-break/>

<margin>See the written Conference held at Delft / the 26th of February, the year 1613.</margin>

be hidden / so that the congregation is thereby forced to live in constant unrest and mistrust / such that there is no hope of peace / nor any appearance of bringing the Church to silence / unless those whom they first brought into the public eye / and even onto people's tongues, and whom they would gladly / if not trip up / at least ring and muzzle / should become pupils again instead of Teachers: so that instead of having previously, while standing on an equal level with their fellow brothers, friendly conferred before the High Authorities over some doctrinal points / they should now, like schoolchildren, recite their lesson to their masters / and answer all such questions as these new Inquisitors and Masters of faith would present to them according to their appetite / and all that being done / not only to subscribe to the Catechism and Confession <note>Referring to the Heidelberg Catechism and the Belgic Confession, the standard doctrinal documents of the Dutch Reformed Church.</note> without any restriction or limitation, but even another "Article Letter" <note>A set of binding rules or a creedal statement.</note> that would then be laid before their noses / framed and put together on their own initiative by three or four men (who think they carry the Church on their shoulders / and are judged by many to be people who would even hawk their views abroad) / to sign promptly with their own hands without any tergiversation <note>Latin: *tergiversatio*; meaning evasion or making excuses to avoid a direct answer.</note> or refusal / not just as it stands / retaining the interpretation thereof for themselves / but according to the sense and meaning that the original authors and drafters thereof have / or might still in time, as it suits them, extract and Distill from it / and promise to maintain the contents thereof to the very last detail / on pain

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of religious authorities, shifting focus to the self-confessed moral failings of church leaders based on their own Reformed doctrines.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

there are weighty reasons to fear such things; simply have the patience to hear the honest confession of these great **Church-Masters** <note>original: "Kerckmeesters," referring here to high-ranking church officials or elders</note> and **Lords of the Faith** from their own mouths. You shall see with your own eyes, and feel and touch with your own hands, that this account cannot fail. For however beautifully they present themselves, it must ultimately result in a **Tyranny** worse than the **Spanish** one <note>A reference to the "Spanish Tyranny" of the Inquisition and the rule of the Duke of Alba, which the Dutch had recently fought to overthrow</note>, if such people are ever appointed as Church-Masters.

<margin>
See the 5th, 60th, 62nd, and 144th questions of the **Heidelberg Catechism**; also articles 15 and 24 of the **Netherlands [Belgic] Confession**.
</margin>

These are people who, before and after the **Sermon**, in their **Public prayers** before all the people, openly confess: that they are by nature not only blind in understanding, but also incapable of any good; that they have strayed from God and followed their own desires, and still sin horribly every day. Because they are so impure through **original sin**, all kinds of evil lusts—warring against God and their neighbor—dwell within them. Thus, they frequently and incessantly transgress God's commandments. In short, they confess that they are poor, miserable sinners, conceived and born in all wickedness and corruption, inclined toward all evil and useless for any good. So they also declare and confess, by the signing of their own hands, that they are by nature inclined to hate God and their neighbor. Therefore, their conscience also accuses them of having sinned against all of God's commandments and having kept none of them, but being still constantly inclined toward all wickedness. Consequently, they cannot produce any work so good that it is not defiled by the flesh and sin, and worthy of punishment; indeed, even the most holy among them, as long as they are in this life, have only

<column-break/>

a small beginning of the righteousness that God demands of them. Therefore (like defenseless and despondent soldiers), they often wish to be released from their bodies. Whoever is not yet satisfied by this, but desires a broader account to truly know these people, may add here the testimonies of the **Holy Scripture**. These they have either placed in the margins of this confession or use daily in their writings or sermons. With these scriptures, they testify fully of themselves and their own: that they have the will to do good, but cannot come to the performance of it; so that they do not the good they want, but the evil they do not want. This is because they are carnal, sold under sin, in which no good dwells. They claim to be full of unrighteousness, malice, greed, and wickedness; full of envy, murder, strife, and deceit; of evil character. They are whisperers, backbiters, God-haters, insolent, haughty, boastful, inventors of evil things, disobedient to parents, senseless, faithless, without natural affection, implacable, and unmerciful. Their throat is an open grave; they have used their tongues for deceit; the venom of vipers is under their lips; their mouth is full of cursing and bitterness. Their feet are swift to shed blood; in their ways are destruction and misery, and the way of peace they have not known. There is no fear of God before their eyes, but they have all turned away and become useless together. There is none righteous among them, no one who seeks God, not even one. Instead, all their righteousness and best works are like a filthy, soiled rag, or like leaves that wither in the Autumn.

# PREFACE.

This is their daily confession—such a public confession of themselves and their fellow believers—signed with their own hands. They teach and drum it into the people; indeed, they refuse to recognize as fathers in Christ or pious members of the household of faith—much less as good **Shepherds** and pious **Teachers**—anyone who will not repeat the "donkey's bray" <note>original: "esels-be," a derogatory term likely referring to the rote, unthinking repetition of the Catechism's words</note> after them word for word, sign it in the same way, and continue to promote it. Thus, there is no reason to doubt that they are, both inwardly and outwardly, exactly as they themselves claim to be. Is it not then quite reasonable that one should pay close attention to the nature of such people, and not only to their hands, but also to their counsels and their entire conduct? Would we not be in a fine mess if these **Holy Fathers** were nominated as **Heresy-masters** of the Church, approved by the **High Authorities**, and given such power that not only would everyone hereafter have to give an account to them—as often as it pleased these people—of the hope that is within them, but even the **Magistrates** themselves would have to come at their command <note>original Latin: "ad iube"</note> to please and heed their holiness? They would first target all their most faithful subjects who might even keep an eye on this group, because they dare to speak up against their wishes, causing them to forfeit their lives through fines, imprisonments, or banishments; then, in due time, they would prepare swords, gallows, **Wheels**, **Sacks**, **Barrels**, and **faggots** <note>original: "mutsaerden," bundles of wood used for burning heretics at the stake</note> against all those who could not or would not praise and approve every statute and ordinance of this **Holy Office**. Is there

<column-break/>

even a craftsman so simple, a farmer so blunt, or a fisherman so coarse, who does not notice that nothing more dangerous or harmful for the state of these lands and Churches can be imagined than for the high **Authorities** to allow themselves to be made into the blind **Executors** of such people's cruelty?

This will surely be called **Slander** here, and they will use the zeal of many honest people to mock this as a lampoon. But nevertheless, those who have kept even half an eye on the wind these years know well whether the harmful **Vaigach Strait** <note>original: "Waigats," a treacherous Arctic passage the Dutch explored while seeking a northern route to China</note> for merchants to travel concisely north to **China**, or the **Cape of Good Hope** <note>original: "Caput bona spey"</note> to go to **India** or the **Moluccas**, lies as much in the way as does that dam of the requested and far-seeing wisdom and prudent discretion of the **Lords States**. These Lords have always held, with the peace-loving Emperor **Maximilian the Second** <note>Holy Roman Emperor (reigned 1564–1576) known for his religious tolerance and refusal to coerce consciences</note>, that whoever wishes to rule over the consciences of men wars against Heaven, and commonly loses his dominion over the earth. If these people could once displace that wisdom, whether by force or by schemes, they have no doubt that their **Rooster** would soon be King. And what does it help to deny what previously seemed doubtful, but now manifests itself so clearly that he who does not smell what these people intend must have a wooden nose, or at least be severely congested in the middle of summer? For (so that no one thinks the whole case rests on a single proof) just read, if you can find the **Patience**, that fine piece of work, I
->say<-

<margin>
This Remonstrance was printed in Utrecht by Salomo de Roy, 1612.

I speak of the *Remonstrance* of G. Boot, License-master <note>An official responsible for collecting "licenten," a type of export/import tax common in the Dutch Republic.</note> at Gorinchem, which focuses solely on this and insists most strongly: that no more laudable or praiseworthy task can be undertaken than to drive away, destroy, ruin, annihilate, chase out, and pull out by the roots from this land—throwing them as if upon a manure heap—all those who have not made profession of the **Reformed Religion**, nor intend to do so. To this also tends the earnest admonition of Johannes Sceu, Minister at Middelburg, in his book called *A Short Description of the Office of the Magistrate*.

And to show an even more striking example <note>original: "een paert te wysen dat beter draeft," literally "to show a horse that trots better"</note>: people have not been ashamed to propose this land-destroying opinion through public and printed disputations, and to plant it into the youth—those who are being raised for the service of the Church and schools of Holland and West Friesland through the exceptional piety of our
<margin>
Disputation on Lawful Homicide, that is, of the lawful killing, 1612.
</margin>
**High Authorities** at the College of Holy Theology in Leiden. They do this so that they might timely plant this poison even in the forecourts of the Lord, and in the orchard and nursery-garden of the Church, fashioning the mold from which all churches of these lands would hereafter be shaped toward this ferocity and murderousness.

If this ball were allowed to run its full course, where would it end? Would it not eventually come down to what a certain Minister in Delft <note>The text likely refers to the hardline Calvinist Petrus Guntanus, a former priest who became a Reformed minister.</note> (whom some old folk used to compare to **Guntanus**, formerly Pastor of the Old Church there—not so much for his small stature as for his arrogant presumption) did not
<column-break/>
<margin>1607.</margin>
shrink from saying boldly in a certain conference, held at the time of the last local **Synode** there? He stated: That our Government could not be considered a true Christian Government until they commanded all those of a different conviction than the **Reformed**—who, after half a year's warning, refused to join them—to depart the country with a formal notice. This is more extreme than even the King of Spain, who, in the Cologne peace negotiations through the Duke 
<margin>
Cologne peace negotiations Page 156.
</margin>
of Terra Nova, willingly offered a period of four whole years before those of a different conviction than the Roman Catholic would be required to leave the land.

Dear God, where will the madness of these people lead? What shall be the end of such counsels and pretenses? Lord Jesus, what times are we living in? Are these the hardest times, or are there harder yet to come? Must our eyes see this and our ears hear it? Must our hands feel and touch this: that we must return again to the hanging, the burning, and the drowning <note>original: "sacken," the practice of execution by tying a person in a sack and throwing them into water</note> of poor Christians? Shall we see, instead of one **Pope** far removed from us—whom we have abjured—some six or more set up in our land and placed upon the throne to command every man’s faith? Instead of one or two **Bishops**—whose introduction against the privileges of the land we so manfully resisted with our property and blood—shall we see such a great heap and multitude of new **Bishops** and **Inquisitors**, not only introduced everywhere, but also having thrust themselves into all the most prominent offices, services, and commissions among the Ministers?

->B iii<-

in great and small ecclesiastical assemblies, to the notable oppression of all native-born church ministers—and those who love the freedom of the fatherland and of conscience—to the certain destruction of the lands and churches, and the introduction of a tyranny and lordship over men’s consciences. Indeed, it is so vile; is it not even viler than what we have resisted? That against the Court of Holland they shall erect another spiritual court, not subordinated to the aforementioned court, but having its power and commission immediately from God, and its own spiritual laws forged in the Synod? 

Shall we have to suffer that, where we hoped for freedom, a yoke shall be placed upon us harder than the Spanish Inquisition <note>The Spanish Inquisition was a tribunal established to maintain Catholic orthodoxy; here it is used as a benchmark for ultimate religious oppression.</note> itself? A yoke which neither we nor our fathers before this have been willing or able to bear? Shall that be the long-desired outcome of such a bloody and long-lasting internal war <note>Referring to the Eighty Years' War (1568–1648), which had been raging for over forty years at the time of writing.</note>, of more than forty years, awaited with great patience? Shall that be the praiseworthy freedom of conscience with the preservation of the Privileges of the lands, in hope of which we endured everything and considered all burdens light? 

What will then become of us and our flourishing Republic, as well as such glorious churches, when by the advice of certain firebrands <note>original: "stokebzandẽ," meaning people who stir up trouble or fan the flames of conflict</note> who distrust the truth, we shall see the whole land set on fire? The old, extinguished flames of blood, the scaffolds and gallows erected again throughout the land? And through the harmful abuse of the Christian earnestness of the Magistrates, introduced into the merciful kingdom of the kind Savior Jesus Christ: the old Draconian Laws <note>Draconian laws: named for Draco of Athens, these laws were famous for being written in blood rather than ink due to their extreme severity.</note>, written with the

<column-break/>

red ink from the sharp quiver of certain foreign and violent men, to stain the neutral and innate goodness of the Dutch States with such foreign and Marrano-like <note>original: "Maraensche," a derogatory term referring to the forced converts in Spain, here used to imply a cruel, inquisitorial Spanish style of persecution</note> bloodiness? Shall they at last have to bathe their hands again in the blood of poor, pious Christians? Shall they have to sprinkle their land again with the ashes of the simple, and set their very bodies as food for the birds of heaven? 

Or, at best, shall they drive away a great part of their most faithful subjects? Those who, in departing, will leave the land desolate, poor, and miserable, and not only populate foreign lands but also make them rich by bringing and making common their crafts and trade, in which the principal prosperity of the lands consists? Would that not be the right way, like Delilah, to shave off Samson’s hair <note>A biblical reference to Samson and Delilah; the author suggests that stripping the land of its religious freedom and industrious citizens is like cutting Samson's hair, leaving the state weak and defenseless.</note>, and then easily deliver him (though perhaps unknowingly) into the hands of the Philistines?

You will say: "it is not yet so far along here; it hasn't come to hanging and strangling, nor to banishment and expulsion." Wonderfully well said; we also hope that the merciful God will never allow such a thing, and keep our gracious Authorities from such cruelty, so that they would never intend to punish anyone physically, or drive them from the land, for no other reason than that he cannot feel or believe any evil of the good God. 

But meanwhile, one notices well that there is pain enough for many, and that they do not change their course; yes, even daring to say openly that it only depends on two or three heads that they cannot achieve their aim...

They even go so far as to challenge and invite others to refute it, as if the same had not been excellently done during Beza's lifetime, without him answering even the least bit to clarify his opinion. However, to tell the truth and give them the honor they deserve, none of these people here in this country have rightly disputed the matter itself on its principles; rather, they have remained focused on translating the work of others or merely advising and <margin>Page 12</margin> praising what was recounted more broadly by others. The reason, say those from Sneek in their preface for Beza's tract, is so that they would not be accused in this <unclear>point</unclear> of having anything in mind other than what other godly teachers hold, thus expressing their own grievances through another’s mouth, thinking *Company eases misery* <note>Original Latin: "Societas levat miseriam," a common proverb suggesting that a shared burden or a common plague brings a sort of rest.</note>. <column-break/>

There, leaving behind their pretenses, they would be forced to fight in open battle over the main issue with all their might, either to gain victory with much blood and sweat or to expect a miserable defeat. Or, if they do not trust their strength, they might crawl back into their shells and save themselves by fleeing into strong denial and high protests. For without that, it is not to be believed that these people, who are so keen-eyed, would have left such rich material—from which honor and thanks could be gained here in this country, and special edification—untreated for so long. Indeed, for over thirty years, they have been openly dared and challenged to speak and declare their views, yet they have remained so obstinately silent, to the great harm and disadvantage, even noticeable damage to their affairs. I will not even mention how hostilely they hated and slandered those who openly spoke against this <term>soul-murder</term> <note>Original: "sielmoorderie."
