<meta>This page continues the author's preface, shifting from a critique of contemporary clergy to a personal defense and a theological argument against the use of capital punishment for religious dissent.</meta>

# PREFACE.

(God knows that I speak the truth) I have no fellowship with the opinions of <term>Servetus</term> <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish theologian executed in Geneva for heresy, whose death became a flashpoint for debates on religious tolerance.</note>, <term>Neuser</term> <note>Adam Neuser (c. 1530–1576), a German pastor who fled to the Ottoman Empire after being accused of anti-Trinitarianism.</note>, <term>Gentile</term> <note>Giovanni Valentino Gentile (c. 1520–1566), an Italian humanist beheaded in Bern for his heterodox views on the Trinity.</note>, or others of that sort; nor do I set myself up as a champion for them. Instead, I have an abhorrence of this bloody, cruel **killing of heretics**, and I would not gladly see this noble body <note>Referring to the body politic or the church community.</note> poisoned by it, or our churches contaminated by it. I set myself against this. Having no better material at this time (the future may perhaps yield something better), I place into your hands as an old medicine, a little old treatise, never before published, to serve as an **antidote** <margin>2 Thessalonians 2:8<br/>2 Corinthians 10:4<br/>Hebrews 4:12<br/>Titus 1:9</margin> against the aforementioned poison <note>The Dutch "vergist" is likely a variation of "vergift" (poison), fitting the medical metaphor of the "antidote."</note>. Read it, reader, with good judgment and attention; and believe that the doctrine of killing heretics is destructive to soul, body, and land; and that opposing it serves to advance the knowledge of God's truth for the soul, rest for the body, and godly peace for the country.

Do you wish to destroy the "Son of Perdition" other than by the Spirit of God’s mouth? Truth overcomes with its own weapons, which are spiritual and mighty through God to pull down strongholds, casting down every argument and every high thing that exalts itself against the knowledge of God, and bringing every thought into captivity to the obedience of Christ, as the Apostle says. The teachers who, abandoning these, do not take the field with others—or who, as if these spiritual weapons were not sufficient in themselves, wish to sharpen or bolster them with others <note>Meaning worldly force or state violence.</note>—are led astray and sin against the Truth, which they do not trust because they do not know its power, and against the love they do not sufficiently practice toward their neighbor. They may kill the body, but they win no souls; indeed, they lose them by killing someone 

<column-break/>

in their unbelief due to a misunderstanding. They can make hypocrites, but not a Christian. And if they then happen to kill a sheep (as most often happens) instead of a wolf—a true teacher instead of a heretic, a pious servant and witness of Christ instead of a blasphemer—do these theologians now think they are without worry? Where are their warrants for such tearing apart? Holy Scripture, this "privilege," is not to be found there for them. And how would one find it there, where the means of spreading the truth is recommended as persuasion, not force, and the uprooting of heretics and heresies is forbidden: as is clearly proven in this treatise, as well as in the book published against **Beza's** <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), the successor to Calvin in Geneva, who wrote a famous defense of the execution of heretics.</note> *Killing of Heretics*.

So then, let (as those from Leeuwarden <margin>In the Warning, Page 3.</margin> desire) the Church judge over both, and let it do so impartially according to God’s word. But if the Church did not do so: if it did not hear the parties, and would suffer no correction—indeed, if it rose up against its admonishers and even erred grossly—this has happened all too often. Let the Israelite Church be an example, which killed the prophets and Apostles, and condemned Christ Himself as a blasphemer (a label with which others are now too easily smeared) and crucified Him among criminals. Has this bloody path not also too predictably run through the Papal Church? It is (you will say) "Reformed" in this country. Oh, if only it were so, that it were well-Reformed in all things, including this matter, and remained well-Reformed! But just as the former, so also the Church of Geneva and Switzerland has erred, even in this matter of killing heretics and conscience...

<meta>catchword: science</meta>

<summary>The author clarifies that while he does not support heretical doctrines, he vehemently opposes the execution of heretics, arguing that truth should be defended with spiritual persuasion rather than state violence. He warns the Dutch Reformed Church not to repeat the historical errors of the Catholic Church or the ancient Israelites by persecuting dissenters.</summary>

**coercion of conscience**; likewise, the <term>Reformed Church</term> <note>The Dutch Reformed Church (Gereformeerde Kercke), which at this time was grappling with internal divisions between strict Calvinists and more moderate Remonstrants.</note> here in this land—being composed of many imperfect men (as proven above) and not of all-knowing gods, and as it is not always governed by the best among them, or if the shepherds should turn into wolves (or be succeeded by them, which **Beza** and **Calvin** admit can happen)—can also err grossly. Error can creep into it just as easily as into other churches. What shall we do then? Who shall judge the points of doctrine in dispute—whether they be heresy or not, and who the heretics are? The **authorities**? No, Beza will not suffer that: "For the knowledge thereof belongs to the Church, and not to the Prince," he says. Thus, many theologians nowadays, like the Papists, appeal to the Church and the Councils. Shall such a Church or Synod, in such a circumstance and amidst such great differences, be accuser, party, and judge in its own cause? Who would want to endure such treatment from their opponent? What else can arise from this but a <term>Midianite War</term> <note>A reference to Judges 7, used here to describe a state of internal chaos, confusion, and mutual destruction within a group.</note>, or a new **Popedom**, tyranny, and **coercion of conscience**, the muzzling of the Truth, and the extinguishing of prophecy? We ought to be wary of this, especially where such a Church has the authorities so devoted to her that they act as her "nursing fathers" and "kiss the son"—which is to say, **killing heretics**, as Beza so strangely glosses it. Would this be anything other than a kiss from Judas or Amasias, and as <term>Nazianzenus</term> <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), an early Church Father known for his defense of Trinitarian theology.</note> says, acting as if for Christ, but against Christ?

<margin>pages 252, 253.</margin>
Therefore, this treatise shall now be useful: both to set right those who rely too much on 

<i>

<column-break/>

men, and who might be misled within the Reformed Church by holding the two aforementioned authors <note>Theodore Beza and John Calvin.</note> in too high a regard concerning the **killing of heretics**; and to cleanse the Church of such 
<margin>2.</margin>
**soul-murdering and land-destroying error** that might slip in there; as well as to put a stop to the excessive zeal used to incite 
<margin>3.</margin>

<margin>4.</margin>
**the authorities** (for whose circumspection, courtesy, and wisdom, especially in this matter here in this land, we cannot thank God Almighty enough—as also for the prosperity, rest, and peace of the inhabitants arising from it, notwithstanding that this liberty is being campaigned against by certain sophistical theologians) regarding the greatness of the perils. May they never at any time allow their power to be abused for the appetite of the clergy (as happened under the Antichrist) to become blind executors and servants of other people's cruelty, which is as consistent with Evangelical doctrine and Christian love as Christ is with **Belial**, who has been a murderer from the beginning; whereas the Son of Man came not to destroy, but to save the souls of men. It may also happen that someone deigns to answer this treatise, giving us the occasion to then handle the material somewhat more broadly, which shall be much more useful than constantly breaking the congregation's heads with that high question of <term>Predestination</term> <note>The theological doctrine that God has foreordained all events, especially the salvation of some and the damnation of others; the central point of contention in the Dutch church at the time.</note>.

-># PREFACE.<-

In the meantime, Beloved Reader, you cannot turn back Death; keep yourself free from such counsel, and do not stain your soul with blood. <note>The author warns the reader not to participate in or support the "bloody" councils that sentence people to death for their beliefs.</note> Pray to God that He might halt this doctrine—which is destructive to the soul, the body, and the land—and that He might give better minds to those who promote it within Christendom. Pray also that He may always preserve our **Authorities** <term>Overheydt</term> <note>The secular government or magistrates. The author argues that the state should not be the executioner for the church.</note> from the execution of such deeds, and endow them with such wisdom that they may always distinguish the true Mother—who seeks the child’s life—

<column-break/>

from the false one, who votes for the child’s death. <note>This is a reference to the Judgment of Solomon (1 Kings 3:16–28). The author compares the true Church to the mother who would rather lose her child than see it killed, whereas the "false" persecuting Church is willing to see the dissenter executed.</note>

Use this **Treatise** for your benefit, focusing on its substance and its core meaning <note>original: *’t Mergh*, literally "the marrow."</note>, without dwelling too much on minor points here or there—which are of little consequence and do not truly serve the matter at hand.

And fare well.

## PREFACE,
->by<-
# SEBASTIAEN CASTELLIO,
->on this his Treatise.<-

It would be a desirable thing if good men possessed great **Authority**, while the wicked had none at all, because people nowadays look so much toward Authority and commonly assert and believe things that are entirely unstable, relying solely on that Authority. Because of this, it happens that one errs and sins the least when a good man holds great Authority; conversely, one errs and sins most grievously when an evil person holds great Authority. 

But since in these evil and crooked times, **Authority** <term>Authoriteyt</term> <note>Castellio refers here to "social and religious influence"—the power to sway public opinion and church policy.</note> almost always rests with the greatest multitude of people, and the majority is commonly devoted to those who provide what serves their own profit, pleasure, and honor, or to those who are famous among foreign Nations—which also serves in some way as an honor and ornament to themselves—and since, on the other hand, they have an aversion to those who rebuke them and will not turn a blind eye to everything: So it commonly happens that the **flatterers** <term>vleyers</term> and **hypocrites** <term>huychelaers</term> are held in high esteem by them, and that, conversely, the upright and steady people—who hold idle fame and honor in low regard—are despised and rejected by them.

Again, as there is nothing so hidden that it shall not in due time be revealed, and because the present sweetness of **sycophancy** <note>original: *pluymſtryckerie*, literally "feather-stroking," a vivid Dutch term for fawning flattery.</note> eventually gives birth to bitter pains, so it shall happen after the

<summary>In this preface, Sebastiaen Castellio laments that the public often grants authority to flatterers and famous figures rather than upright men, warning that relying on human authority rather than truth leads to spiritual error.</summary>
<keywords>Sebastiaen Castellio, religious authority, flattery, hypocrisy, public opinion, Reformation ethics</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues Sebastiaen Castellio’s preface. The previous page discussed how people often follow the wrong authorities because of their social standing or popular appeal, and warned that hidden truths would eventually be revealed. This page continues that thought, explaining that those who follow flatterers will eventually regret it, just as in the biblical examples of Noah and Lot.</meta>

<header>PREFACE.</header>

men will have fallen into great harm through **flattery** <term>pluymſtriyckerien</term> <note>Literally "feather-stroking," a vivid Dutch term for sycophancy or toadying.</note>, so that they will at last recognize their flattery, and even curse it; whereas, on the contrary, they will praise and protect the good, whose counsel they first despised because it was struggling against their flesh. And from this it commonly happens that the good are praised and the evil are rebuked after their death. The cause of this is the ignorance of men, who prefer present short-lived joy over the eternal joy to come. Just like children who would rather have an apple or a pear immediately than an entire inheritance later. If men were like grown men, they would do the opposite and avoid much evil.

We have many examples of this: Noah <note>original: "Noe"</note> did not have much authority in his time, yet had he possessed a bit more, the world would not have perished so lamentably back then. This same thing also happened to Lot <note>original: "Loth"</note>, whom the Sodomites <term>Zodomijten</term> despised, only to be destroyed. This also happened to the Prophets, John the Baptist <note>original: "Ioannes den Doper"</note>, Christ, and the Apostles, all of whom the Jews despised so greatly that they brought terrible and long-lasting plagues upon their own heads; this occurred because they followed the sweet and flattering, yet very bloody, counsel of the **Scribes and Pharisees** <term>Schriftgeleerden en Phariſeen</term>. 

Christ also says that at his coming, the world shall be in exactly such a state as the times of Noah and Lot were: that is, there shall be great carelessness, and that people will utterly despise those who wish to turn men away from their sins. Since, then, the coming of Christ is near, those who occupy the place of the Scribes and Pharisees among us ought primarily to be suspect and a cause for reflection, lest they, through their sweet fawning
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

He parallels the present day with the times of Noah and Lot, urging suspicion toward modern religious leaders who mirror the ancient Scribes and Pharisees.</summary>

### SEBASTIAN CASTELLIO.

The number of those who wish to be wise and prudent is
small, yet it is for their sake that we perform this labor.
Those who despise good counsel now will surely wish for it
either tonight or tomorrow. Happy is he who does not
flatter himself, nor wishes to be flattered by anyone.

The reason why I have said all this is this:
John Calvin is, in this day and age, in great regard
and authority, and I would wish him even greater
from the heart, if I saw that he were a gentle-minded
and merciful man. But as he has recently, in a certain

nature and through good instruction have an utter horror
and aversion (as indeed all men ought to have) for
bloodshed—will now (God willing) make known to the
entire world what that man has sought to bring about.
This is so that those who do not wish to perish willfully
may no longer allow themselves to be deceived by him,
but may instead be brought onto the right path.

### SEBASTIAN CASTELLIO.

Michael
Servetus,

otherwise

In the past year 1553, on October 26th, in Geneva, for certain opinions in religious matters, Michael Servetus, a Spaniard, was burned; and that through the management and instigation of Calvin, who is a shepherd and preacher of the congregation there. This burning of Servetus being spread everywhere, there were many who took it poorly, and especially the Italians and French, for these reasons: First, because someone was killed for certain opinions in religious matters. Second, because he was killed so cruelly. Third, because such had occurred through the management and instigation of a <term>preacher</term> <note>Castellio emphasizes the contradiction between the role of a Christian minister and the act of pursuing a death sentence.</note>. Fourth, because Calvin had consulted with the man’s enemies to bring about his death; for that was also being said.

Fifth, because his books were burned at Frankfurt. Sixth, because the preachers had condemned him to hell after his death. Calvin, having heard all this, published a book against Servetus to vindicate himself, to refute Servetus, and to prove that such people should henceforth be punished with the sword. Yet there are few who notice what Calvin seeks to achieve through that book. For it is so beautifully whitened and adorned with the false appearance of godliness that it cannot easily be perceived, and especially not by those people who are all too devoted to Calvin. My intention is to examine this book of his, and to expose it more nakedly, so that through this, if it were possible, the eyes of some might be opened. Yet I pray that no one will rage and rave at me for this, but that the reader may well weigh my reasons, and not cast his eyes upon the dignity and authority of the persons, but upon the truth alone (which otherwise cannot be perceived).

I hope, with God’s help, to accomplish so much that what this man intended shall come to light in such a way that everyone who is not willfully blind will be able to perceive it. But Calvin will perhaps say, according to his old habit, that I am a disciple of <term>Servetus</term> <note>Michael Servetus (c. 1511–1553), a Spanish theologian and physician executed in Geneva for heresy, specifically for his rejection of the Trinity and infant baptism.</note>; yet let no one take that to heart, since I do not defend Servetus’s teaching, but only demonstrate that Calvin’s teaching is false. We shall not burn Calvin’s books, for the author still lives, and his book, published by him in both <term>Latin</term> and <term>French</term>, is still at hand; so that no one will be able to accuse us of pointing anything out poorly or falsely. <meta>This page begins the formal point-by-point critique of John Calvin’s "Defense of the Orthodox Faith," following the author's introductory remarks on the previous page. -># A Dialogue<-

After this, I shall also (for the sake of brevity) place those little numbers before my words, which I shall bring forth in response to what I have to say about his writing. First, I shall set down here the title of <term>Calvin’s book</term><note>John Calvin published "Defensio orthodoxae fidei de sacra Trinitate" in 1554 to justify the execution of Michael Servetus and the right of the state to punish heresy.</note>, which reads as follows:

<column-break/>

### Calvin 1.

> **Defense of the orthodox faith of the Holy Trinity, against the dreadful and strange errors driven by Michael Servetus the Spaniard: Wherein it is proven that heretics must be punished by the right of the sword, and that specifically that godless man was justly and deservedly punished at Geneva.**

### Vaticanus.

He calls those who wander "Heretics," just as if he wanted to say: "I shall write against Servetus’s errors to prove that those who wander—that is, the heretics—must be punished with the <term>right of the sword</term><note>The "jus gladii" or "right of the sword" refers to the authority of the secular government to execute criminals or, in this context, religious dissidents.</note>, just as was done to the wandering Servetus." That this is Calvin’s meaning—namely, that one must kill all those who wander grievously in error unless they immediately agree with Calvin’s views—will become clear later. Here he is occupied in this dispute of his concerning heretics (as we shall prove) to show that all those whom Calvin and his followers label as heretics must go the same way as Servetus. In which case, all Christians would then have to be burned, except for the Calvinists alone—that is, those who hold to Calvin’s institutions. Which...

<column-break/>

<margin>He takes it up thus from now on.</margin>

### Calvin 2.

Although the Devil has recently sought, among other things, to darken the newly born light of the Gospel with very gross [errors], nonetheless this is primarily a hideous heap (1) of all impieties, which Servetus has spewed out in his published books; yet it never seemed good to me before this to specifically apply myself to refuting this man, because in his (2) frenzies such absurdities were hidden that I hoped they would (3) bleed out <note>original: "doot ghebloet"; a metaphor meaning to die out naturally or lose momentum.</note> of their own accord and come to nothing.

### Vaticanus.

(1) He calls those things "impieties" here which he called "errors" in the title, and even "frenzies" here; which things cannot stand together, for an error may not properly be called impiety, unless you would want to call <term>Paul</term><note>Referring to the Apostle Paul, who persecuted the early Christian church before his conversion, an act often cited by reformers as a "gross error" done in ignorance rather than malice.</note> impious when he persecuted the congregation, or <term>Thomas</term><note>The Apostle Thomas, known for his doubt regarding the Resurrection of Jesus.</note> when he did not believe that Christ was risen—which errors of ours were quite gross. Holy Scripture calls those "Godless" [impious] who sin willfully and knowingly out of a godless heart, without being overcome by weakness or led astray through ignorance. Such a one is...

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>The author begins a systematic rebuttal of John Calvin’s justification for executing Michael Servetus, arguing that Calvin conflates genuine religious "error" with "impiety" to justify the death penalty for dissenters.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Michael Servetus, heresy, right of the sword, Trinity, Geneva, religious tolerance, impieties, Apostle Paul, Doubting Thomas</keywords>

was Cain, who killed his brother, and Saul, who persecuted David—as he himself admitted—doing him wrong. Christ does not pray for such as these when he says he does not pray for the world; but he does pray for those who stray (as he says): "Forgive them, for they know not what they do." That is to say, they sin out of error. For had they known him, they would not have crucified the <term>Lord of Glory</term><note>A title for Jesus Christ found in 1 Corinthians 2:8.</note>. It seems that the man here traps himself, for the mysteries and secrets of God before which one ought to tremble are the Trinity, <term>Predestination</term><note>The doctrine that God has eternally decreed the salvation or damnation of every individual soul.</note>, and Election. And yet he speaks of these things as firmly and certainly as if he had been in Paradise. Even in this book, he treats the matter of the Trinity so subtly that he causes the hearts and consciences of the simple to waver and doubt. Indeed, in the matter of Predestination, he proceeds so harshly that he has brought countless people into such great carelessness as existed before the <term>Flood</term><note>original: "Diluvie"; referring to the biblical Great Flood in the time of Noah.</note> and in the time of Lot, so that the world, having been brought into such indifference by him, might be all the more unexpectedly overtaken by the coming of the Lord.

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between "Vaticanus" (Sébastien Castellio) and John Calvin. Vaticanus is criticizing Calvin’s hypocrisy, pointing out that Calvin condemns others for creating "novelties" and "laws" in the church while he himself has strictly regulated religious life in Geneva.</meta>

<center>Dialogue</center>

### Vaticanus.

The evil and godless whom he mentions are adulterers, drunkards, and the like, who are in truth evil and godless people: but he does not know that there are no more godless people than the hypocrites, who, being clothed in sheep's clothing, devour the sheep.

### Calvin 6.

And passing over many other things, we see how (1) bold and insolent some are in their fictions, and with what an insatiable desire for novelties; others are constantly tormented as if by a mad itching and tickling <note>Calvin is describing the "restless" nature of those he considers heretics, who cannot stop coming up with new ideas.</note>.

### Vaticanus.

(1) Just like he himself, who is so bold and insolent that in Geneva it is held unlawful to contradict his fictions in the slightest. So that one may baptize children with no other words, nor sing the Psalms on any other days, than exactly as Calvin has prescribed them. He alone has introduced more novelties within 10 years than the whole Church has in the past 600 years: though he nevertheless rails very bitterly in his *Institutes* against such people who seek to introduce novelty: so that it seems nothing else than that he wanted to drive all those others away, so that he might be the <term>cock of the walk</term><note>The original Dutch "Haentgen alleen" literally means "the only rooster," referring to someone who wants to be the sole authority.</note> alone. I will bring his own words here, from his first printed *Institutes* (which were printed in Basel in the year [15]36) from the 6th chapter or the 429th page.

<column-break/>

### Calvin 7.

<margin>Calvin's Institutes.</margin>
(1) Since even the Apostles themselves were not known to have any power to give laws—indeed, the same power was so often taken away from the ministers of the Church by the word of God—I marvel at those who, against the express command of God, have dared to arrogate such power to themselves. <margin>James 4:11-12</margin> James writes clearly enough: (2) "He who judges his brother, judges the Law: if you judge the Law, you are not a doer of the Law, but a judge: there is one Lawgiver, who can save and destroy." And a little further: Peter also, when he presents the Gentiles with their office, <margin>1 Peter 5</margin> warns them that they should feed the flock in such a way that they should not exercise <term>lordship</term><note>original: "heerschappie"; referring to ecclesiastical tyranny or overreaching authority.</note> (3) over the same. See, there indeed is cut short all power that they would want to arrogate to themselves: those who would exalt themselves without God's word. For it was not given to the Apostles to confirm their own doctrine and their own kingdom: but that they should only glorify the doctrine and the kingdom <note>That is, the Kingdom of God.</note>.

### Vaticanus.

(1) But such power was well known to you when, for eight years in Geneva, you established laws which you used against others: and a few days after <term>Servetus’s</term><note>Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian executed in Geneva for heresy in 1553.</note> death, you came with a summons for <term>Berthelier</term><note>Philibert Berthelier, a Genevan politician who opposed Calvin’s use of excommunication.</note> and requested help from the <term>Magistrate</term><note>The civil government or city council of Geneva.</note> according to the laws that you yourself had made and the Magistrate had confirmed: yes, you have those same laws

<meta>catchword: laws</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus accuses Calvin of hypocrisy, noting that while Calvin’s "Institutes" argues that even Apostles lacked the power to create laws, Calvin himself established strict religious laws and novelties in Geneva. The text highlights specific legal conflicts involving Servetus and Berthelier as evidence of Calvin’s ecclesiastical overreach.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemic between Vaticanus (Sébastien Castellio) and John Calvin. Vaticanus is currently attacking Calvin's authoritarian rule in Geneva, specifically referencing the legal measures used against political and theological opponents like Philibert Berthelier and Michael Servetus.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

[These] laws were sent to your Swiss churches so that they would pronounce judgment according to them: to cut off Berthelier <note>Original: "Vertellerius." Philibert Berthelier was a Genevan politician and critic of Calvin who was excommunicated and later fled the city.</note>, just as they had shortly before pronounced judgment against Servetus <note>Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian burned for heresy in Geneva in 1553.</note>. Or will you say that you have made no new laws, but have drawn them all from God’s word? To that I answer first: that you ought to have written nothing at all, but should have left the laws as they were established by God. Nor should you have so shamelessly poked your dirty snout into that precious treasure. Secondly, one will ask you where God’s word teaches that certain Psalms must be sung on certain days according to Calvin’s institution—and many other things besides, which according to your institutions are so strictly observed there that it would almost be weighed more heavily if one did not follow them precisely than if one had committed a sin against the Holy Spirit. (2) Even as if you were not the man who condemned Servetus and many others. (3) He even holds such lordship that he is called "my Lord" by many simple folk. Indeed, there was once a great dispute among the preachers whether Calvin should be called "Lord," while the others were only called "Masters" <note>In this period, "Master" (Meester) usually referred to a university graduate or teacher, whereas "Lord" (Heere) implied sovereign authority.</note>. Yet lordship so prevailed that he came to be called Lord. For it happened once that a poor, simple man came to his house and asked if "the Brother" was at home? To which he received the answer: "What Brother? He is man enough to be called 'Lord' by you."

<column-break/>

In short, Calvin rules so in Geneva that one might rather—indeed, with less peril—anger the King of France himself in his own Court than this man in Geneva. Countless people have well experienced this, whom he has cast out and miserably tormented. If one were to name them all, one would have to make an entire history out of it. Now let him go and prove that the Apostles also did or taught such things. But we shall return again to his book.

->### Calvin 8.<-

These defects God repays with righteous and deserved punishment: while He lets people, who are otherwise clever of mind and well-learned, fall into foul, stinking error through a beastly carelessness. I intended this as a remedy to improve such things, though not as timely as the matter required. However, after I became better informed and aware of the situation, I was not so greatly surprised that so many in (1) Italy are infected with that evil. This will not only be well received by the impartial and peaceful of that nation, but they will also gladly be helpful to me in correcting that evil with which their (3) fatherland (oh, that other lands, and especially our France, were also [so helped]) is so greatly infected.

->### Vaticanus.<-

It is worth noting why he says such things about the (1) Italians and French:

<sig>C ii</sig>
<meta>Catchword: In</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus criticizes Calvin’s absolute authority in Geneva, comparing his power to that of a monarch and detailing his strict enforcement of religious "novelties." Calvin responds by attributing the "errors" of his opponents in Italy and France to a divine punishment for their carelessness.</summary>

### A Conversation

# In all other countries,
Calvin is known more by his writings than by his manners <note>Or "character/conduct."</note>. From this it follows that many people who live in distant lands hold him for a God: because they believe that his life corresponds with his profession <note>His "Profession" refers to his public teaching and status as a reformer.</note>. Many come daily from Italy and France to Geneva, and through constant conversation they learn to know Calvin. And just as his humors <note>In the medical and psychological sense of the time, "humors" referred to one's temperament or disposition.</note> first began to break out occasionally, so they came openly to light regarding Servetus. For during the time that Servetus lay there imprisoned, there arrived by chance an Italian, a famous jurist <margin>This learned jurist seems to have been Matteo Gribaldi.</margin> <note>Matteo Gribaldi (c. 1500–1564) was a celebrated Italian legal scholar who initially admired Geneva but was horrified by the execution of Servetus.</note>, who, being told of Servetus, said among other things that he had never been of the opinion that anyone should be put to death for any opinion, even if it were heretical; adding also certain reasons. Further, discussing somewhat Servetus’s view on whether Christ, also according to his humanity, was the true and natural son of God—being born of God and the virgin—he said that such a view did not so much displease him; indeed, that he himself had held such a view from his youth. But the brothers <note>The "brothers" refers to the local ministers or the Genevan consistory.</note> were immediately so offended by this that they accused him of being a promoter of Servetus's views, and that for this reason, if he remained in such a view, he ought to be shunned as a heretic. He, being in doubt regarding such a high and weighty matter, desired that for his sake they would call a meet-

<column-break/>

-ing, so that he might present his reasons to them: or that they would at least allow him to hear Calvin (whose authority he had always held in high regard) on the matter, so that he might be better instructed in such a weighty cause. But this was refused him, which he took poorly and departed; and he wrote about this treatment to the brothers in Vicenza. Item <note>Latin: "Likewise" or "Also."</note>, one Lelio Sozzini <note>Original: "Lelius Kosinus." Lelio Sozzini (1525–1562) was an Italian humanist and reformer; his nephew Fausto later founded Socinianism.</note> sent a certain letter to his friend in Geneva, in which he wrote that Abel’s blood cried out to God, and that Cain <note>A reference to Calvin as the murderer (Cain) and Servetus as the victim (Abel).</note> for that reason would have no peace on earth. Item, Bernardino Ochino <note>A famous Italian reformer and former General of the Capuchin Order who fled to Geneva and later England.</note>, coming from England, arrived in Geneva the day after Servetus was burned; and when someone came to him and informed him of Servetus’s death, he answered in such a way that one could easily perceive he did not approve of that deed, because of which he came under suspicion <note>Literally: "came into the eye."</note>. In addition to this, there came from some of the brothers living in the Grisons <note>Original: "Ghetien." Most likely referring to the Grisons (Graubünden), a region in Switzerland where many radical Italian exiles settled.</note> certain *Carmina* <note>Latin: songs or poems.</note> or rhymes, in which it was said that one Servetus was burned by Calvin, but that countless others had become alive again: that his body was burned but his soul remained unharmed: and that if Christ himself came to Geneva, they would crucify him: that one should no longer go to Geneva for Christian liberty because there is now another Pope there, who inflicts a thousand torments on the living, whereas the Roman Pope first [strangles] the people <note>The text cuts off, but historically implies that the Roman Catholic Inquisition often strangled victims before burning them, whereas Calvin's Geneva was accused of burning them alive.</note>...

### Vaticanus and Calvin.

[It] becomes. <note>original: "Wordt." This likely completes a sentence from the previous page: "Thus it becomes clear..."</note> Whereby nearly all the Italians / even those who accepted Calvin's teaching / have been as it were offended by this cruelty / which also happened in many places in France. So that Calvin, through this, has fallen into bad repute with these nations. (2) He calls "impartial and peaceful" those / who agreed with him in his cruelty. (3) The best quality they have, Calvin calls an evil / namely / that they do not want to believe every man light-heartedly. The greatest vice with which the Italians are tainted / Calvin passes over in silence: I mean restless ambition <note>original: "waerckgiericheyt." This could mean a lust for works-based righteousness or a meddling, restless nature; the text suggests Calvin shares this trait.</note> / with which many Italians are inflamed. For the Italians are by nature either entirely evil or entirely good. But because Calvin is so tainted with this same evil / that he far surpasses them / he deliberately does not even touch upon it.

->Calvin. 9.<-

'Tis a common plague that prevails almost everywhere / that the more clever someone is in understanding / the more idle curiosity plagues him / and makes the heart throb; and it may well be that the Italians / because of their exceptionally clever understanding / excel in this most of all.

->Vaticanus.<-

He <note>Referring to Calvin.</note> would rather see clumsy and dull people / to whom he could pin whatever he wanted on their sleeves. He dreads clever understandings / and wished they did not exist so as not to be discovered; such a man was also the Sorcerer Alexander <note>Referring to Alexander of Abonoteichus, a 2nd-century false prophet described by Lucian as hating Christians and Epicureans because they saw through his tricks.</note> / who, being a false Prophet / allowed no Christians nor Epicureans near his conjuring-

<column-break/>

trickery. Calvin, proceeding / calls all those disciples of Servetus who will not agree with or help him in his cruelty / in order to thus bring them into hatred. This is a habit for some / that whoever will not give them their way in everything / they go about besmearing and blackening with many more vile names than the devils in hell have: for such a one is immediately an Atheist / a Libertine / an Anabaptist <note>original: "Wederdoper."</note> / and so forth; which manner of acting / they have learned from their Forefathers <note>A reference to the accusers of Jesus in the Gospels.</note> / who used to work thus: *He is a Samaritan / he has a devil in him / why do you listen to him?* <note>original: "hy is een Samaritaen / hy heeft dẽ duyvvel in / wat hoort ghy hem?" cf. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the negative reactions among European reformers to John Calvin's role in the execution of Michael Servetus. It focuses on the shift in Calvin's reputation from a distant, respected theologian to a man perceived as a tyrant by those who visited Geneva in person.</meta>

# Dialogue

In all other countries, Calvin is known more by his writings than by his conduct. From this it follows that many people living in distant lands hold him for a God, because they believe his life matches his profession <note>The author suggests that Calvin's followers in other countries only see his public "profession" of faith through his books, rather than his daily actions.</note>. Many come daily from Italy and France to Geneva, and through constant conversation, they come to know Calvin. And just as his tempers first began to break out occasionally, so they came openly to light regarding Servetus.

<margin>
This jurist appears to have been Matteo Gribaldi.
</margin>

For during the time that Servetus lay imprisoned there, an Italian happened to arrive—a renowned jurist—who, being told about Servetus, said among other things that he had never held the opinion that anyone should be put to death for any opinion, even if it were heretical; adding also certain reasons. Discoursing further a little on Servetus’s view—whether Christ, according to his humanity, was the true and natural son of God, being born of God and the virgin—he then said that such a view did not displease him so much; indeed, that he himself had held such a view from his youth. But the brothers <note>The "brothers" refers to the local church leadership or the Consistory of Geneva.</note> were immediately so offended by this that they told him he was a supporter <note>original: "dyper," likely a follower or partisan.</note> of Servetus’s view, and that he should therefore be shunned as a heretic if he remained in such an opinion. He, doubting such a high and weighty matter, requested that a meeting be held for his sake,

<column-break/>

so that he might present his reasons to them, or that they would at least allow Calvin (whose authority he had always held in high regard) to be heard on the matter, so that he might be better instructed in such a weighty cause. But this was refused to him; taking this poorly, he departed and wrote of this treatment to the brothers in Vicenza. <note>original: "Vincentien"</note> Likewise, one Lelio Sozzini <note>original: "Lelius Cosinus," a famous Italian reformer and antitrinitarian.</note> sent a certain letter to his friend in Geneva, in which he wrote that Abel’s blood cried out to God, and that Cain would therefore have no peace on earth. <note>This is a biblical metaphor: Sozzini is casting the executed Servetus as the innocent Abel and Calvin as the murderous Cain.</note> Likewise, Bernardino Ochino, <note>original: "Bernardinus Ochinus"</note> coming from England, arrived in Geneva the day after Servetus was burned; and when someone came to him to inform him of Servetus’s death, he answered in such a way that one could easily perceive he did not approve of that act, through which he became a marked man. In addition, from the brothers who live in the Grisons <note>original: "Ghetien," referring to a region in modern Switzerland that was a haven for religious refugees.</note> came some *poems* <note>original: "Carmina"</note> or rhymes, in which it was said that one Servetus was burned by Calvin, but that countless others had become alive again; that his body was burned, but his soul remained unharmed; and if Christ himself came to Geneva, they would crucify him. [They wrote] that one should no longer travel to Geneva for Christian freedom, because there is now another Pope who inflicts a thousand torments on the living, whereas the Roman Pope first strangles people <note>The Roman Inquisition often garroted (strangled) victims before burning them as a supposed act of mercy; the text argues Calvin was more cruel by burning Servetus alive without this "mercy."</note> [before burning].

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page is "Worcht" (strangles).</meta>

How almost all the Italians, even those who support Calvin’s teaching, were offended by this cruelty <note>original: "vreethept," a variant of "wreetheid" meaning cruelty or ferocity</note>, which also happened in many places in France. This is the manner of some: that whoever will not agree with them in everything, they go and smear and blacken with much viler names than the devils in hell have: for such a person is immediately an Atheist, a Libertine, an Anabaptist <term>Wederdoper: a member of a radical Reformation movement often persecuted by both Catholics and Protestants</term>, and so forth; which manner of doing they have learned from their Forefathers <note>referring to the religious authorities of the New Testament</note>, who used to work thus: "He is a Samaritan, he has a devil, why do you listen to him?" By which slander they brought the ignorant and simple people to the point that they would neither hear nor see the opposing party, and thus they held the victory before others were heard. After that, he says they are like the Midianites, although the Midianites were never as divided among themselves as he is with his own. And after he has taken it upon himself to write against Servetus, he answers for his deed thus, in order to bring others to sobriety.

If it is said against me that it is a faint-hearted struggle to attack the dead, and that it is a true sign of one's bad...

<summary>Vaticanus accuses Calvin of twisting Servetus's final request for forgiveness to make himself look innocent, arguing that Servetus only apologized for his angry tone, not his theological beliefs. Calvin defends himself by claiming he acted without personal spite and had tried to help Servetus for sixteen years.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between John Calvin and his critic, Vaticanus (a pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio). ->Dialogue<-

I could not have done such things without danger to my life; indeed, it was not for lack of effort on my part that all godly people did not offer him their hand, had he wished to be converted. Indeed, I also dealt with him gently through letters in private, and I never sought any glory for it. At last, I did not fail to show him every willing service until the time that he became so embittered by my admonishing reproofs, (2) that he was possessed much more by madness than by wrath <note>original: "gratschap," likely a typo for "gramschap" (anger/wrath)</note>.

### Vaticanus.

(1) He says that he never sought to avenge any wrong that was done to him in private, yet he burns so with a thirst for revenge that there is not even anyone in the Magistrate who dares to oppose him in the smallest thing, unless he wished to bring an irreconcilable enmity upon his own neck. And this is all too well known. It is worth noting from this man’s writings the "great, willing service" that he showed to Servetus. Calvin wrote Commentaries on the Gospel of John, and Robertus Stephanus <note>Also known as Robert Estienne, a famous printer and friend of Calvin</note> added a letter to the front of it, in which he wonderfully praises Calvin’s mildness <note>original: "sedicheyt," meaning modesty, sobriety, or mildness of character</note> in refuting the opinions of other people. On the first page of his Commentary, Calvin attacks Servetus thus: *Servetus, a Spaniard, the most rotten rogue.* <note>original: "Servetus een Spanjaert de alderrotste boeve." This is likely a translation of the Latin phrase Calvin used in his writings: "Servetus Hispanius, perditissimus nebulo."</note> See, there you have a small sample of Calvin’s mildness, which Robertus

<column-break/>

exalts so to the heavens. But Robertus does as all Calvin’s disciples do: they think so much of the man that they each strive to praise all his words and works without judgment, indeed, without even paying attention to them. Whoever praises this man most shall be the best in Heaven. I shall bring forward another example from which one may trace Calvin’s mildness toward Servetus. In his book *On Scandals*, <note>original: "boeck vã de ergernissen," referring to Calvin’s 1550 work *De Scandalis*</note> page 59, he writes thus: *To bring forward many examples of Servetus, who, being puffed up with a Spanish pride, thought to make a name for himself by overturning all the foundations of religion. For everything regarding the three persons in the Godhead <term>the Trinity</term> that has been accepted from the time of the Apostles, by the Church Fathers, and generally until now by all the godly—that he does not merely reject as a heap of smear <note>original: "gecfmeer" (filth/smear)</note>, but he knows not with what abusive words he shall portray that doctrine. This great madness alone, which makes him bite and bark like a raving dog—of which his writings are full—testifies sufficiently by what spirit this man is driven. If you come to the matter itself, you shall find that he is ignited with a vain gloriousness, and that he has run into nothing but the most absurd follies <note>original: "narierien," derived from "nar" (fool)</note> to make himself drunk therewith, etc.* Meanwhile, he throws together a heap of useless speculations that have no color <note>meaning no validity or plausibility</note> at all, so that anyone who is in his right mind can easily see that he can deceive no one through them, except those who are enchanted by self-love.

<summary>Vaticanus refutes Calvin's claim of private kindness toward Servetus by citing the harsh, insulting language Calvin used in his published theological commentaries and his book "On Scandals."</summary>

->**Vaticanus and Calvin.**<-

These are Calvin’s words; who will now believe that Calvin was prepared to put his life in peril for Servetus’s sake? Upon whom he—as a sign of his "kindness"—snarled and bit with greater madness than a possessed dog, to prove by what spirit he himself was driven. And yet he is not ashamed to speak with printed books before the whole world (2) what is to be known here and by those who burned Servetus alive.

See now here this man's godliness; he really ought at least to have spoken that without such rage. What he writes further here I shall skip over, since I do not have Servetus’s books, and it is also not without danger to believe Calvin. So he says then at the last:

### Calvin. 14.

After I gained nothing through admonishing and advising, I (1) did not wish to be wiser than my master <note>Calvin refers to Christ or the teachings of the Bible as his "master."</note> has taught me to be, (2) but have shunned the stubborn heretical person, according to the command of the Apostle Paul.

### Vaticanus.

He cunningly conceals what Servetus answered, though he nevertheless promised above to say what Servetus had confessed two hours before his death. (1) Our master teaches that one should first admonish someone who sins against us alone; after that, before two or three witnesses; finally, that one shall tell it to the congregation <note>Vaticanus is referencing the process of church discipline found in the Gospel of Matthew, chapter 18.</note>. Calvin’s first "admonitions" were those insults which we related above:

<column-break/>

the second was imprisonment; the third was the wood and the faggot <term>Mutsaert: a bundle of sticks or kindling used as fuel for the fire at an execution by burning</term>. This way of acting he did not learn from his master, Christ. (2) Calvin calls those "heretics" here who do not accept the teaching and admonition after they have been admonished several times. Well, let that be so for now, but that admonition must first be true and good. An admonition is good when it springs from upright love; but Calvin’s "admonition" was imprisonment. Such an admonition would surely make a friend into an enemy, far from it being able to make an enemy into a friend. An admonition is true when it is made concerning things that are true—indeed, which concerns such things through which the sinner who is being admonished finds and knows that he is sinning. 

By way of comparison: you tell a drunkard properly and kindly that he should improve himself; if he does not improve himself after you have admonished him several times, then he is stubborn and may be regarded as a publican <term>Publicaen: in a biblical context, a tax collector or social outcast, often grouped with "sinners" who are excluded from the religious community</term>. If Servetus then had been admonished by Calvin kindly and according to Christ’s command, and having seen his errors, had nevertheless remained stubborn in them, then Calvin might rightly call him a heretic. But that he was not such a person is clear enough from Calvin’s own words, when he says that Servetus asked him for forgiveness—which truly was not the work of a stubborn and obstinate heart. And I am certain that Calvin would not wish to [humble] himself so far—

### Calvin 15.

That I did not seek to dispute against him in public before the common people about such good and pleasant things <note>Calvin is likely referring to the doctrine of the Trinity, which he considered a "pleasant" and fundamental truth of the faith.</note> did not happen out of fear or trepidation; but all good, impartial judges will easily understand that it arose from my modesty and humility.

### Vaticanus.

These words will be better understood from page 663, where Calvin speaks as follows:

> When at last we had held long and various arguments on both sides, and he occasionally brought up his old complaint, saying: that one ought not to dispute about matters of divinity in prison: I answered, as was the truth, and as I had also given as an answer before, namely that nothing would be more pleasant to me than that one might dispute openly in the church before everyone about the matter. 

It was pleasant to both of them; how then did it come to pass that this did not happen? Did Servetus himself refuse it afterward? Why then does Calvin attribute this to his own modesty? By the "good and pleasant things," he understands the matter of the Trinity, which is so esteemed and pleasant among the common people because they 

<column-break/>

know of no other Trinity than the one the preachers daily portray to them.

### Calvin 16.

Indeed, I was somewhat afraid that, if I had undertaken to handle that matter with earnestness and purity, perhaps some malicious people and slanderers would have taken occasion from it to cry out that I was all too eager for battle.

### Vaticanus.

But why did you, shortly thereafter—after Servetus was thrown into prison—on a Sunday in a full church, present his views before the common people and explain them far and wide? Did you dare to fight better against him in his absence than in his presence?

### Calvin 17.

But dear me! What an improper courtesy that would be, to keep a man's shame covered with silence, and meanwhile place a thousand souls in peril of the devil's snares.

### Vaticanus.

If Servetus's errors are such nets and snares, then it is certain that you are the man who has placed at least a thousand souls in peril of the devil's snares, since you have stirred that filth <note>Vaticanus uses the word "Dreck" (dung/filth) to describe the controversial ideas Calvin is publicizing while trying to suppress them.</note> so much and hung it under everyone's nose. And although you play the blind man in many things, and bring many things forward in a crippled and broken <term>Gherae-braeckt: literally "broken on the wheel," meaning mangled or distorted arguments</term> manner, nevertheless many eyes will be opened by your book. However little you...

### Vaticanus and Calvin. 45

bring forward; yes, however much you mingle of your own among it, many will still be infected with that venom. ->**Calvin 18.**<-

->**Vaticanus.**<-

You yourself are the cause that they fly everywhere. For just as one did not regard Servetus’s first book much, one might also have easily let these other books be sold in silence. But now that you have burned the man with his books, everyone’s heart burns with a desire to read them: thinking that there must be something good in them, since you desired that the book should be burned and now do not stop attacking and smothering it with insults. You have concocted this whole little game yourself, and without you, it would not have come to this.

->**Calvin 19.**<-

I shall give no reasons for 

<column-break/>

this my writing, but rather for the punishments inflicted upon this man: for they lay everything on my neck <note>To "lay on one's neck" is an idiom meaning to hold someone solely responsible.</note> regarding what was done to him. Yet I will not deny that he was thrown into prison by my advice and doing. For according to the legal custom of this city, there had to be someone to accuse the defendant: and that I followed and managed the case thus far, I do not deny. But after the time he was convicted, I did not speak a single word regarding the punishment: which all good people can not only testify to, but I also give all detractors permission to prove otherwise.

->**Vaticanus.**<-

In the beginning, the rumor went that Servetus was imprisoned by Calvin's instigation, which Calvin's friends then denied in order to excuse him of that matter. But now Calvin comes and takes that excuse out of their hands and frankly confesses that he is the author of it; yet he adds that he did so with good right—though how true that is, we shall see hereafter.

->**Calvin 20.**<-

Therefore, it will be better that I take something else in hand.

->**Vaticanus.**<-

# Dialogue

but look closely at this man’s <note>Referring to Calvin.</note> opinion and advice; you will be terrified when you see it clearly. He takes it upon himself to prove—not only that he did well—but even that one ought to deal with all heretics <term>Ketters: individuals whose religious beliefs contradict established church doctrine</term> just as was done with Servetus. Haman’s counsel has come to his mind; he who was not satisfied with taking Mordecai’s life (once he learned he was a Jew) but would have gladly seen all the Jews throughout Ahasuerus’s kingdom put to death. <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Esther, where Haman plots a genocide.</note> In the same way, it is not enough for this new Haman of ours to have put one man to death, but he would gladly see all people in the world treated the same way, as we shall prove. For he desires that all heretics should be killed: and that all those who do not agree with him should be considered heretics. From which it would follow, according to his view, that one would have to kill all Papists, Lutherans, Zwinglians, Anabaptists, and furthermore all others who might exist; so that only the Calvinists, along with the Turks and Jews (for these he exempts), would possess the world. How much all these together differ from Calvin, we shall prove hereafter.

### Whether the Christian magistrate may punish heretics.

I beg the reader that he would please look quite impartially upon what we both have to say. My intention is to prove that Calvin, in order to justify this,

<column-break/>

->Calvin 22.<-

And just as I have to deal with two kinds of people, so I shall approach each separately as they deserve. Nowadays there are these unruly spirits who, if they were permitted to vomit up everything they have in their hearts, would dare to overthrow I-know-not-what through their unregulated audacity. Therefore, it is no wonder that such people are so fond of not punishing, which is indeed the mother of all unbridled behavior: all Epicurean and Lucianic spirits <note>Epicurus and Lucian of Samosata were historical figures often used as symbols for atheism or mockery of religion.</note> will also be very fond of this; they who, in all their luxury, lack only one thing: namely, that they are not permitted to cry out with a full mouth all their sacrileges and blasphemies, which they secretly and craftily stifle and brew among themselves.

<meta>This page continues the polemic between "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the execution of Michael Servetus. The text is a dialogue where Vaticanus uses the writings of Martin Luther and other reformers to prove that Calvin’s use of the death penalty for heresy contradicts the foundations of the Reformation itself.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

do you wish that to happen? Now there is indeed a dispute concerning matters of religion; why then do you shut the mouths of others? Truly for no other reason than because you, having a bad conscience, worry that your schemes <note>original: "kuerkens," referring to biased choices or private opinions</note> would come to light; and that you, being thus defeated, would be kicked from your ruling seat. But what will you think of *Luther*, whom you used to hold in such high esteem? Or will you now also consider him an *Epicurean* <term>Epicurean: here used as a slur for someone who lacks religious seriousness or believes only in material pleasure</term> or an ignorant fool? For in his *Postils* <term>Postillen: collections of sermons or commentaries on the Gospels and Epistles</term> on the 13th *Chapter of Matthew*, he speaks plainly against you as follows:

> "Look here at how insane we have been until now, we who have wanted to force the Turks to the faith with war, the heretics with fire, and the Jews with the fear of death and other threats! Just as if we were the people who had power over hearts and souls, and as if it stood in our hands to bring people to righteousness and godliness. If God does not do it, then it will never be done. With such a way of acting, we pull people away from the Word rather than toward it; for by killing them, we ensure that the Word cannot work in them. Indeed, we make ourselves (as much as is in us) into double murderers: first of the body, to which we bring temporal death, and thereafter also of the soul, which we send toward hell, bringing it into eternal death. And then we even boast that we have done God a service thereby, yes, even imagine for ourselves a special reward which we should receive hereafter in Heaven. By right, then, this passage should strike a terror into all those heretic-

<column-break/>

masters and murderers who, for all kinds of errors, so lightly kill people according to their own judgment. Truly, if these people did not have a forehead of iron, it would make them look back <note>meaning to repent or reconsider</note>, even if they truly had real heretics in their hands. Now, on the contrary, they burn the true saints and are heretics themselves. What else is that but pulling out the wheat under the cover of rooting out the weeds? <note>A reference to the Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13:24-30.</note> That is what these insane people achieve, and nothing else." Do you see now, *Calvin*, that *Luther* has now condemned you in this matter? And you, in turn, are scolded by him as a heretic, a murderer, and one who has a forehead of iron and is insane. Boast now of the unity that exists between you two—and there are many more besides this one man *Luther*. A book has been printed, the title of which is: *Whether one may persecute heretics* <note>original: "Ofmen de Ketters mach vervolgen," referring to the Latin work "De haereticis an sint persequendi" published by Castellio under a pseudonym in 1554</note>; in which book many authors are pointed out who are all likewise condemned by you. For in it are cited *Augustine*, *Jerome*, *Hilary*, *Chrysostom*, *Theophylact*, *Pellican*, *Luther*, *Brenz*, *Otto Brunfels*, *Urbanus Rhegius*, *Caelius Secundus*, *S. Castellio*, *Georgius Kleinberg*, and *John Calvin* himself, and furthermore others more who are all against *Calvin*. And these *John Calvin* binds all together in a bundle with himself and throws them onto the fire on top of *Servetus*. Such an offering he makes here to his dead enemy; to this his great vengefulness brings him. And still he dares to say that those people commit sacrilege and schism...

# Dialogue

just look at this man's opinion and advice; you will be terrified when you look closely at it. He takes it upon himself to prove, not only that he has done well, but even that one ought to deal with all **heretics** <term>heretics: those holding religious beliefs at odds with established church doctrine</term> in the same way as was done with **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian executed in Geneva in 1553 for heresy</note>. The counsel of **Haman** has come into his mind; it was not enough for him to strike at the life of **Mordecai** (since he had learned that he was a Jew), but he would have gladly seen all the Jews who were in the kingdom of **Ahasuerus** <note>Reference to the Book of Esther, where Haman plots the genocide of the Jews</note> put to death. In the same way, it is not enough for this new Haman of ours to have brought one man to his death, but he would gladly see all people in the world treated in such a way, as we shall prove. For he wants all heretics to be killed: and he wants all those who do not agree with him to be considered heretics. From which it would follow, according to his view, that one would have to put to death all **Papists** <term>Papists: a derogatory term for Roman Catholics</term>, **Lutherans**, **Zwinglians**, **Anabaptists**, and furthermore all others who might exist; so that only the **Calvinists**, along with the **Turks** and **Jews** (for these he excludes), would possess the world. For we shall prove hereafter that all these differ from Calvin.

### Calvin. 21.

### Whether the Christian authorities may punish heretics.

### Vaticanus

I wish to ask the reader to please pay impartial attention to both of our statements. My intention is to prove that Calvin, in order to prove his point,

<column-break/>

does not bring forward a single reason that stands firm or is clothed with any authority. And that this comes from nothing other than a simple desire to be the sole master, and from an insatiable bloodthirstiness. If I cannot prove this, I am content to be condemned by all men.

### Calvin 22.

And just as I have to deal with two kinds of people, so I shall also approach each of them separately as they deserve. Today there are these **trouble-makers** <note>original: "werregeesten," meaning chaotic or factious spirits</note>, who, if they were permitted to vomit up everything they have in their hearts, would dare to overthrow I know not what through their unregulated boldness. Therefore it is not to be wondered at that such people are so fond of not punishing, which is indeed the mother of all lawlessness: here also all **Epicurean** and **Lucianist** spirits <term>Epicurean and Lucianist: terms used here as slurs for skeptics, atheists, or those seen as mocking religion</term> will find much to like, for in all their luxury they lack only one thing: namely, that they are not permitted to cry out with a full mouth all their church-robberies and blasphemies against God, which they secretly and craftily smother and cook up among one another.

### Vaticanus.

I ask you, Master Calvin, if you were in a dispute with someone over some inheritance, and if he demanded of the judge that he alone should be allowed to speak, and that you should always have to remain silent, would you not say that you were being done an injustice? Why then do you do to another what you do not want done to yourself?

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

Shall that happen? Now there is indeed a dispute over religious matters; why then do you shut another’s mouth? Truly for no other reason than because you, having a bad conscience, fear that your little tricks might come to light, and that you, being thus defeated, would be kicked out of your ruling seat. For what will you take **Luther** <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the central figure of the Protestant Reformation</note> for, whom you used to hold in such high regard: or will you now also consider him an **Epicurean** <term>Epicurean: here used as a derogatory term for someone who is godless or seeks only worldly pleasure</term> or an ignorant fool? For in his *Postils* <note>original: "Postillen" — a collection of sermons or commentaries on the Gospels and Epistles</note> on the 13th chapter of Matthew, he speaks plainly against you as follows:
> Look here once how insane we have been until now, we who wanted to force the Turks with war, the heretics with fire, and the Jews with the fear of death and other threats to the faith! As if we were the people who had power over hearts and souls: and as if it stood in our hand to bring people to righteousness and godliness. If God does not do it, it will never be done. By such a manner of acting, we rather draw people away from the Word, for by killing them, we ensure that the Word cannot work in them: yes, making ourselves (as much as in us lies) into double murderers; first of the body, to which we inflict temporal death, thereafter also of the soul, which we send toward hell, bringing it into eternal death; and then we still boast of ourselves, that we have thereby done God a service, yes, imagining for ourselves a special reward which we should receive hereafter in Heaven. Rightly then should this passage strike all those heretic-

<column-break/>

masters and murderers with terror: who for all sorts of errors, according to their own discretion, so easily put people aside <note>meaning: to kill or execute them</note>. Truly, if these people did not have an **iron forehead** <note>original: "ijseren voorhooft" — a biblical idiom for being shameless or stubbornly unrepentant</note>, it would make them look back, even if it were true that they had real Heretics in their hands. Now, on the contrary, they burn the true saints and are Heretics themselves. What else is that but pulling out the wheat under the pretense of rooting out the weeds? That is what these insane people achieve, and nothing else. Do you see well now, **Calvin**, that Luther is now condemned by you here, and you in turn are scolded by him as a Heretic, a murderer, and one who has an iron forehead and is insane? Boast now of the unity that exists between you both: and there are yet many more than this one man Luther. A book has been printed whose title is: *Whether one may persecute Heretics:* <note>A reference to "De haereticis, an sint persequendi," a collection of texts against persecution edited by Castellio himself in 1554</note> in which book many authors are pointed out who are all likewise condemned by you. For in it are pointed out **Augustine**, **Jerome**, **Hilary**, **Chrysostom**, **Theophylact**, **Pellicanus**, **Luther**, **Brenz**, **Otto Brunfels**, **Urbanus Rhegius**, **Caelius Secundus**, **S. Castellio**, **Georgius Benbergius**, and even **John Calvin** himself, and furthermore still others more who are all against Calvin; and which John Calvin, binding them all together in one bundle with himself, throws onto the fire with **Servetus**. Such an offering he makes here to his dead enemy; to that his great vengefulness brings him. And still he dares to say that those people commit sacrilege and schism <note>original: "kerckroverie eñ schederie" — the text cuts off at "schederie," likely meaning "scheurderie" (schism/division)</note>.

### Calvin 24.

In the same way, there is also no law against the fierce / greedy / and vengeful / as long as they keep those flaws to themselves / and do not put them into action. # Vaticanus and Calvin.

...that they must be postponed. Therefore / no punishment is softened or changed in the Law: but in the New Testament / punishment is ordained / and one must fish it out from there.

### Calvin 26.

Somewhere another madman <note>original: "dolhooft" — literally 'mad-head' or 'maniac'</note> comes crawling out of his hole / this one calls **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus, the Spanish physician burned at the stake in Geneva in 1553</note> his dear brother / and for that reason denies that one must kill heretics / and says that everyone, according to his own head / makes a particular sense of the Holy Scripture / so that the certain truth is clothed with dark clouds and the light is covered.

### Vaticanus.

It displeases the man that there is anyone who says that Holy Scripture is dark: he believes that it is bright and clear. In this, he conflicts with **Zwingli** <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), a leader of the Reformation in Switzerland, who held different views on the clarity of certain scriptures</note> / who believes that it is dark: indeed, he conflicts with himself / who, in explaining these "clear" scriptures / has had countless commentaries and interpretations printed. In this, he conflicts with all those who up until now have written so many books upon it / and still write daily / which are so many in number / that no one could read them all / even if he lived three men’s lifetimes / he himself, in the preface of his **Institutes** <note>Calvin’s "Institutes of the Christian Religion," his foundational work of Protestant systematic theology</note> / says that his *Institutes* are a necessary instrument / to understand the Holy Scripture. Yes, it is certain that for many years now he has daily preached / written / and disputed / to explain the scriptures / which he nevertheless says are so clear.

### Calvin 27.

And thus this "good interpreter" would rather call faith out of all

<column-break/>

men’s hearts / than to allow that those should be punished / who seek to weaken and loosen the same.

### Vaticanus.

He does not call faith out of men's hearts / when he wants the punishment of heretics to be postponed until the arrival of the Judge <note>Meaning Christ at the Last Judgment</note>: unless you would wish to accuse Christ himself / who commands that one shall let the weeds <note>original: "tonkrupt" (onkruid) — a reference to the Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13, where the weeds are left until the harvest</note> remain until the harvest. He also does not seek to shield them from punishment / who desires that they be punished in due time by God / and not too early by men.

### Calvin 28.

(1) What religion shall there be? (2) By what signs shall the church be known? (3) Indeed, what shall Christ himself be / if the (4) doctrine of godliness is doubtful and uncertain?

### Vaticanus.

(1) It shall be founded on a certain faith in things that are hoped for / and not seen: Such was Abraham's / who, being commanded to depart / did so / although he did not know where he was going: being commanded to offer up his son / he complied / although the outcome was not known to him / yet his faith was firm and certain / because the faithful God had made him the promise. (2) By the love which springs out of faith / of which we have a certain command. By this (He says) shall men know that you are my disciples / if you love one another.

For faith is a certainty of things that one does not see. Blessed are those who have not seen and yet have believed. (4) The teaching of **godliness** <term>godliness: (Dutch: Godsalicheydt) a term for living a life of active piety and moral rectitude</term> is to love your enemies, to do well to those who do you evil, to hunger and thirst for righteousness; blessed are those who suffer persecution for the sake of righteousness. ### ->Calvin 29.<-

But what a shameful thing it would be before God, that one should deem Him to be so ambiguous <note>original: "dobbelsinnich" — literally double-meaning or equivocal</note> in His words, that He would want to mock us in a jesting manner; for if it were true that no certain Religion has been revealed to us in the Holy Scripture, then it must follow that God is only keeping us busy in vain with a heap of detours, indeed, deceptions.

### ->Vaticanus.<-

<margin>2 Cor. 3</margin>
Indeed, what a shameful thing it would be for God if He were *not* ambiguous: for if He spoke clearly and plainly, then He would be throwing pearls before swine, seeing as there are many swine who read His word. First, Paul teaches that the Jews have a veil over their faces when they read the scriptures of the Old Testament,

<column-break/>

and that they cannot see Christ, who is the end of the law: so that the entire law of the Jews was nothing other than pure ambiguity. And Moses himself teaches this also, saying: <margin>Deut. 29:29</margin> "The secret things belong unto the Lord our God, but those things which are revealed belong unto us and to our children forever, that we may do all the words of this law." It is as if he wanted to say: that which is signified through this law, and the ceremonies, and other things, is hidden from us; but that which He teaches us to do—such as to love God and our neighbor, not to kill or steal, likewise to offer sacrifices and so forth—those things are bright and clear to us; at least as far as the obedience required to perform them is concerned, even if we do not exactly know what is signified by them. For truly, this is the first step of true faith and obedience: to trust God so much that you perform what He commands to the death, even if you do not know what purpose it serves. **Isaiah** himself calls his prophecies a sealed book, which neither the unlettered nor the learned shall be able to read or understand. At the end of **Daniel’s** book, it is written that the prophecy shall be hidden and sealed until the end times, and shall be understood by none of the wicked. In the Psalms, there are certain riddles <note>original: "raedtselen" — could also be translated as 'dark sayings' or 'parables'</note> which are nothing other than pure ambiguities. Here one must surely admit that God is ambiguous and obscure; nevertheless, it does not follow (as Calvin says) that He would mock us in a jesting manner. For it is an honor for God to conceal His word. <margin>Prov. 25:2</margin>

<meta>This page continues the debate between John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio) regarding the clarity of the Bible.
