Here, Vaticanus counters by arguing that Christ intentionally used parables and that true faith requires accepting mysteries we cannot fully comprehend, rather than obsessing over complex doctrines like Predestination.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

<column-break/>

...and Calvin thinks that it would be a shame. One cannot say either that there would be no ambiguity <note>original: "dubbelstinnicheydt" — likely a typo for "dubbelsinnicheydt," meaning double-meaning or equivocation</note> hidden in the New Testament, for it is written that Christ
<margin>Matt. 4. 33. 34.</margin>
did not speak to the Jews without a parable (now those are certainly ambiguities), but that he explained all things to his Disciples in private. From which it appears that the Holy Scripture can be understood by no one other than Christ’s true Disciples. And these are Christ’s true Disciples: those who are obedient to him and have love, as was said above. To all other men, however greatly experienced they might be in all arts and languages, they [the scriptures] are but a snare. The revelation of John is certainly full of darkness <note>meaning obscurity or mystery</note>, which can only be understood by the wise, that is, by the god-fearing.
<margin>Apoc. 5.</margin>
For it is a sealed book, which can only be opened by the Lamb. Paul himself prophesied very obscurely about the **Antichrist**, the last trumpet, and the coming of Christ. Indeed, all things are full of riddles and mysteries, which shall serve to the highest honor of God when he shall reveal them hereafter to his own, that is, to the **Godly**. And yet Calvin thinks that it would be a shame for God if he were ambiguous and obscure, whereas true faith consists in this: that one gives belief to the dark and ambiguous words that one does not understand, and has a firm trust that He is truthful. Someone might ask: Well, is there then nothing certain? I answer: Yes, namely that which is necessary for us to obtain salvation, to obey God,

<column-break/>

and to do our bounden duty. There is no doubt that those things are all certain which are in the Holy Scripture. It is certain that Jesus is the Christ, that he is the Son of God, and that he has performed everything that is described of him. It is certain that within our time many gross and clumsy errors have come to light, which one could almost touch with one’s hands. All the commandments of **Godliness** <term>Godliness: (Dutch: Godsalicheyt) a state of active, lived piety and moral devotion</term> are certain, such as to love God, to love one’s enemies, regarding patience, mercy, kindness, and other similar obligations. But we, throwing behind the bench <note>original: "achter den banck werpende" — an idiom meaning to ignore or cast aside</note> what we must do, are always busy with the things that God wants to do, just as if we had been his counselors. We spend much time and dispute over **Predestination** and the **Trinity**, in which we have never seen much certainty, and that which lies right before our nose we set to one side. From this spring innumerable disputes, which are ended with the shedding of the blood of poor and weak people when they simply will not agree with us.

### ->Calvin. 30.<-

And what remains then, other than that these people, wanting to pave a smooth path for their wantonness, seek to entirely renounce Holy Scripture, since it stands in their way.

### ->Vaticanus<-

That is certainly a smooth lie, for he does not take the Ark <note>The Ark of the Covenant, symbolizing the sacred and hidden mysteries of God</note> away who says that it is closed.

# Dialogue

-> ### Calvin. 31. ### <-

Meanwhile, it remains even clearer that what I said above is true: namely, that those Lucians <note>referring to followers of Lucian of Samosata, a classical satirist often used as a label for scoffers or atheists</note> and dull despisers of God—indeed, also all those mutineers and rebellious spirits—seek nothing else but to make their case appear smooth and fair. They do this so that they might throw everything into confusion <note>original: "int warre garen," literally "into tangled yarn"</note> without fear of being punished, specifically when they do not want the authorities to protect Religion or defend it through the punishments of the laws.

-> ### Vaticanus. ### <-

Here he wretchedly condemns his own Luther and Augustine—indeed, all those men whom we named above—and says that all of them together, freely and frankly, seek to confuse everything because they do not wish to be punished. He is a cunning fox, and resembles certain murderers who warn a traveler to watch out for murderers, so that they themselves might not fall under suspicion. So does Calvin here as well; for since he seeks nothing else than to throw everything into confusion, he shifts the blame for that onto others so that people would not suspect it of him. It is indeed a certain fact that all things are most of all confused by persecutions (which he would gladly arrange), just as he, through the killing of Servetus <note>Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian burned for heresy in Geneva in 1553</note>, helped to unsettle not only his Geneva, but almost the whole of Europe, and

<column-break/>

has weakened and troubled the hearts of many people. When he says that others seek nothing but to make their case smooth and fair, he is describing himself. For it was not so long ago that he himself was persecuted, and at that time he wrote quite differently about persecution than he does now. And so that no one may doubt it, I shall here bring forth his own words, as they stand in his *Institutes* <note>original: *Institutien*; Calvin’s seminal work of Protestant systematic theology</note> which he first published: for in those *Institutes* which he later had printed, to suit his own ends <note>original: "om sijnen naet te napen," an idiom meaning to tailor things to his own advantage</note>, he overturned and changed everything. He says then in Chapter 2, page 144, regarding the excommunicated <note>those cast out from the church community</note>, as follows: "Those indeed who do not agree with us in the same points of faith, or even if they confess them with their mouths, yet deny God—whom they confess with their mouths—with their works (such as those who have been rogues and rascals throughout their whole lives, and are lost in the lust of sins: yes, who rest and sleep peacefully in their sins), all these reveal themselves enough that they are currently no members of the church. To this end excommunications are established, so that by the same might be banished and warded off all those who, while making a false profession of the Christian faith, are yet nothing other than scandals in the church through their unrestrained and godless lives, and are therefore unworthy to bear the name of a Christian. Firstly, so that they are not named among Christians to the slander of God's name, as if his holy congregation were a gathering <note>original: "t'ſamenrottinge," implying a mob or a gang</note> of all kinds of rogue-hunters."

<meta>This page continues the debate between "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The previous page ended with Vaticanus accusing Calvin of hypocrisy regarding religious persecution. This page transitions into a discussion on the purpose of church discipline and the limits of human judgment regarding who is "saved" or "damned."</meta>

# vaticanus and calvin. #

Secondly, so that they do not corrupt others through daily conversation by the example of their godless life. Finally, so that they may feel shame for their own wickedness and might repent, and thus learn to better themselves. Of such people, one may judge that for that time they do not belong to the church, as far as we can see, and according to that rule of knowledge of which we have spoken; Yet one must take care not to despair of them, as if they were entirely rejected by God. Indeed, it is completely forbidden to scratch anyone from the number of the <term>elect</term> <note>The "elect" refers to those predestined by God for salvation, a central concept in Calvinist theology.</note>, or to despair of them in such a way as if they were completely and utterly rejected by God: unless it appears certainly from God's word that such are already certainly judged and damned: as if someone willingly and knowingly, with a set malice, opposed the truth in order to suppress the Gospel, and to root out the name of God, and to resist the Holy Spirit. For over these God has already passed judgment, as he says that the sin against the Holy Spirit shall not be forgiven, neither in this, nor in the coming age. Yet this can very seldom be known by us (if it is at all possible that we could ever know it in any way) so that it is more advisable to wait for the day of revelation, and not lightly go before God in his judgments. Let us not be too quick in condemning, unless we wanted to set a limit to God's power and a law to his mercy: who so often

<column-break/>

as it pleases Him, can change the worst into the best, yes, makes the strangers become <term>ingrafted</term> <note>A biblical metaphor from Romans 11, referring to being joined to the community of faith like a branch onto a tree.</note>, and those who are outside become accepted as members of the church, so that He might thus deceive the false opinions of men, and put their boldness to shame, and that they might learn thereby not to judge their neighbor otherwise than it is permitted to them. Much rather should one labor toward this, that we altogether might judge one another from good hearts, and have the very best feeling: that we interpret one another's words and works for the best, and not like suspicious people, interpret or take all things wrongly and at the worst. Is there someone so godless that one could feel nothing good of him? let us surrender him to God, and commend him to His goodness; and let us thus (feeling better of them) maintain love and peace, and not foolishly step into the most secret judgments of God; let us not put ourselves in the dark niches of errors. And to make this short, let us not condemn the person himself who is in God's hands to death, but let us only judge from God's law (which is the only rule of good and evil) what kind of work each one's is. In this sense and meaning must excommunications be understood and held, not that they who are excluded by men from the fellowship of the church should also be cast out from the hope of salvation, but so that they might be brought back onto the right way, just as Paul writes that he had [delivered] the man to Satan <note>Referring to 1 Corinthians 5:5, where the Apostle Paul advocates for a form of excommunication intended to lead to the eventual salvation of the spirit.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (the pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The previous page discussed the limits of human judgment and the possibility of repentance even for those who seem lost. This page continues that argument, contrasting Calvin’s written theories on church discipline with his actual treatment of Michael Servetus.</meta>

# dialogue #

...handed over for the destruction of the flesh, so that the spirit might be saved on the day of the Lord <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 5:5, a passage often cited to explain that excommunication is intended to lead a sinner to repentance rather than to destroy them.</note>. This means, in my opinion, that he has cast him into temporal suffering so that he might be saved in eternity. Therefore, although church **Discipline** <note>The system of rules and punishments used by the church to maintain moral and doctrinal order.</note> does not allow one to maintain familiar friendship with the **Excommunicated**, we are nevertheless bound to do our utmost and use all possible means—whether through exhortation and teaching, or through friendliness and gentleness, as well as through prayers to God—to bring them back to the unity and fellowship of the church through their repentance. Indeed, one must act this way not only toward these people, but also toward the **Turks** and **Saracens** <note>Historical terms used in the 16th century to refer to Muslims.</note> and all other enemies of religion. Far be it from anyone to praise those methods by which people have sought to force them into our faith until now—namely, by denying them water and fire (which are, after all, common elements) and refusing them all human services, and even pursuing them with shield and spear. This is what **Calvin** says. Now go and compare all this to what he does and writes here; one will find as great a disparity between them as there is between light and darkness. For now that he is in his power, he plays his game and advances his cause. And because he has put **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553) was a Spanish theologian and physician executed in Geneva for heresy, an event that sparked intense debate over religious tolerance.</note> to death, he wants everyone who does not agree with him to be put to death as well; yes, he wants these persons to be condemned to death against his own teaching and rule. For this reason, it is no wonder that he

<column-break/>

wished that everyone else had to remain silent. For he undoubtedly fears that this slight change and instability of his might be brought to light by someone. He hates the light because his works are evil. Nor can he excuse and defend himself by wanting to say that he was then only speaking of those who committed some small and minor sins. For he writes of such people (I shall use his own words) "whom we see have been scoundrels and rascals throughout their entire lives, and are drowned in the lust for sin, yes, who lie and sleep peacefully in their wickedness." It is certainly not to be believed that **Servetus** was such a man, even if everything that **Calvin** says about him were true. And even if he had been such a man, **Calvin** should have acted toward him according to his own rule, and not have fought so shamefully against himself.

->Calvin. 32.<- <note>Likely a reference to a specific section or page in one of Calvin's published works, such as the *Institutes of the Christian Religion*.</note>

But now I turn to the good and simple people who fall through ignorance and are deprived of a sincere and good cause, especially when they are offended by the cruel **Edicts** <note>Original: "Placaten," referring to the formal proclamations or laws issued by authorities.</note> of the **Papists** <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics.</note>. For they see that although religion in the **Papacy** is so godlessly contaminated and riddled with countless heresies and errors, nevertheless this "devilish confusion and hodgepodge" is maintained by sheer threats, so that under penalty of death one may not utter a single word against it.

<summary>This page critiques John Calvin for hypocrisy, arguing that his harsh treatment and execution of Michael Servetus contradicts his own earlier writings on the importance of gentleness and prayer in church discipline. The author also compares the forceful tactics used in Geneva to the "cruel edicts" of the Catholic Church.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Michael Servetus, excommunication, church discipline, religious tolerance, Papacy, heresy, Geneva, Sebastian Castellio</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (the pseudonym used by the humanist scholar Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The previous page contrasted Calvin's earlier writings on mercy and church discipline with his later actions in Geneva. On this page, Castellio (as Vaticanus) confronts Calvin with his own words to highlight the hypocrisy of his role in the execution of Michael Servetus.</meta>

-># Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

### Vaticanus.

Who would not jump out of their skin <note>A Dutch idiom meaning to be moved to extreme outrage or indignation.</note> when they hear the devil preaching the Passion? <note>A proverbial expression used to describe a hypocrite who speaks of holy things while acting wickedly.</note> Just listen, good people: the man seems to forget himself when he condemns in others the very thing he does himself. A shameless "wooden face," <note>original: "Een houten aengesicht," implying someone who is unblinking and shows no remorse or shame.</note> who throughout this entire book of his is busy with nothing else but defending his own views with the sword and bringing down all who contradict him. He complains about the tyranny and threats of others—he, who has struck such terror into all of Geneva that even the Burgomasters <note>The city magistrates or mayors of Geneva.</note> (except for those who are his open enemies) do not dare to utter a word.

### Calvin. 33.

Truly, it is not without reason that people are offended when they see the truth of God so oppressed by the sword that no one dares to embolden themselves to help and protect it. And that errors are so forcefully maintained under the cloak of legal judgment that there is no freedom to rebuke or challenge them. It is about this that I and all good people complain so piteously. Thus, we do not only groan about it in secret, but bewail with a loud voice that the world is now brought under such great slavery that people will neither hear nor see: indeed, that those who rule under the title of Supreme Sovereignty will not suffer a single word that is in any way contrary to their decrees; and what is more, that

<column-break/>

they cannot endure that anyone should even doubt or investigate those decrees.

### Vaticanus.

I cannot attack the man more harshly than Calvin here attacks himself; for he does everything himself that he punishes in other people.

### Calvin. 34.

It is an unbearable cruelty when there is no knowledge of the truth at hand, yet one still wants to maintain one's position with the sword—something that cannot be defended with even a shadow of reason.

### Vaticanus.

Why then do you maintain your own position with the sword? Why was no freedom of speech given to Hieronymus Gribaldus, <note>Matteo Gribaldi (c. 1500–1564), an Italian jurist who was forced to flee Geneva because his views on the Trinity differed from Calvin’s.</note> Servetus, <note>Michael Servetus, the physician and theologian burned at the stake in Geneva in 1553 for heresy, an event that sparked this entire debate on tolerance.</note> and many others? Or does one find "freedom" with you as one finds a rope in the house of a hanged man? <note>A reference to the proverb: "Mention not a rope in the house of one who was hanged," implying that Calvin's talk of freedom is an insult to those he has executed or exiled.</note> If you have good reasons, why do you not rely on them? Why do you want to maintain everything with power and violence? You once wrote to the King of Denmark <note>Christian III of Denmark, to whom Calvin dedicated his commentary on Acts in 1552, discussing the nature of religious authority.</note> that wisdom is driven out wherever everything is defended with the sword: why then have you now driven wisdom away?

### Calvin. 35.

But when all the Holy Prophets and Apostles were treated in this same way, they did not take their refuge in the idea that it was not permitted to protect the service of God and his heavenly Teaching...

<meta>catchword: Teaching</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Vaticanus (Castellio) accuses Calvin of extreme hypocrisy, using Calvin’s own previous writings against religious persecution to condemn his current tyrannical rule in Geneva and the execution of Michael Servetus.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Sebastian Castellio, religious tolerance, Geneva, Michael Servetus, Matteo Gribaldi, hypocrisy, the sword vs. the word, persecution</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (the pseudonym used by the humanist scholar Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The discussion centers on the definition of a "heretic" and whether the state has the right to execute those who disagree on the interpretation of scripture. Vaticanus argues that differing interpretations do not equate to the "godless" rejection of God.</meta>

...to protect doctrine through legal power, so that one might punish, according to God’s command, those who godlessly sought to incite rebellion against it. Truly, God has ordained a severe judgment against those who sought to lead the people away from the true Religion.

->Vaticanus.<-

We do not deny that either. But herein lies the difference: who these godless inciters are. For one must not immediately consider everyone as godless inciters of the Heavenly doctrine simply because they differ from Calvin in things that are currently being disputed. I will bring a small example here to clarify the matter. Before and during the times of Christ, there were four Sects among the Jews: the Scribes, Pharisees, Sadducees, and Essenes. Each of these held the Law in equal regard, but one interpreted it in a different way than the others. Now, had any of these dared to forsake the Law, or to godlessly incite against it, and sought to lead his followers astray to serve Jupiter <note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology; here used to represent a pagan idol.</note> or any other Idol, he would have been rightfully punished. Therefore, the Maccabees <note>A group of Jewish rebel warriors who led a revolt against the Seleucid Empire and preserved Jewish traditions.</note> rightfully punished those who had introduced certain Greek ceremonies into the Jewish Synagogues. But not a tittle is found <gloss>it is found</gloss> in the Law suggesting that one should punish those Sects who did not seek to godlessly and with a willful intent incite against the Law, but only erred in the interpreting and glossing <term>glossing: the practice of providing explanatory notes or interpretations for a specific word or passage</term> of that same Law. Likewise, there are today many Sects among the Christians, who all together have a good and

<column-break/>

divine feeling regarding the Gospel, but one interprets it differently than the other. Now, were there someone who completely forsook the Gospel, of which he had previously made profession, and spoke slanderously of God and Christ, or shamelessly blasphemed the same—for such a man I would not wish to speak. But if there is someone who, believing the words of Christ, yet interprets them somewhat differently than we do, I cannot believe that such a man is a godless inciter of Religion: for there is a great distinction between godlessness and error. Had Calvin been a Pharisee, he would have killed all the Scribes, Sadducees, and Essenes; nor would he have wanted to let them live to be taught by Christ. Yet I believe that before Christ’s coming, one could not call them heretics; but after Christ had come, then they all became heretics and stubborn ones who, having been warned and taught by Him, nevertheless did not want to obey Him. Likewise, I also consider that the sects existing today should not be called heretics: but when the "mouth and wisdom" (Luke 21:15) <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke where Jesus promises: "For I will give you a mouth and wisdom, which all your adversaries shall not be able to gainsay nor resist."</note> which no one shall be able to contradict arrives, whoever is not obedient to that, him I shall hold for a heretic. Were Calvin’s wisdom such wisdom <gloss>wisdom</gloss>, then I would not be able to appropriately contradict it. From which it also appears that it is not such wisdom. Therefore, one must await Christ the judge, for it does not befit us (as Calvin has well said above) to presumptuously anticipate God in His judgment.

<summary>In this passage, Vaticanus (Castellio) argues that differing interpretations of the Gospel among Christian sects do not constitute godless heresy, drawing a historical parallel with Jewish sects in Christ's time. He rebukes Calvin for confusing honest error with willful blasphemy and cautions against human authorities usurping God's role as the final judge.</summary>

<keywords>heresy vs error, religious tolerance, Sebastian Castellio, John Calvin, Jewish sects, Scribes and Pharisees, biblical interpretation, Luke 21:15, divine judgment, Maccabees</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the polemical exchange between the reformer John Calvin and his critic "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio). Following the discussion of whether the Mosaic Law's punishments for idolatry should apply to Christian "heretics," the debate now turns to specific biblical precedents, specifically the trials of the prophet Jeremiah and the conduct of the Apostles.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

->Calvin. 36.<-

Jeremiah, having been dragged toward death by wicked tyrants and thrown into the pit, did not take his refuge in the argument that one ought not to defend and protect the service of God with the sword; rather, he used as a reason this: namely, that the enemies of the truth shall be plagued if they shed innocent blood.

->Vaticanus.<-

This example is brought forward here entirely impertinently <note>In this context, "impertinently" means "irrelevant" or "unfitting" to the argument.</note>, seeing as Jeremiah had not yet been thrown into the pit; Calvin has forgotten this, but we shall easily forgive him this fault, even though he himself is unwilling to endure anyone else's errors. Jeremiah was imprisoned by the Priests and false Prophets, who sought to inflict a terrible death upon him because he had prophesied against Jerusalem. Therefore, Jeremiah spoke to them thus: "Behold, I am in your hands; deal with me as seems good to you. But this you must know: if you kill me, it shall come to pass that this city and its inhabitants shall be punished for the sake of innocent blood." Now, you see clearly that nothing is being said here regarding Religion, but rather regarding a murder. He does not threaten them if they should reject the truth, but if they should shed innocent blood. He also does not threaten them with the Magistrate (for they wanted to kill him themselves, and not through the Magistrate), but he threatens them with God. And just as this example has been brought forward, it cannot serve Calvin, but serves wonderfully well for Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish physician and theologian executed in Geneva for heresy, whose death sparked this literary debate.</note>. For

<column-break/>

Servetus could have said, against the Priest Calvin and against those of Geneva: "Behold, I am in your hands; yet if you kill me, you shall be punished by God as murderers." In this same Prophet Jeremiah, there are many more beautiful examples that would fit better here, yet they are all flatly against Calvin. Jeremiah admonishes Zedekiah <note>The last king of Judah before the destruction of Jerusalem by Babylon.</note> that he should surrender himself into the hands of the Chaldeans, and says if he refused to do so, he would be severely punished by the Lord; but here not a word is spoken of the Magistrate. He also admonished the false Prophet Ananias <note>Usually referred to as Hananiah in English Bibles; a prophet who opposed Jeremiah.</note> that he should cease to lead the people astray with his false flattery and prophecy; and as he still persisted in his purpose, Jeremiah declared to him that he would die a death which would be inflicted upon him, not by the Magistrate, but by God.

->Calvin 37.<-

What did the Apostles do? Did they also rebuke their enemies—those who wished to protect the service of God according to the punishments ordained in the Law? No; they, trusting their good cause, were willing to be subject to all severity, if they were found to have committed a crime in any way.

->Vaticanus.<-

They also did not punish those who wished to maintain circumcision and other Ceremonies, which Christ had nonetheless abolished; but would it then follow that those who wished to maintain the law of Ceremonies in that way did well? Calvin

<meta>catchword: none</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus argues that John Calvin misuses the story of Jeremiah to justify executing heretics, noting that Jeremiah’s warnings concerned murder and divine judgment rather than state-sanctioned execution for religious dissent. He further contrasts the tolerance of the Apostles regarding Jewish ceremonies with Calvin’s lack of tolerance for differing theological views.</summary>

### Conversation

having no reasons or proof, 
when he cannot say "they 
affirm it," then he says "they 
do not deny it," just as he did a little 
while ago when he spoke of the Apostles 
and Prophets; he said, "they did not 
deny it." And now here, Jeremiah 
did not take his refuge in this—
these are his "fine" evidentiary 
reasons. By doing so, one could 
prove countless errors and heresies, 
and in this way: the 
Apostles did not deny

they did not deny that there 
is a purgatory <note>Dutch: *vaghevyer*</note>; or that one might

that is permitted. If 
Calvin had good evidentiary reasons, 
he would not bring 
such a market of trifles <note>Dutch: *buesel-merckt*, meaning a collection of frivolous or worthless arguments</note> 
to light.

I admit well that Hilary <note>Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a bishop and theologian who was a staunch opponent of Arianism.</note> 
goes somewhat further: for he complains 
that it has come so far that the 
Church strikes fear into 
men with imprisonment and exile; 
and thus compels them to believe, 
where she herself was once 
kept in exile and prison

greatly inclined toward and looks too much

nevertheless sanctified through the

persecuted her; that she drives out

nevertheless propagated by the

of the favor of the world, though she 
nevertheless could not have become

to heart (since a fine 
opportunity now presented itself to him) 
than he was occupied 
with deliberating how 
the matters of faith might henceforth

### Vaticanus

The best thing to be 
praised in Hilary is what 
Calvin disparages: for 
if one looks closely at these words 
of Hilary, they are altogether 
Divine words. Indeed, Calvin himself, 
alleging this Hilary against

Preface which he placed at the

Hilary has already, in his own time, 
considered it a great error and sin 
that many people,

<meta>This page continues the polemical exchange between "Vaticanus" (the pseudonym of the humanist Sebastian Castellio) and the Reformer John Calvin. Castellio is attacking Calvin for his role in the execution of Michael Servetus and his use of state power to enforce religious orthodoxy, contrasting Calvin's later actions with his earlier writings and the examples of biblical figures.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

Calvin wrote this when he was busy securing his own position. But now that he has become a Bishop himself, he has turned the leaf <note>Changed his tune or reversed his position.</note> and, having won over the hearts of men through a foolish admiration for Calvinist dignity, he now breeds a destructive evil under that cloak. (1) Indeed, the boldness of Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish physician and theologian executed in Geneva for heresy regarding the Trinity.</note> (who dared to rebuke him) so disturbed Calvin that he gave in more to his present thirst for revenge—since a fine opportunity presented itself regarding the matter of the Trinity—than he seriously considered the means by which the cause of faith should always be protected, as Hilary <note>Hilary of Poitiers, an early Church Father who argued against using state force in religious matters.</note> once rightly prescribed.

### Calvin. 39.

That people cry out that Christ's power is diminished when one wants to defend matters of faith with worldly help—(1) that must not be taken to mean that the authority of the believers hinders the certainty of the Gospel, or that religion would be held in less regard when supported by Kings. No: but one only complains about those who, (2) for lack of God’s word, (3) maintain blatant errors with an armed hand <note>By force or military power.</note>.

### Vaticanus.

Nevertheless, when Ezra came out of the Babylonian captivity, he desired no help from King Cyrus against his enemies, saying: "The hand of our God is for good for all those who seek Him." <note>Ezra 8:22 (Vaticanus attributes the protection to God's hand rather than the King's escort).</note> And Christ says in Isa-

<column-break/>

iah 63:3 that He treads the winepress alone, and that no one from the people is with Him. (2) They <note>The early Christians and prophets.</note> had God’s word just as well as Calvin has it, for they were just as much Christians as he is. (3) For if you had the truth, you would be given a mouth and wisdom that no one could resist; but now that you mistrust the weakness of your word, you seek to help yourself with an armed hand.

### Calvin. 40.

Thus today one may well describe the state of the Papal church as being maintained by nothing other than pure violence and murder. Casting the office of shepherds aside, they force the people with (1) tyrannical edicts <note>Dutch: *placaten*, official decrees or proclamations used as law.</note>; and this is done mostly by those who bear the title of Prelates. (2) Whereas nevertheless the true and upright Church of Christ, which first sprang out of persecution, also flourished most under the same.

### Vaticanus

Calvin writes this with hands that are still full of Servetus's blood, so much so that if he did not help himself with tyrannical edicts and the title of his prelacy, he would have to clear the field for those who know him. For he has no more knowledge of the true office of shepherds than those he wants to rebuke here. I must here set down an edict which was made by the Magistrate of Geneva, by which Calvin’s *Institutes* <note>John Calvin's seminal work, *Institutes of the Christian Religion*.</note> are supported as if by a strong prop.

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Vaticanus (Castellio) accuses John Calvin of hypocrisy, arguing that while Calvin condemns the Catholic Church for using force and "tyrannical edicts," he uses those same methods in Geneva to suppress dissent and protect his own teachings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Calvin's use of institutional power in Geneva. # A Dialogue

The reason why this <term>Edict</term> <note>original: "Placaet"—a formal public decree or proclamation issued by an authority.</note> was made is as follows. In Geneva, there was a certain good citizen named Trouillet <note>Jean Trouillet, a Genevan citizen who challenged Calvin’s teachings on predestination.</note>; he attempted to rebuke and contradict certain things in Calvin's *Institutes* <note>The *Institutes of the Christian Religion*, Calvin's most influential work of Protestant theology.</note>. Because of this, he was summoned to court, and after the legal proceedings had lasted for quite some time, the council made an Edict in French, reading as follows:

The Council, having heard the learned ministers of the word of God, Master Guillaume Farel <note>An early leader of the Reformation in Geneva who originally recruited Calvin to the city.</note> and Pierre Viret <note>A prominent Swiss Reformer and close associate of Calvin.</note>, and after them the honorable Master John Calvin and Master John Trouillet, regarding their statements and objections—which were often debated—concerning the *Christian Institutes* of the said Monsieur Calvin; and everything being well considered, the Council, having heard and understood all things, has pronounced and declared the said book of the *Institutes* by the said Monsieur to be well and holily made, and its doctrine to be the holy doctrine of God; that he is held to be a good and true minister of this city, and that from this time forward, no person shall dare to speak against the said book or the said doctrine. Commanding the parties and all others that they must adhere to this. On

<column-break/>

Wednesday, which was the ninth of November, the year fifteen hundred and fifty-two.

### That is:

Since the council has heard the prominent men and highly learned ministers of the Divine word, Master Guillaume Farel and Pierre Viret, and thereafter also the venerable Master John Calvin, along with Master John Trouillet in all that he has frequently said and raised against the *Christian Institutes* of the same Master Calvin: Therefore it is that the Council, having maturely weighed everything and having heard and understood everything well, pronounces and declares by this that the book of the *Institutes* by the aforementioned Master Calvin is well and holily made, and that his teaching is a holy teaching of God, and that the Author is held to be a good and faithful servant of this city. It is therefore forbidden that henceforth anyone should presume to contradict this book and its Teaching. This is thus commanded to both parties and to every individual to obey. Given on Wednesday, the ninth of November, 1552.

Now reader, take note of this Edict! You will soon see with what weapons this man's doctrine is upheld, and how poorly it suits him that he thunders so against the Papists <note>A term used by reformers for Roman Catholics, implying they follow the Pope rather than the Bible.</note> for the very things he does himself. (2) Just as the Church at Geneva sprang from persecution, so it also flourished while Farel was its leader;

<summary>This page documents a 1552 decree from the Geneva Council that officially sanctioned Calvin's "Institutes" as "holy doctrine" and forbade any public criticism of the book. The author uses this historical document to argue that Calvin employed the same authoritarian tactics he condemned in the Catholic Church.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between "Vaticanus" (a critic of religious persecution) and "Calvin" (representing the views of John Calvin). The previous page established the setting in Geneva and the legal protections Calvin sought for his doctrine. This page shifts to a debate over the use of state power—"the sword"—to enforce religious orthodoxy, specifically referencing the execution of Michael Servetus and the selective use of Church Fathers like Saint Augustine.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

In former times, there was a greater love for Geneva; but since she began to support herself with Calvinist weapons—that is, with the sword—she has perished, and her silver has turned into dross <note>original: "schuym"—literally "foam" or "scum," referring to the waste material produced when refining metals like silver.</note>.

## Calvin. 41.

Because others have persecuted the good with the sword, it does not follow that godly <term>Magistrates</term> <note>Civil officials or city governors responsible for maintaining order and law.</note> should not be permitted to protect the churches with their power, which were previously unjustly persecuted. Nor does the cross and the suffering of the martyrs prevent the faithful from being supported by the beneficial help of Edicts and laws, so they may serve God peacefully.

## Vaticanus.

Had Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish theologian executed in Geneva for heresy, specifically for his rejection of the Trinity and infant baptism.</note> fought you with weapons, then the Magistrate would have done well to protect you; but since he approached you with his writings, why did you meet him with fire and sword? Do you call this the protection of a godly Magistrate? Is there no other piety in you than to put strangers who come traveling there into the fire? Are you not yet ashamed that you still dare to rage and storm against the <term>Papists</term> <note>A derogatory term used by Reformers to refer to Roman Catholics.</note> in this way? Show me once where the Papists have ever captured any Lutheran or Calvinist during the Mass, as you captured Servetus during the sermon in the church.

## Calvin 42.

Most wisely says Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian whose writings were frequently cited by both Catholics and Protestants to justify their positions.</note>: (1) if the Emperors were stuck in any error (from which may God protect them), they would establish laws to maintain their errors, directly against the Truth, through which the godly would be tested and crowned when they did not obey them, because God has forbidden such things. Just as Nebuchadnezzar <note>The Babylonian king mentioned in the Book of Daniel who commanded his subjects to worship a golden statue.</note> had commanded that the golden image should be worshipped; yet those who would not do so pleased God thereby. But if the Emperors held the truth and established laws against error, whoever would not obey them would bring judgment upon their own head. For he is punished by men and has no part with God who refuses to do what the truth commands through the heart of the King. Just as the same Nebuchadnezzar, afterwards changed and converted by the miracle, issued a command for the truth against error.

<column-break/>

## Vaticanus.

Very cautiously, indeed craftily, Calvin conceals all the other passages from Augustine where he speaks quite strongly against persecutions and condemns this whole Calvinist system <note>original: "Calvinisterie"—a derogatory term for the administration and religious climate of Calvin's Geneva.</note>, such as: *Against Cresconius*, Book 3. Also, *On Faith and Works*, Chapter 5. And *Letter 158 and 159 to Marcellus*, and *150 to Appius*, and in the *Book of Questions on the Gospel according to Matthew*, Chapter 13. And *Against the Letter of Mani* <note>original: "contra Epistolam Manichæi"—Augustine's polemic against the Manichaeans, a group he once belonged to.</note>, and in many other places besides. But Calvin throws all these places to the wind. Indeed, in this, he treats Augustine as if he were an <term>Epicurean</term> <note>Followers of Epicurus, often accused by theologians of being materialists or atheists who cared only for pleasure.</note> or a <term>Lucianist</term> <note>A follower of Lucian of Samosata, a 2nd-century satirist known for mocking all religions and philosophies; used here to mean a mocker or a skeptic.</note>, or an ignorant fool.

This is the way people act: they value one thing from various authors as gold, and reject the other as filth, according as it [suits] them...

<meta>catchword: suits (haer)</meta>

<summary>This page presents a heated debate regarding religious tolerance, where the critic "Vaticanus" accuses Calvin of hypocrisy for using state execution against Michael Servetus while simultaneously complaining about Catholic persecution. Calvin justifies his actions by citing Saint Augustine's view that "godly" rulers have a duty to enforce the truth, a point the critic disputes by accusing Calvin of cherry-picking Augustine's writings.</summary>

# Dialogue

...comes into their heads. But they mostly despise that which most accords with Christian kindness / in order to make it known / that they are strangers to all mercy. But let us look at **Augustine's** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose writings were frequently cited by both Catholics and Protestants to justify their positions.</note> words: for if one examines them well, they are flatly against **Calvin**. (1)
<margin>
Luke 18:8
2 Tim. 3:1
Rev. 17:14.
</margin>
For in the last times many shall be stuck in error / as Christ himself indicates / when he says / that he shall find no faith on the earth when he comes. And **Paul** says that the last times shall be evil. And **John** says in his revelation that the kings shall worship the Beast / and that the Lamb shall be fought against. There are many more such places in the **Holy Scripture**: from which it would much sooner follow that the **Emperors** and **Magistrates** in the last / that is in these our times / shall establish laws for their errors: and since **Calvin** is now so busy inciting them to persecution / he does nothing other than arm them against the truth / seeing as they all together believe they possess the truth.

->**Calvin. 43.**<-

(1) Those who follow the pure word of God / by them is religion well and properly maintained. (1) But those who wish to forge a service to God out of their own brain-pan <note>original: "hersenbecken"—a vivid term for the mind or skull, suggesting man-made inventions rather than divine revelation.</note> or by chance accept something which has been lightly invented by someone else / the more they are busy in it / the more they sin / since **superstition** <note>In this context, "superstition" refers to religious practices or beliefs deemed false or human-made by the Reformers.</note> deserves no praise.

->**Vaticanus.**<-

(1) That is what all the sects do. **Cal-**

<meta>This page continues a dialogue or "discourse" between an anonymous critic (Vaticanus) and the reformer John Calvin, focusing on the legitimacy of religious persecution and the execution of Michael Servetus.</meta>

Vaticanus and Calvin.

# Vaticanus.

Why then do you still have [support] from her? You teach people to offer comfort, or will you say that you learned that from Christ? But prove where you learned it.

# Calvin 46.

If false zeal proceeds from ignorance, why should one not praise the zeal that (1) does not lead the children of God astray too quickly, but rather ignites them to (2) defend their **faith**, of which they are certainly convinced?

# Vaticanus.

(1) If you were God’s children, you would do God’s works; but now you kill people whom you have no right to kill—the Son of God never did that. (2) To defend one's faith is not to burn people, but to be burned oneself. Whoever remains steadfast to the <margin>Matthew 10:22</margin> end, says Christ, shall be saved; oh, not by persecuting—no, but by suffering. That is the true defense of the faith, but this is unknown to Calvin.

# Calvin 47.

(1) Finally, although there may be some similarity in the punishment inflicted upon martyrs and false <term>blasphemers</term><note>Those accused of speaking irrelevantly or insultingly about God or sacred things.</note>, it is nevertheless the guilt that makes a great distinction between the two. (2) So also it is that true knowledge makes for godly and upright zealots, whereas levity and blind impulse make for unjust and evil zealots.

# Vaticanus.

Then there is also a great distinction between Gapmonet <note>Likely a reference to Jacques Gruet, a critic of Calvin who was beheaded in Geneva in 1547.</note> who in

<column-break/>

Geneva had his head chopped off for his terrible blasphemies, which he had spat out against God flatly against his conscience; and between Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), the Spanish physician and theologian burned at the stake in Geneva for his views on the Trinity.</note>, who had written of God to the best of his ability, and if he had erred in anything, did so out of ignorance—just as Farel <note>William Farel, the reformer who accompanied Servetus to his execution.</note> himself testified before the people when Servetus begged him for it just before his death. Calvin himself also indicates this clearly enough, for in the title of his book he calls the matters concerning Servetus "errors." Gapmonet never said that he had erred, nor did he ever wish to maintain his blasphemies, from which it is clearly evident that there was a great difference between him and Servetus. (2) Yet Christ instructs the godly zealots, <margin>Luke 9:55–56</margin> saying: "You do not know of what spirit you are. For the Son of Man did not come to destroy, but to save." But the wolf comes only to devour.

# Calvin 48.

When someone is physically punished who considers everything written in the Holy Scriptures to be mere fables: who would still dare call such a monster a martyr? Since, as Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo, the influential early Christian theologian.</note> says, it is not the punishment, but the cause, that makes the martyr <note>original: "niet de straffe / maer de oorsake den martelaer maeckt," translating the Latin legal/theological maxim "Martyrem non facit poena, sed causa."</note>.

# Vaticanus.

This man [Calvin] would gladly seek to make hateful and bring into contempt all the people whom he considers <term>heretics</term><note>A "heretic" is a person whose religious beliefs conflict with established church dogma.</note>, though nevertheless no one holds the Holy Scriptures in higher regard than those people who are commonly [called] heretics...

Vaticanus and Calvin.

...were [known], not understanding: just as they also clearly made known in the letters they wrote to the other churches, in which they say they did not doubt the faithfulness of their ministers.

### Calvin 54.

Therefore it is a terrible barbarism that prevails in the Papacy, and truly it shall not escape the dreadful judgment of God. The martyrs are prepared to give an account of everything, if only one would hear them; but it is so little (1) permitted to make any defense from God's word, (2) that the very beginning of the trial is the refusal to hear any defense.

### Vaticanus.

(1) It is certainly permitted among the Papists to defend oneself, but on the same footing as it was permitted to Servetus. Namely, on the condition that he must die if he did not immediately admit everything <note>The Dutch phrase "door den hals halen" suggests a forced or total admission under pressure.</note>. (2) The beginning of your trial was imprisonment, and refusing to hear anyone who was a friend of Servetus, or at least not an enemy: this is evident in the case of Gribaldus <note>Matteo Gribaldi was a legal scholar who visited Geneva during Servetus's trial and was rebuffed by Calvin for his sympathetic views.</note>, whose request you so arrogantly rejected.

### Calvin 55.

Thereby taking away all defense, they go about inflicting (1) unheard (2) a thousand pains on the good people, and finally, having cut out the tongue, they throw them into such a fire in which they do not immediately suffocate, but in which they must die slowly, so that they might (as a certain tyrant said) feel that they were

<column-break/>

dying. Which truly are examples of great beastly cruelty.

### Vaticanus.

(1) They are certainly heard, but what does it matter whether they are heard or not when they are in the hands of unjust judges, from whom they have nothing to expect but death, whether they speak the truth or not: just as was done with Servetus, who was heard by no one other than his opponents—that is, by Calvin and the Swiss, who ought not to have been judges, but accusers, since Servetus had written against them all together. (2) Thus those of Geneva also wanted to deal with Servetus: for after he had (as I have been told as the truth) voluntarily and of his own accord confessed concerning his book and all other things, they still wanted to put him on the rack <note>The "pijnbanc" or rack was a torture device used to extract information or confessions.</note>, and they would have severely tortured him, had it not been for the intervention of one from the Council, named Petrus Vandalus <note>Pierre Vandel was a prominent Genevan politician and a leader of the "Libertine" faction who opposed Calvin's influence.</note>, a great enemy of Calvin; so that you can easily perceive from this that it is not Calvin's friends who have any mercy in their hearts. (3) Calvin is even <unclear>satisfied</unclear> that he did not cut out Servetus's tongue. But he brought him to his death, and burned his books, so that Servetus, at least after his death, could not [defend] himself before the whole world...

# Dialogue

...could answer. Yet Calvin believes that everyone ought to accept the sentence he passed over Servetus as good and valid, without anyone being allowed to revise or reconsider it, as if it should be enough that our "Master John" saw fit to do so. Why did he burn the books? He was concerned, I think, that people would be infected by them. Well then, why did he not have that same concern regarding the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim (1548) was an imperial decree ordering Protestants to readopt many Catholic practices; Calvin published a famous critique of it</note>? Which booklet he himself allowed to be printed in Geneva, along with his accompanying rebuttal? How is it that he did not act the same way with Servetus? I suspect it was for no other reason than that Servetus depicted Calvin a bit more vividly than the *Interim* did. Perhaps he will say that he could not well have kept the *Interim* hidden. But why could he not at least ban such "primers" from his city? How is it that in Geneva he does not forbid the printing or selling of other books that are much more harmful? But no—for there one may have Aristotle, who denies the first article of faith (namely, the creation of the world); one may have the Quran, Apuleius, Martial, Plautus, Terence, Horace, Catullus, Tibullus, Propertius, and similar wicked corrupters of good morals. Likewise Ovid's *The Art of Love*—which is to say, the art of adultery—and his clever follower Clement Marot <note>A French poet whose secular works were often criticized by the more austere reformers</note>. It happened some years ago that a man from France 

<column-break/>

had come to live in Geneva; he wrote to one of his good friends thus: "I have departed from Babylon; send me all of Ovid's works with commentaries." They call the Papacy "Babylon," and every other place is a "Jerusalem" to them. But what shall one say of all the nonsense printed there? Such as Beza's *Zoographia* <note>A satirical and controversial work by Theodore Beza, Calvin’s successor</note>, likewise his *Pas-avant*, and the epitaph on the death of Viret’s wife <note>Pierre Viret was a reformer and close friend of Calvin; the author mocks these writings as trivial or vulgar</note>, which booklets are stuffed with trifles and rascality. Yet these rascalities are considered "healthy" by them, for they sit upon the seat of scoffers. While they carry such books with them every day, they burn Servetus’s books. Why indeed? So that their "smothering pot" <note>A metaphor for hidden corruption or secrets</note> should not be uncovered, and so they might do and say everything according to their own lust and pleasure without punishment. They fear the eyes of men, but the eyes of God—who sees through all of this—they do not respect. (3) He describes the death of Servetus and goes on to reproach others for their tyranny, believing that such things are only permitted to him and his own.

# Conversation

<page-num>66</page-num>

[what kind of] children you are: the Son of Man is not come to destroy / but to save <note>A reference to Luke 9:55-56, where Jesus rebukes his disciples for wanting to call down fire on a Samaritan village</note>: but of moderating such zeal / Calvin does not even know how to speak.

->Calvin 61.<-

So we must then without doubt be very cautious and also gentle: indeed, one must have a calm religious knowledge of the doctrine / before one judges.

->Vaticanus.<-

Just as such [calmness] was maintained in the case of Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish theologian executed in Geneva for heresy regarding the Trinity</note> / who as soon as he had been seen / on a Sunday during the Sermon / was brought into the prison / from which he never emerged until he was led to the fire. ->Calvin 62.<-

But the Magistrate must not therefore fail to compel with the sword and other punishments / such people / who being apostates themselves / seek to pull others away from the upright faith / and being blasphemers of God with their false tricks bring poor souls into the net / yes, those who overthrow the peace of the churches / (1) and tear and destroy the good harmony / in matters of godliness.

blasphemers / apostates and Heretics were all one little group of people. And so that no one would see the distinction / and Calvin’s deception would not be noticed / it is the case that he nowhere even describes what a Heretic is <note>original: "sp." likely an abbreviation for "is" or "signifies"</note>. So that he mocks us enough in such a serious matter. Finally, see what he says of the Apostates / that is / those who fall away. For the Holy Scripture calls them Apostates / who fall away to another God: but 
<margin>What an Apostate [is].</margin> 
Heretics are those who, remaining in the service 
<margin>What a Heretic is.</margin> 
of the same God / nevertheless adhere to some false sects or opinions. Calvin seeks to mix these two together / and that entirely out of place. For if he must be called an Apostate who seeks to pull others away from the upright faith / having first fallen away from it himself (otherwise he may not be called an Apostate / just as one may not call a Jew an Apostate / since he never believed in Christ) / then Servetus is no Apostate / for he never fell away from the Lutherans / or Calvinists / since he was always against them: or if he was an Apostate / as it seems Calvin wants to say (for on this he began this dispute) / then the Pope’s faith / which Servetus fell away from / must be upright and good: and thus one should / according to his own judgment / burn Calvin himself too / since he also fell away from the Papacy. (1) He considers the Calvinist sect to be the church / and all the rest he dismisses outside of it.

<meta>This page continues the debate between John Calvin and Sebastian Castellio (writing under the pseudonym "Vaticanus") regarding the use of state force to punish religious dissent. Following the discussion on page 66 about the treatment of Michael Servetus, this page shifts to the fundamental argument regarding the "two kingdoms"—the spiritual kingdom of God and the earthly kingdom of the state.</meta>

# Vatican and Calvin

### Calvin 63.

The reasons which some people, out of ignorance—though they are not the most wicked of men—propose to prove that one ought not to punish such sects, we shall now briefly examine. The Lord, they say, did not wish His kingdom to be built up by hands or weapons, but by the spiritual sword of the Gospel; and He commanded His own to be ready to shed their own blood, but nowhere is it commanded that they should shed the blood of others. He sent His Apostles like sheep into the midst of wolves, without giving them any fleshly power. But to this I will also say: the Lord has nowhere commanded His own to punish theft, robbery, adultery, murder, and sorcery. What do you think—should those crimes therefore also remain unpunished? And should they be allowed to run unbridled, or should they not rather say that the tolerance <note>The Dutch *verdraechtsaemheyt* refers to Christian forbearance or patient endurance of others</note> required of everyone does not prevent the enforcement of the laws from prevailing?

### Vaticanus.

When theft, robbery, adultery, murder, etc., are punished, they are not punished for the purpose of building up Christ’s kingdom thereby, nor to make man righteous or godly, nor to establish the new creature <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 5:17, meaning a person's spiritual transformation</note>, but to protect the bodies and property of good men. 

<column-break/>

Calvin allows the Papists <note>A sixteenth-century Protestant term for Roman Catholics</note>, Jews, Turks <note>In this period, "Turks" was used as a general term for all Muslims</note>, and all others whom he believes to be outside the kingdom of Christ, to punish such crimes. But that they should kill anyone for matters of faith—that he will not allow, as he has said above that such a thing is permitted to no one except the godly and good Magistrate <note>The civil government or authorities</note>. Therefore, one ought not to make a jumble <note>original: "mengelmoes" (mishmash)</note> of things here, but put everything in its proper place and distinguish the heavenly kingdom from the earthly one. But Christ has sufficiently made known and revealed everything that belongs to His kingdom; therefore, we must also draw all of that together from Him. But we shall speak of this more broadly hereafter.

### Calvin 64.

I well acknowledge that Christ’s kingdom was not built up with an armed hand in the beginning, and that it is also not maintained now by the force of weapons; for Christ must reign through the preaching of the Gospel, so that this prophecy might be fulfilled: 
> The people whom I did not know have become subject to me; through the hearing of the ears, they have been obedient to me. <note>A translation of Psalm 18:43-44</note>
It is for this reason that the Lord sent out His Apostles naked, without weapons, with only the power of His word, in order to be all the more glorious; and He not only willed that they should lack all earthly power, but even that almost the whole world should be against them, so that everyone might be able to

<sig>E ii</sig>
<meta>catchword: notice [bemercken]</meta>

<summary>This page features a debate on whether the state should punish religious "sects." Calvin argues that just as civil crimes like murder are punished despite no specific New Testament command, so should religious offenses, while Vaticanus argues for a strict separation between civil protection and spiritual matters.</summary>
<keywords>separation of church and state, religious persecution, two kingdoms, John Calvin, Sebastian Castellio, civil law, Gospel, spiritual sword</keywords>

## Calvin 61.

## Vaticanus.

## Calvin 62.

But the Magistrate <note>The civil government or city officials responsible for law and order</note> must not, for that reason, neglect to compel with the sword and other punishments those people who, being themselves apostates, still seek to pull others away from the true faith, and being blasphemers of God, bring poor souls into their net with their false tricks; yes, those who overthrow the peace of the churches, (1) and tear apart and destroy the good harmony in matters of godliness.

## Vaticanus.

Calvin had said that he would deal with the punishment of Heretics: and afterwards he spoke much of the wandering godless, and blasphemers; just as if wanderers, godless,

<column-break/>

blasphemers, apostates, and Heretics were all one group of people. And so that no one would see the gap, and Calvin's deceit would not be noticed, he nowhere even describes what a Heretic is. So he mocks us quite enough in such a serious matter. Finally, consider what he says of the Apostates, that is, the fallen.

<margin>What an Apostate is.</margin>
For the Holy Scripture calls them Apostates who fall away to another God: 
<margin>What a Heretic is.</margin>
but Heretics are those who, while remaining in the service of the same God, nonetheless adhere to certain false sects or opinions. Calvin seeks to mix these two together, and that is entirely beside the point. For if he must be called an Apostate who seeks to pull others away from the true faith, having first fallen away from it himself (otherwise he may not be called an Apostate, just as one may not call a Jew an Apostate, since he never believed in Christ), then Servetus is no Apostate; for he never fell away from the Lutherans or Calvinists, since he was always against them. Or, if he was an Apostate as it seems Calvin wants to say (for he began this dispute on this point), then the Pope's faith, which Servetus fell away from, must be upright and good: and thus one would have to burn Calvin himself according to his own judgment, since he also fell away from the Papacy. (1) He considers the Calvinist sect to be the church, and all the rest he dismisses as being outside of it.

<summary>This page presents a sharp debate over the execution of Michael Servetus, with "Vaticanus" (Castellio) accusing Calvin of using inconsistent definitions of "heretic" and "apostate" to justify state violence. Castellio points out the irony that if falling away from one's original church (apostasy) merits death, Calvin himself would be subject to the same punishment for leaving the Catholic Church.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (the humanist Sebastian Castellio) regarding the use of state force to punish religious dissent. On the previous page, Vaticanus criticized Calvin for conflating different types of "sinners" (heretics vs. blasphemers) to justify the execution of Michael Servetus.</meta>

->Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

### Calvin 63.

We shall now briefly examine the reasons that some people—out of ignorance, although they are not the most wicked of men—put forward to prove that one ought not to punish such sects <note>In this context, "sects" refers to dissenting religious groups that diverged from the established Reformed or Catholic doctrines</note>. The Lord, they say, did not wish for his kingdom to be built by hands or weapons, but by the spiritual sword of the Gospel: and he commanded his own that they should be ready to shed their own blood, but nowhere is it commanded of them that they should shed the blood of others. He sent his Apostles as sheep in the midst of wolves, without giving them any carnal <note>Physical or worldly power</note> violence. Yet to this I will also say: the Lord nowhere commanded his own to punish theft, robbery, adultery, murder, and witchcraft; what do you think, should these then also remain unpunished? And should one let them run so unbridled? Or should they not rather say that the tolerance required of everyone does not prevent the enforcement of the laws from having the upper hand?

### Vaticanus.

When theft, robbery, adultery, murder, etc., are punished, they are not punished for the purpose of building up the kingdom of Christ thereby, nor to make the person righteous or God-fearing, nor to establish the "new creature" <note>A biblical concept from 2 Corinthians 5:17 regarding spiritual rebirth</note>, but to protect the bodies and goods of good people.

<column-break/>

Calvin allows the Papists <note>A term used by reformers for Roman Catholics</note>, Jews, Turks <note>A general term used at the time for Muslims</note>, and all others whom he believes to be outside the kingdom of Christ, to punish such crimes: but that they should kill anyone for matters of faith, that he will not allow; just as he has said above, that such a thing is permitted to no one except the God-fearing and good Magistrate <note>The civil government or city officials</note>. Therefore, one ought not to make a "mishmash" here, but set everything in its proper place, and distinguish the heavenly kingdom from the earthly one. But Christ has sufficiently made known and revealed everything that belongs to his kingdom: therefore we must also take all of that together from him. However, we shall speak of this more broadly hereafter.

### Calvin 64.

I confess indeed that the kingdom of Christ was not built with an armed hand in the beginning, and that it is also now not kept standing by the force of weapons: for Christ must reign through the preaching of the Gospel, so that this prophecy might be fulfilled: *The people whom I did not know have become subject to me; through the hearing of the ears, they have been obedient to me.* <note>Original: "Het volck welck ick niet en kende is my onderdanich gheworden, door het gehoor der ooren , is het my gehoorsaem gheweest" — a reference to Psalm 18:43-44</note> It is for this reason that the Lord sent out his Apostles naked, without weapons, only with the power of his word, in order to be all the more glorious; and he did not only wish that they should lack all earthly power, but even that almost the whole world should be against them, so that everyone might be able to

The text highlights the tension between the "spiritual sword" of the church and the "carnal sword" of the state.</summary>

<margin>(1) It is so, if you understand this regarding the true Religion: But your Religion is held up by human hands. Just put the violence and the persecution of your Magistrate on one side, and grant freedom of writing and speaking, just as Paul did (saying: you may all prophesy), and you shall soon learn what the free truth is capable of. If you think otherwise, try it once, and the outcome shall give the Verdict: indeed, it seems that you also know this well, since you are so busy forbidding others to speak and write, which you certainly would not do, unless you relied upon human hands.</margin>

notice that the Victory of the Gospel is heavenly / when it has sprouted and grown up like a Palm tree / against so many powerful obstacles. Yet also that it has risen up again against the will / and the violence of the world / and still flourishes. From which it then appears that Religion is maintained by its own strength / and not by the hands of men. (1) <note>Vaticanus argues that if Calvin’s doctrine were truly "maintained by its own strength," he wouldn't need the city government (the Magistrate) to ban books or execute dissenters.</note>

And it is not in vain / that Christ arms the ministers of the Gospel / more with patience / than with power to wage war: for it becomes them to be so named / so that they should confirm the Doctrine which they present with the tongue / with their blood / if it were necessary.

### Vaticanus.

All this is true / but oh, if only the man would stick to this. (1)

### Calvin 65.

First, one sees: that generally the Princes of this world oppose the true and sound Doctrine.

### Vaticanus.

Therefore, if they were to go to work according to Calvin’s law / then they would have to put to death all those who hold the true and sound Doctrine. <note>Vaticanus points out the irony: if princes have the right to kill "heretics," then a Catholic prince would be "right" to kill Calvinists, as they would be the heretics in his eyes.</note>

### Calvin 66.

Secondly, it is so: that the Doctrine, through its severity / embitters many / whom it previously pleased well.

### Vaticanus.

Then Calvin’s Doctrine is not sound, for I / and many with me / have daily found that there is no severity in it / indeed, that none is more delightful and pleasant. So that those who desert the Papacy <note>Roman Catholicism</note> for Calvin / immediately notice / that they are enlightened / and not burdened: indeed, they can much more easily bear the Doctrine of the Genevan / than of the Roman Pope. <note>Vaticanus sarcastically refers to Calvin as the "Genevan Pope," implying he has merely replaced one religious autocrat with another.</note>

### Calvin 67.

Few are those who want to bow their necks under the Yoke / and just as they tickle themselves / (1) so it is also that they seek soft Teachers / who talk to please them like their servants / and smear honey around their mouths. <note>This is a Dutch idiom (Heunich om den mondt smeeren) meaning to flatter someone or tell them only what they want to hear.</note>

### Vaticanus.

<meta>This page continues the debate between the Reformer John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (the pseudonym of Sebastian Castellio). They are discussing the danger of involving civil authorities in religious judgment. Castellio argues that if a government is given the power to execute "heretics" today, a different government could use that same power to execute the "godly" tomorrow.</meta>

->Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

...the greatest crowd will be outvoted.
(1.) How gentle those Teachers are, those who have experienced both the gentle and the harsh know well. <note>Vaticanus is likely referring to the experience of religious refugees who found the Protestant authorities in Geneva to be just as strict as the Catholic ones they fled.</note>

### Calvin. 68.

Now we see how seldom the Princes who succeed one another are of the same nature and condition. For it often happens that the son, with sheer violence, destroys the good state of the church which his Father had established.

### Vaticanus.

Suppose the state of Geneva is currently good and upright; it could easily happen that tomorrow it would be changed and overturned, so that it might easily result from this: if a godless <term>heretic</term> <note>In this context, a "heretic" is someone holding beliefs contrary to established church doctrine.</note> has been burned today, tomorrow the godly might be treated in the same way.

### Calvin 69.

It befits the ministers of the Gospel not to turn with every wind, but even if all men changed, and however the times might turn, they must nevertheless keep to the right course: so that the eternal and unchangeable Truth of God, which is entrusted to them, does not fall underfoot through their unfaithfulness and sloth.

### Vaticanus.

Had Calvin done so, he would not have changed his opinion on persecution, and other things as well, which I do not wish to recount here.

<column-break/>

Had he changed his opinion for a better one, I would praise him; but now he must bear shame, for he has changed his good opinion into a worse one. <note>Castellio often pointed out that Calvin’s earlier writings (like the first edition of the *Institutes*) were more tolerant of dissent than his later actions in Geneva.</note>

### Calvin 70.

Thus we also see that the ancient Prophets, although they sometimes used the help of good Kings, nevertheless were often miserably persecuted: so that one may rightly say that they all fought together under the banner of the cross of Christ. And truly, it is nowadays a common plague: that no one who wishes to serve Christ faithfully can also please men.

### Vaticanus.

And according to Calvin's saying, men shall be judges in matters of Religion: and in so doing, they will always condemn those who do not please them. But they will not condemn Calvin: for he desires that the <term>Magistrate</term> <note>The civil government or city officials.</note> shall judge concerning heretics. Now, if they judge by votes, and since the greatest crowd overcomes the best (for there are few good people, as Calvin said above), then the Calvinists will stand in the favor of the majority—that is, of the worst crowd—so that he may thereby destroy the <term>Servetists</term> <note>original: "Servetisten"; followers of Michael Servetus, who was executed for heresy in Geneva in 1553.</note>; for otherwise, he would not be able to achieve such a thing.

### Calvin 71.

They object to this, saying it is absurd that the spiritual kingdom of Christ should rely on the power of the flesh,

<meta>This page continues the debate between John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio). Calvin is arguing that while faith is not built on human skill, the "lawful aids" of the state and human arts can support the church. Vaticanus is countering that Calvin’s comparison of "eloquence in preaching" to "the sword in religion" is a false and dangerous analogy.</meta>

->Dialogue<-

...just as it would be absurd if faith were founded on human eloquence; but suppose there were someone who wanted to assert that all those who are eloquent, well-learned, and experienced in the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>The 'liberal arts' (vrye konsten) in the Renaissance included grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy—the essential education for a scholar.</note> ought to remain silent and stay away from the pulpit, so as not to make the faith void through their eloquence: would such a man not openly wrong God and His gifts? It is true that Paul says that he who thinks himself wise should become a fool, so that they might become disciples of Christ; <note>Calvin is referencing 1 Corinthians 3:18: "If any man among you seemeth to be wise in this world, let him become a fool, that he may be wise."</note> namely, so that the wisdom of the flesh, being used properly, might make faith alone shine forth. If then there is no reason in the way why the preaching of the Gospel should not be powerful through the power of the Spirit alone, even when it might be accompanied by some help of human arts: so it is also not absurd that Religion—although it is held upright by God’s hand alone and triumphs in the midst of the cross and persecutions—should nevertheless be helped by some <term>lawful aids</term> <note>By 'lawful aids,' Calvin refers to the use of civil laws and the power of the state (the 'sword') to protect the church and punish heresy.</note> when it might please God.

### Vaticanus.

This comparison is absurd and deceitful, yes, brought forward entirely impertinently: this is Calvin’s manner; when he wants to deceive readers or listeners, he speaks obscurely in order to hide his absurdities beneath it, and thereby to slip away like an eel in the mud. Yet we shall, in explaining this comparison, uncover the

<column-break/>

<note>original: "smoopot", literally a 'smothering pot' or 'muffler', used here to mean a deceptive cover or smoke-screen.</note> smoke-screen. He wishes to say: just as one can well use eloquence in proclaiming the Gospel, although it is not necessarily required for it, one may also just as well use the Sword to defend Religion, although the same is not necessarily required for it. But it is absurd that he fits the Sword to Religion in the same way eloquence is joined to teaching. For the Sword is not used for Religion in the same way speaking is used for teaching: for without speaking one cannot teach anyone well, but Religion is well maintained without the Sword; yes, the Sword is no more useful for it than money, or a farmer’s plow. Otherwise, if one wanted to imitate Calvin, one could well argue thus: just as the <term>physician</term> <note>original: "Medecijnmeester", a master of medicine.</note> can lead the sick through eloquence to take their medicine, just so he could well protect their health with the sword. Does that follow logically? But the sword so haunts the man’s mind and grinds away at him that he seeks to apply it everywhere. But now we shall bring forward a comparison that will fit better, thus: just as one needs a sword for fighting, and it is equally useful whether it is rusty or shiny, so long as it is sharp; so also, for teaching the Gospel, speaking is useful, although it matters little whether it is crude and blunt or neat and adorned, so long as it has the sharpness of the Spirit.

<summary>Calvin argues that the state's 'sword' can be a lawful aid to religion just as eloquence aids preaching, while Vaticanus mocks this as a false analogy, stating that religion requires the Spirit, not physical force.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between "Vaticanus" (the humanist scholar Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin.
