Here, the argument shifts to the qualifications of ministers: whether they should be simple and "unlearned" like the apostles, or highly educated in Latin and the liberal arts as Calvin required.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

Just as in fighting one goes at another not with words, but with swords, so must one also in religious matters go against one another with words, and not with swords.

->Calvin 72.<-

God has called the foolish of this world to shame the wise, but He who began with crude <term>fishermen</term> <note>A reference to the Twelve Apostles, many of whom were simple fishermen, used here to represent uneducated but divinely inspired leaders.</note> also chose other (1) ministers afterward who were not so crude, but were somewhat more learned.

->Vaticanus.<-

Calvin has rejected the foolish of this world to exalt the wise; and for this reason, no one among them may teach or preach unless they are experienced in science and languages, and especially in Latin. So much so that if Christ himself came now and spoke no Latin, they would not receive him. Some time ago it happened that there was a preacher in <term>Lausanne</term> <note>A city in Switzerland that was under the influence of the Genevan Reformation.</note>; this man was a pious and blameless man, yet he knew no Latin. Therefore they forced the man, who had already reached his later years, to go and learn Latin from <term>Corderius</term> <note>Mathurin Cordier, a famous schoolmaster and Latin grammarian who taught children in Geneva and Lausanne.</note>, who taught the young boys. The good man, unable to bear such an insult, departed from there. So greatly are those people obsessed with the Latin language. If one were to compare them to Christ, one would find that they are entirely unlike <margin>Matthew 11:25.</margin> him. For Christ thanks his Father that he has hidden the secret things from the wise and understanding, and revealed them to the small and unlearned; these [Calvinists] thank him that he has hidden those things from the small and un-

<column-break/>

learned and revealed them to the wise. Christ’s church boasts that she does not have many wise and noble; these always boast of their wise and noble. (1) He speaks of such Teachers who have begun to tear down the Church, which was built up by the Fishermen. For after they began to mix human arts with holy Theology: so has everything become <margin>2 Timothy 3:16.</margin> spoiled; indeed, idle and useless questions (which Paul forbids) have been brought onto the stage, through which the pure truth is so soiled and darkened—indeed (if I may say so) so greatly rooted out—that it cannot be helped or healed, unless one returned again to those same Fishermen: so that this word remains true: "out of the mouth of children and infants you have ordained praise." <note>A quote from Psalm 8:2, also cited by Jesus in the Gospels to defend the simple faith of his followers.</note> We have a clear example in Calvin himself. For <term>Farellus</term> <note>Guillaume Farel, the fiery French reformer who first brought the Reformation to Geneva and recruited Calvin.</note> taught the Gospel in the beginning with a great zeal; he shunned all worldly writers like a plague, and rejected all poets like dung, and was so estranged from greed that, though he suffered much and great wrong for the Gospel, he nevertheless took no one to court over it; indeed, he bravely rebuked those who wanted to avenge their wrongs. Through these "arts" [of the spirit] he did wonderful profit in driving out errors and building up love. But after Calvin arrived there, he mixed worldly sciences with spiritual ones; he devised a thousand cunning inventions to avenge his <term>bile</term> <note>Original: "keet" (likely "gal" or "heetigheit"), referring to Calvin's famously sharp temper and his use of logic to defend his personal authority.</note> under the cloak of Christ.

# Conversation

Indeed, all things have fallen into such a pitiful state that even Farel <note>William Farel (1489–1565), a key figure in the Reformation in Switzerland and a close associate of Calvin.</note> (is it not a lamentable thing?) now also **Calvinizes**, and has now learned many vices from Calvin, whom he himself ought to have taught virtues: Such power has that (as Paul calls it) **falsely named knowledge** <note>original: "valsch ghenaemde wetenschap," a reference to 1 Timothy 6:20.</note> had. Indeed, the more that human and worldly sciences have been credited, the further one has strayed from the Divine and holy. And those who are so busy founding and maintaining colleges and schools for this purpose do nothing else than arm the opponents of the simple, plain truth. Yet they may do their best; God will do what He was accustomed to do in the past, namely, that the great and terrible Goliath be slain by David—who came armed in the name of God—with a sling, and his head be cut off with his own sword.

### Calvin. 73.

Indeed, Paul himself, who admits to having no eloquence, was not unlettered like his fellow Apostles, for from his youth he was instructed (1) in the law.

### Vaticanus.

(1) It is true [he was instructed] in the law, but you, dear friends, exalt worldly sciences; and yet what you say about Paul still argues against you. For he, being puffed up by literal knowledge, persecuted the truth much more bitterly than he otherwise would have done. But after he had accepted the true knowledge, that is, Christ and love,

<column-break/>

he held literal knowledge to be as **dung**, which Calvin here still so greatly praises and exalts.

### Calvin. 74.

Ick <note>I</note> will not bring up here all those who came after him. But look at Isaiah’s writings, which are not only neat and pure, but also artistically (1) adorned, which sufficiently shows that eloquence is often an aid to faith.

### Vaticanus.

(1) That is not true: for the prophetic person uses no art as men do, nor have the holy men of God spoken by human will or art, but by the driving of the **Holy Spirit**. Isaiah did indeed have some eloquence, but it was innate to him without his having learned it as an art; in which there is such a distinction as between the innate beauty of an honorable woman and the **face-painting** <term>blanketten: the use of cosmetics or white lead to artificially enhance beauty</term> of a harlot. Then what should one say? They seek to mix holy things with worldly ones. So also, some time ago, Sebastian Castellio <note>A French preacher and theologian (1515–1563) who famously clashed with Calvin over religious tolerance.</note> did in his preface to the books of Moses, which he had translated, in which he writes profanely of all the arts that Plutarch formerly attributed to Homer: and for this purpose, as if he were erecting a shameful work, he uses a great fabrication of reasons and words, just as if Moses had not written by the driving of the Holy Spirit, without ever looking to human art. Yet the frivolous people seek

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

...to pull all things to their own profit and intent, and to exalt the human arts (to which they are as slaves), they mix these among the Divine Scriptures, so that they, like pestilential flies, might infect the fragrant ointment of the Holy Scripture by their touch.

->Calvin 75.<-

And indeed, Christ sought out the wise (1) through the rays of the star, just as he sought the shepherds through the voice of the Angel.

->Vaticanus.<-

(1) In this way, He would also lead the Calvinists, if they would humble themselves to it. For God does not despise the learned, but rather the proud. But since the learned are commonly infected with pride (for knowledge puffs up), it happens that so few learned people are saved. There will certainly be some among the learned who will humble themselves to the manger of Christ, just as the wise men did, while others—namely, the Scribes and Pharisees—despised Him. But the first and greatest group to whom the Angel shall announce Christ’s birth will be shepherds and unlettered people, although they will be rejected and despised by the others. For if a shepherd or unlearned person came to announce something of the truth to them, I am certain they would be no more moved by it than if a donkey brayed.

->Calvin 76.<-

Therefore, even though Christ first built His church out of that crude and un-

<column-break/>

knowing rabble, this does not prevent (1) kings from offering themselves and all their service to Him, and that the power of the sword which they possess should not be a holy sacrifice.

->Vaticanus.<-

(1) Paul says that Christ, after He had ascended, gave Apostles, Prophets, Evangelists, and teachers, for the joining together of the Saints, for the work of the ministry, for the building up of the body of Christ—that is, the congregation. These are Holy sacrifices that are useful for this holy work. 
<margin>Against the Münster Anabaptists.</margin> <note>The Münster Anabaptists (1534–1535) were a radical sect that seized power in the German city of Münster, attempting to establish a "New Jerusalem" by force of arms; they were eventually crushed with extreme violence.</note>
But the sword is not used for the kingdom of Christ, except to kill Christ and His followers. It is true: those who bear the sword are God’s servants, but only to punish the wicked, and not to build up Christ’s kingdom. Christians share the use of the sword in common with other nations, who also wield the sword well; nevertheless, they are not of service to Christ’s kingdom through it.

->Calvin 77.<-

Now we see that all ministers of the Gospel <term>Gospel</term> <note>original: "Euangeliums." The word literally means "good news" and refers to the core Christian message.</note> ought to be prepared to endure every cross, hatred, and scorn, just as the Lord has armed them with no other weapons than patience; and yet, kings are commanded to protect the (1) doctrine of godliness <term>Godliness</term> <note>original: "Godsalicheyd," referring to a life lived in accordance with God's will.</note> according to their ability.

->Vaticanus.<-

(1) To put a man to death is not to protect the doctrine, but to kill a man. Yet those of Geneva who killed Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553) was a Spanish scholar and physician who was burned at the stake in Geneva for heresy, a decision supported by John Calvin.</note> did not then protect the doctrine, but killed a man. It is the office of the Magistrate <term>Magistrate</term> <note>original: "Magistraet," meaning a civil officer or government official.</note>...

He further argues that civil power and the "sword" should not be used to enforce religious doctrine, specifically referencing the execution of Michael Servetus in Geneva.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Calvin and Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio) concerning the use of state power in religious matters. On the previous page, they discussed whether kings and worldly power have a place in the service of Christ.</meta>

->Dialogue<-

<meta>catchword: of the</meta>

office to protect the doctrine with the [sword] (what communion has the sword with the doctrine?) <note>The speaker argues that spiritual teaching and physical force belong to different realms.</note> but it is a teacher’s office. It is the Magistrate’s office to protect the teachers, just as he protects a farmer or a craftsman, or a physician, and furthermore all others against all violence and injustice. Therefore, had Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish theologian executed in Geneva for heresy.</note> wanted to kill Calvin, then the Magistrate would have properly protected Calvin. But since Servetus had approached Calvin with reasons and writings, then he ought to have been driven off with such weapons as well.

### Calvin 78.

Furthermore, they argue against this that it is entirely absurd to compel people to the faith (which is a voluntary obedience) with force. But I do not wish to bring up here everything that Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD). In his later years, he famously justified the use of force against the Donatist heretics, a position Calvin cites here.</note> truthfully and learnedly disputes concerning this—namely, that the unwilling are very profitably drawn, those who resist the almighty God, so that through punishments, being humbled, they might come of their own accord: so I answer, that to maintain the state of the church, the sword is used for yet another purpose than to compel people to the faith.

### Vaticanus.

<margin>John 6:44</margin> Christ says: no one can come to me unless my Father draws him: <note>original: "niemant kannot my komē ten sy dat mijn vader hem treck"</note> and these people want the Magistrate to draw all such people who resist the drawing God, just as if the Magistrate could accomplish more than God. As if they would then come because they were subjected to punishments!

<column-break/>

I believe that those who would otherwise have come of their own accord would sooner be frightened away by the punishments: like the fish in the water, which come to the bait and the net of their own accord, and flee away when one wants to force them. Of this, Geneva and a good part of Savoy <note>A region neighboring Geneva that was forcibly converted to Protestantism during the Reformation.</note> can bear witness, which have accepted the Gospel <note>original: "Euangelium"</note> through force. For there, very few exist who do not deadly hate the Preachers, and long for the Mass <note>The Roman Catholic liturgy, which was banned in Geneva.</note> as a fish longs for water. I take that entire people as my witness.

### Calvin 79.

For even if it does not lie in the Princes' hands and will to take possession of the hearts of men with their command, so that they might obediently accept the doctrine of salvation and subject themselves to God; nevertheless, their calling requires that they do not turn a blind eye <note>original: "door vingheren sien," literally "look through their fingers."</note> nor allow the godless and wanton tongues to dishonor the holy name of God, or to disrupt His divine service.

### Vaticanus.

There he immediately praised Augustine, because he disputes very profitably that the unwilling are drawn so that they might come of their own accord. Now, having forgotten himself here, he confesses that the commands of Kings cannot take possession of the hearts of men so that they, accepting the doctrine of godliness, should subject themselves to God; which things clash flatly against each other: for coming of one’s own accord and obediently accepting the doctrine is one and the same thing. said that to trample godliness out of "licentiousness" is to sin willfully. If Servetus has done anything wrong, he did it out of ignorance. For which of these two causes did you kill Servetus? If you killed him because he spoke thus, then you killed him for the truth; for it is the truth when a man speaks as he thinks, even if he were in error. And in the 15th Psalm, those are called blessed who speak the truth from their hearts—and yet you have killed such a man. If you killed him because he felt <note>believed</note> thus, you ought to have taught him otherwise, or prove from Holy Scripture that one must kill those who are erring and ignorant.

->Calvin 81.<-

So then, the godly Magistrates shall be defenders of the doctrine of godliness, not only to (1) compel the unwilling to the faith, but to ensure that (2) Christ is not cast out of their land, by whose grace and benefit they possess their land and kingdom. (3) Likewise, that the holy name of God is not mocked with impunity, through whose glory their authority is so eminent. (4) Likewise, that the ungodly do not trample His doctrine with their feet, which maintains their kingdom in peace. (5) Indeed, that they do not silently turn a blind eye when the weak are sought for destruction, over whom they have been appointed by God as protectors.

# Dialogue

He admits that such a thing was not feasible, and now he says the opposite; and so he shamefully contradicts himself: indeed, what is more, he now attributes to the **Magistrates** <note>The civil government or city officials</note> that which he attributed to the **Teachers** in his first *Institutes*. For there, in Chapter 6, page 427, he speaks of **God’s word** as follows:

> These are the hidden spiritual workings of God, to cast down the strongholds, wherewith the soldiers of the faith destroy all counsels and high things that exalt themselves against the knowledge of God, and to lead every thought captive to the obedience of Christ, and have vengeance ready against all disobedience. See, there is described clearly and plainly the godliness with which all Shepherds of the church, by whatever name they may be called, ought to be adorned: namely, that they should boldly, through the word of God (whose ministers and dispensers they are), dare to stand against all things; so that through the glory and excellence of the same word, they cause all glory of this world, power, and height to yield, and that He may have dominion over everyone from the least to the greatest; that they build up Christ’s house and destroy the devil’s, feed the sheep, and punish, rebuke, overcome, bind, loosen, Lightning and Thunder against the rebellious and stubborn. But all of this only from the word of God.

Calvin wrote this at that time, yet performing everything with God's word, forcing the rebellious with the word to the obedience 

<column-break/>

of the faith, killing the Wolves with the word, yes, Thundering and Lightning with the word. But now he goes against his own word (since it is powerless) to forge a sword, and lays it upon the necks of the **Magistrates**, demanding that they shall force the unwilling to the faith with the sword. In this matter, he is again very shamefully at odds with himself. For in his last *Institutes* printed by **Robert Estienne** <note>original: "Robertus Stephanus"</note> in the year 1553, Chapter 8, section 170 (which I point out so precisely so that no one may be mistaken, in case Calvin might have changed something afterward, as he was often accustomed to do), he writes as follows:

> So there is then a great distinction in this, since the church does not take upon itself what properly belongs to the Magistrate; nor do the Magistrates do what is performed by the church.

Calvin says that there, distinguishing the Magistrate from the church as if the **Shepherds** and **Teachers** alone were the church. And there he denies that the Magistrate can do what is performed by the Church, yet here he says it is the office of the Magistrate to force the unwilling to the faith—which he had, in his first *Institutes*, attributed not to the sword, but to the word! ❧ Fickle bird, who changes everything according to your whim on the fly! ❧ Blind people, who let yourselves be led by this blind man! -> **Dialogue** <-

that is, concerning the word of God, which word was not given to the **Magistrates** <term>Civil authorities</term>, but was given and entrusted to the teachers. With this word, they must kill the wolves, destroy the house of the Devil, and furthermore establish everything that we have just recently cited from Calvin’s own writings. Likewise, since Christ did not resist any injustice done to Him or others but endured it—as many in His time were innocently put to death, most notably **John the Baptist**—so must the **Magistrate** follow that same example. Finally, if one wishes to model the Magistrate’s office after the life of Christ, then the Magistrate should carry no sword at all, so that he would be allowed to kill neither murderers nor **Heretics** <term>Those holding beliefs contrary to established church doctrine</term>. <column-break/>

lightened upon him. Thus, having completely forgotten himself, he committed a shameful murder; and furthermore, seeking to cover up this murder, he seeks to bring about countless more murders and twists all the words of God to his own ends. It is as if he let himself imagine that all people had made a pact with him to be willing to believe everything he might belch out, just as his disciples have a habit of doing. Now I think, Calvin, that your little tricks have been brought enough to the light of day: God sees you clearly, and before long, men shall see you clearly as well. (2) It is entirely absurd that this man goes about comparing the buyers and sellers against the **Apostates** <term>Those who have abandoned their faith</term>, Christ against the Magistrate, and the "driving out" against punishing and compelling. For Christ was no Magistrate, nor do the Apostates buy or sell in the Temple of the Lord, nor is "driving out" the same as punishing or compelling with the sword. For an Apostate is one who goes out from the Temple of the Lord—that is, out of the Congregation—of his own accord. How then could such a person still buy or sell in the church, or be driven out of it, when he is already outside? # Vaticanus and Calvin.
### f ij
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

If one were allowed to tell the truth, they are true **Canaanites** <term>A play on the Hebrew root 'knʿn', which can mean both 'Canaanite' and 'merchant'</term>, that is, merchants: for the name Canaan signifies such. For they teach for nothing but wages and pay. <margin>1 Corinthians 9:13</margin> I know well that "he who serves the altar must live from the altar," <note>original: "Ich weet wel dat hy van den Altaer moet leven / die den Altaer dient"</note> but they look so much toward the reward that if one did not wish to give them pay, not one in twenty among them would want to remain in the preaching office. It happened that in the **Duchy of Montbéliard** <term>A territory in the Holy Roman Empire, now part of France</term> an **Interim** <term>A temporary religious decree or settlement</term> arose by which the ministers' wages were cut, through which many were so affected that they suspended their preaching, at least until Easter, which was then at hand, even though they were requested by their Superintendent to continue. In **Lausanne**, there are always crowds who hunt after the preaching office just as in the Papacy they hunt after parsonages, abbeys, and similar "fat prizes" <note>An idiomatic way of describing lucrative church positions</note>; yes, they have made it so chaotic with their buying, quarreling, and bickering (for they are always at each other's throats like merchants) that the **Magistrate of Bern** has deducted one hundred guilders annually, in that currency, because they were always annoying the Magistrate with their bickering. The Magistrate did well when they realized that these merchants looked at nothing but profit, and they wanted to keep them in check with that financial loss. And that is one kind of their commerce; the other is this: that they have made holy Theology equal to other human arts. For they allow no one to the preach-

<column-break/>

ing office unless he be well experienced in human sciences, and unless he has knowledge of languages, such as Hebrew, Greek, and Latin, or at least he must know Latin. For these three languages (which are now dead and spoken by no nations or peoples) they alone count as "the languages." The other languages that still remain and are spoken by the peoples, they do not respect. So that even if someone knew twenty other languages but not these three, they would not give him the name of being a "man of languages." But if it were that he knew those three languages, and twenty more besides, they would still only call him a "man of three languages"—so much does that little sect value the languages that the common people do not understand. Furthermore, it is permitted to no one to preach except him who is sent by them; and they send no one except him who agrees with them in everything and considers all their sayings and doings to be good; yes, what is more, they send only those who flatter them in everything. <note>original: "pluymstrijckt" — literally 'feather-stroking' or sycophancy</note> Whoever has not received this "mark" <note>A reference to the 'Mark of the Beast,' used here to mock clerical ordination</note> from them, it is unlawful for him to preach. It is such that even if **Christ** Himself came, He would not be permitted to preach if He knew no Latin and were not sent by them. Also, as they drive commerce with the souls of men, they say (which we ought not to skip over) that there are two kinds of churches. However, we shall rather bring Calvin's own words here, from his last **Institutes** <term>Calvin’s "Institutes of the Christian Religion," his most influential work</term>, Chapter 8, in the explanation of the fourth part of the **Twelve Articles of Faith** <term>The Apostles' Creed</term>, where he says thus:

# A Conversation
### Page 80
<meta>Catchword: high-</meta>

Holy Scripture speaks in two ways concerning the **Church** <term>Dutch: 'Kercke'. The author is distinguishing between the 'invisible' and 'visible' church, a key concept in Reformation theology.</term>, for sometimes when it names the Church, it speaks of those who are truly with God, into which no one is received except those who, through the grace of adoption, are children of God, and through the sanctification of the Spirit, are true members of Christ. Frequently, the word "Church" is also used for all people spread throughout the whole world who profess and serve one God and one Christ; who are instructed in the faith through baptism and, through the use of the **Lord's Supper** <term>Dutch: 'avont-maels'</term>, testify to their unity in true doctrine and love, modeling their profession after the word of the Lord, and maintaining the ministry of preaching that was established by Him. In this assembly, there are many hypocrites who have nothing else in common with Christ but the bare title and appearance; indeed, among them are also many ambitious, greedy, envious ones, backbiters, and rogues of evil life, who are tolerated for a long time, either because they cannot be legally convicted, or because discipline is not always held as strictly as it ought to be. Just as one must believe that the congregation which is invisible to us is known and loved by God, so must we also follow and maintain fellowship with this one, which is called a congregation in the eyes of men. Item **Distinction 9** <note>This refers to Book 4, Chapter 1, Section 9 of John Calvin’s *Institutes of the Christian Religion*, where he discusses the 'judgment of charity' in the visible church.</note>: nevertheless, since He saw that it would serve our best interests to know who should be held as children of God, He has come to our aid in this.

<column-break/>

And since one cannot judge faith with absolute certainty, He has ordained and willed that, according to the nature of charity, one should hold as members of the congregation those who, by the profession of faith, a good life, and the use of the Sacraments, profess the same God and Christ with us.

> Those are **Calvin's** words; he calls such a people a "congregation"—namely, those who are called a congregation in the eyes of men, in which one does not need to have certainty of faith, but only the judgment according to the nature of charity, by which charity they judge without the certainty of faith. In this group are many hypocrites and others whom he has named. Now I ask: why does he not cast those out? Is it because they cannot be legally convicted? Why, that is much easier than with **Heretics** <term>Dutch: 'Ketters'</term>: for there is no dispute about who is a hypocrite, an ambitious person, or a greedy person. Above all, there is no dispute about the hypocrites who come in sheep's clothing and are known by their fruits—that is, by their murders—just as one knows a wolf by its devouring of the sheep. But there is a great dispute regarding Heretics, and Calvin has until now been unable to describe what a Heretic is, even though he is currently writing about **the execution of heretics** <note>This likely refers to Calvin’s 1554 treatise *Defensio orthodoxae fidei*, written after the execution of Michael Servetus to justify the death penalty for heresy.</note>. Why has he never banished anyone for their greed or envy, since he surely knows so many of them whom he could easily accuse or convict? Or is it because in **Geneva** the Discipline is not maintained as strictly as it—

...heard: Yes, they are kept strictly enough, and that through the urging of Calvin. And above, he has said that only the good Magistrate may kill **Heretics** <term>Dutch: 'Ketters'. In this period, heresy was often treated as a capital crime against the state.</term>. He certainly made his Magistrate believe this against **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553) was a Spanish scholar executed in Geneva for heresy after denying the Trinity. What is lacking then, that he cannot also demand of them that they kill the Hypocrites and the greedy? Or does he think that the Hypocrites are better than the Heretics? If he does not think so, why does the Magistrate listen to him more regarding the killing of Heretics than the killing of these others? But I am nauseated by this and rack my brain: the sins that God considers the heaviest, Calvin considers the smallest, as appears from his laws which he made concerning the **ministry of the Church** <term>Dutch: 'bedieninghe der Kercke', likely referring to the 'Ecclesiastical Ordinances' of Geneva.</term>, in which he makes two sorts of sin: The first sort is such that they are not to be tolerated at all in a **Preacher** <term>Dutch: 'Predikant'</term>, among which Heresy stands at the front, followed by games forbidden by law, and dancing, etc. The other sort is such that they may indeed be suffered in a Preacher, and under this he places mockery, lying, backbiting, greed, great anger, and a quarrelsome nature. These sins, he thinks, can be tolerated, even though no one is more condemned in Holy Scripture than the mockers—who are counted among the ungodly in the **first Psalm** <note>Psalm 1:1: "Blessed is the man who... sits not in the seat of scoffers."</note>—and the greedy, since **the Apostle** <note>Referring to the Apostle Paul in 1 Timothy 6:10.</note>

<column-break/>

calls greed the root of all evil. Yet these sins, which can be most easily recognized by anyone, Calvin can well tolerate; but Heresy, gaming, and dancing, Calvin cannot tolerate. How shall one deal with this man, who considers mockery, greed, and backbiting to be smaller sins than heresy, gaming, and dancing? So one sees clearly from this that the greedy, haters, backbiters, accusers, slanderers, liars, and hypocrites (the latter of which Calvin does not even name in his Laws, so little a matter does he consider hypocrisy) may freely make their home with them. But if there is someone who differs from them in anything, even if he is of a pious life, that is a Heretic, and he may not be suffered.

I shall bring here an example from which you will easily be able to judge the rest: **Johannes Gastius** <note>Johannes Gastius (d. 1552) was a Reformed minister in Basel known for his writings against the Anabaptist movement.</note>, a Preacher at Basel, tells in his **Book of the Anabaptists** <term>Dutch: 'Weder-doperen'. A radical Reformation group that practiced adult baptism and was widely persecuted.</term> that there was a Ropemaker, an Anabaptist, who was sentenced to death in Basel, but **Oecolampadius** <note>Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531) was the primary Reformer of Basel.</note> pleaded for him with the Magistrate, on condition that he should recant everything. He recanted, and afterwards he became a godless rogue, for which nevertheless no one addressed or punished him. # Dialogue

I say; afterwards he arranged it so that one has never heard of a more wicked, more disorderly, or more shameless person. He boasted to his companions that he had suffered persecution when he sought to live piously and followed sound doctrine; but now that he was full of all sorts of villainy—drinking and carousing night and day, chasing harlots, dancing and leaping—he was never once addressed or rebuked by anyone.

->This is related by **Joannes Gastius**.<- <note>Johannes Gast (d. 1552) was a Protestant reformer in Basel. The author uses his account to illustrate how some used the Reformation as an excuse for moral laxity.</note>

This is their doctrine: namely, that where the Church is, there the doctrine and the use of the Sacraments is healthy; that sins cannot be overcome; and that we are and shall remain flesh as long as we live. In such ways they flatter the people and the Magistrate—who knows nothing of this—and they always incite and goad them against the **Heretics** <term>Dutch: 'Ketters'; here referring to those who disagreed with the state-sanctioned Reformed doctrine.</term> (for so they call those who differ from them), and they claim that there are no more wicked people than these, nor any who have better deserved death.

Thus, in their view, it is a much greater sin to be **re-baptized** <term>The 'Anabaptists' believed that infant baptism was invalid and that believers should be baptized again as adults. This was a capital offense in many regions.</term> than to whore, to hate, to backbite, to gamble and play, to waste good and precious time uselessly, to run and race hither and thither—as the peasant-hunters commonly do—to spend the time of youth with harlots and scoundrels, and to be a proud, backbiting, and self-interested person, yes, even a miser, a rogue, or a blasphemer. They command that those who are re-baptized shall be killed, not

<column-break/>

from the Holy Scriptures, but according to the opinion of **Zwingelus** <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the leader of the Reformation in Switzerland.</note>, who prescribed this magnificent law: *ut qui Mersus, fuerit, Mergatur* <note>Original Latin: "that he who was immersed [re-baptized], shall be submerged [drowned]."</note>, which is to say, that he who was re-baptized should be smothered in the water. And although they always cry out that they act according to the Holy Scripture, they have nonetheless not been able to prove with a single syllable from it that one must kill all those who wander in their understanding of the true meaning of the Lord's Supper or baptism. **O Magistrates**, you who should be led, they are misleading you. This is how they play with souls: in doing so, they promise life to souls to whom God does not promise life, and they kill the souls that God does not kill. Therefore, when the Son of God shall come, He will drive out those merchants—not with such a whip as they drive us out with, namely the executioner's sword, but with His judgment and His invincible word—because they defile and violate the entire Temple with public outrages and blasphemies.

### Calvin § 3.

<margin>Luke 12:14</margin>
(1) Here Christ gave proof of His holy zeal, and furthermore, in the entire course of His calling, He refrained from such outward punishments so that He would not mix the spiritual kingdom, which was commanded to Him by the Father, with any earthly dominion. For He who did not wish to be a judge between the brothers who (2) quarreled over their inheritance, nevertheless desired that the godly judges should exercise their right and power.

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

### Vaticanus.

(1) Well then, has he given the Magistrates an example here? Why not later as well? ### Calvin 84.

Besides this, Isaiah clearly indicates in his prophecy that the gentleness <term>Dutch: 'Sachtmoedicheyt'; a core virtue in this debate, meaning meekness, mildness, or lack of cruelty.</term>, which he so highly praises in Christ, does not belong to all men without distinction.

### Vaticanus.

<margin>Matthew 11:29.</margin>
Nevertheless, he is the door, so that whoever does not enter through him is a thief and a murderer. "Learn from me," he says (addressing all burdened people), "that I am gentle and I will refresh you." But Calvin thinks that this gentleness does not concern him, and that is also why he is not refreshed.

### Calvin 85.

To Christ is attributed a sweet and quiet friendliness, through which he bears the weak, and not that through which he further hardens headstrong wickedness.

<column-break/>

### Calvin 86.

Christ is gentle so as not to break the bruised reed, but not to strengthen those in their obstinacy who destroy the weak.

### Calvin 87.

He is gentle so as not to extinguish the smoking flax, but not so as not to drive away the darkness.

### Vaticanus.

With what should he drive it away? With the sword, or with the light?

### Vaticanus.

Crushing heads is not the same as driving away the darkness: and by the iron scepter of Christ, understand the Holy Scripture, the powerful and invincible [word]...

<margin>2 Cor 10:8 / Luke 9:56</margin> Paul boasts that power has been given to him for building up and not for destruction. And Christ says that he did not come to destroy, but to save. So it is that Calvin himself does not possess those powers, though he nevertheless boasts of them. ->Calvin 91.<-

->vaticanus.<-

He contradicts himself: for in his *Institutes* <note>John Calvin’s "Institutes of the Christian Religion," his seminal work of Protestant theology.</note>, as we have pointed out, he says that the <term>Magistrate</term> cannot do what is carried out by the Church: and now he argues thus: "If it is permitted for the ministers of the word to compel the ungodly with physical punishments, then it is also permitted for the Princes." But we shall argue differently: just as the Apostles punished the guilty with their sword—that is, with the word—those who were under the power of the word, and that for their sins which they had committed with words and teachings: so too must the Magistrate punish the guilty with his iron sword, those who are under the power of the sword, and that for their sins which he may punish with the sword. And just as Peter could not have killed any villains with the word, but would have left them to the Magistrate...

...left alone. Likewise, the Magistrate cannot kill any hypocrite, heretic, or liar (for no law has been given to him for that), but must leave them to be punished by the Teachers <note>Referring to religious leaders or ministers who should correct error through doctrine.</note>; or otherwise, ecclesiastical matters would be mingled with worldly things. This is taught quite openly by <margin>2 Kings 6:22</margin> Elisha, when he gives an answer to the King of Israel (when the King asked if he should strike the soldiers <note>The original Dutch uses "Spijers," referring to the Syrian army that Elisha had miraculously blinded and led into the city.</note> whom Elisha had captured), saying: "You shall not strike them: those whom you capture with your sword and bow, strike those." So also will a good shepherd say to the Magistrate: "You shall not kill the hypocrites, heretics, and liars; but those whom you have captured with your sword and bow <note>A metaphor for civil criminals like murderers or thieves who fall under secular jurisdiction.</note>, kill those." The Lord shall capture and kill these [heretics] with the breath of His mouth. Kill the murderers, robbers, and the like, whom you can capture with the sword. But to make an end of this, I shall compare Calvin with Peter, so that one might see plainly what kind of follower of Peter he has been. Peter rebuked Ananias and (as Calvin wishes to say) also killed him with the word alone, without anyone else helping him. Calvin killed Servetus solely with the sword, and that through the help of the Magistrate. Peter killed Ananias for his lies, because he had lied to the Holy Spirit; Calvin killed Servetus for the truth, because he did not wish to lie. For had Servetus wished to recant and speak against his conscience, he would have been left alone; but now that he spoke

<column-break/>

as he felt in his heart, he was killed. Peter achieved so much through this that others afterwards shrank from lying. But Calvin has, through this deed of his, brought it about that others now always, out of fear of punishment, will shrink from helping the truth. Peter demonstrated the power of his Word to the glory of God; but Calvin has shown hereby the weakness <note>original: "cranckheyt" — literally infirmity or sickness of the argument.</note> of his word, to the glory of the sword, which is Calvin’s God. One does not read that Peter did any such thing thereafter; but Calvin has written this book out of pure partisanship and stubbornness, to fill the whole world with similar persecutions and murders. Woe to the blind leaders of the blind! Woe, woe also to those who are led by them, unless they are now willing to open their eyes and let themselves be led on the right path.

->Calvin 52.<-

It is indeed a cruel gentleness that they praise so much, that one exposes the sheep to plunder in order to spare the wolves.

->Vaticanus.<-

Calvin himself previously taught that the wolves would be killed by the word, specifically by the shepherds; and now he wants them killed by the Magistrate. But how can they be killed by both? I say nothing of the fact that the wolves come in sheep’s clothing, though inwardly they are ravening wolves, who shall be known by their fruits.

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

The fruit of wolves is to eat raw meat. From which it then appears that they are not wolves who are killed, but those who kill others are the wolves.

### Calvin 93.

They poison souls with the venom of false doctrine; and should one then still withhold the legal power of the **sword** <note>The "power of the sword" refers to the legal authority of the state to carry out capital punishment.</note> from their bodies?

### Vaticanus.

<margin>Acts 12:23</margin>
The envious, the greedy, and the proud also poison souls, for through their words and works they cause many to become like them: but to no one is the power given to attack them with the sword. Herod was killed by the Angel of the Lord for his pride, but no Magistrate would have been allowed to kill him for that reason.

### Calvin 94.

Should one then let the whole body of Christ perish, because one should not be allowed to touch the stench of a rotten limb?

### Vaticanus.

<margin>1 Cor. 5:5</margin>
Paul cuts off the rotten members when he delivers them over to Satan; that is, when he **excommunicates** <term>Excommunication: The formal exclusion of a person from the sacraments and community of the Church.</term> them: and that as a punishment of the body, so that the spirit may be saved on the day of the Lord: and then he receives them back again when they have repented. This office, which belongs to the Pastors, Calvin shifts onto the Magistrate, even against his own teaching: for in the case of Bartelrius <note>Referring to Barthélemy Berthelier, a Genevan citizen who challenged Calvin’s right to excommunicate individuals without the consent of the city council.</note>, he wrote that it was the office of the pastors and not of the Magistrate. And on this point, there has been a great dispute

<column-break/>

between him and the Magistrate of Geneva, so much so that the dispute was brought before the Swiss churches. If the Magistrate then cuts off the members, the Pastor cannot do so, and they can then also not be taken back in: if the Pastor does it, the Magistrate cannot do it. I will set aside the fact that killing a person is not the same as "cutting off a limb." Members are cut off through unbelief, <margin>Rom. 11:20</margin> as Paul teaches when speaking of the Jews who were broken off from the Olive tree, who nevertheless were not killed. When the Magistrate kills a citizen, he does not merely disenfranchise him, but takes his life. So also when someone is killed as a **heretic**, then he is not cut off from the body of Christ, but from physical life; otherwise, if physical death were a cutting off from the body of Christ, then all who die would have to be cut off from the church.

### Calvin 95.

I acknowledge well that godly teachers must admonish Princes to deal gently with them, and that they, like physicians, should not immediately use the strongest medicines, as is done in diseases where there is little hope. For there is a great difference between that unrestrained gentleness, which is the mother of all evil, and the gentle and polite manner of healing.

### Vaticanus.

Had Calvin followed this last [advice] well, he would [have kept] Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a physician and theologian executed in Geneva for heresy, primarily for his rejection of the Trinity.</note> still on the...

# A Dialogue

on the first day that he saw him, he would have complained about his person; nor would he afterwards have had him put to death so hastily and so cruelly; indeed, I might say, he would not have brought himself to ruin in such a way <note>This refers to the trial and execution of Michael Servetus in 1553, suggesting that if Calvin had truly seen the state of the man, he might not have pushed for his death.</note>.

### Calvin 96.

They cite certain passages of Scripture which also seem to have some color of truth: "Let (says Christ) the **weeds** <term>weeds: Often translated as "tares" in the Parable of the Weeds (Matthew 13), referring to people who look like believers but are not.</term> grow up with the **wheat**, so that you, in gathering the weeds, do not also pluck out the wheat." But if they want to stick precisely to the words, they will take away not only the Magistrate's use of the **sword** <note>The "sword" symbolizes the legal authority to execute criminals.</note> but also all church discipline.

### Vaticanus.

We shall explain more clearly what it means to "pluck out the weeds," since this matter is of great importance.
<margin>1 Cor. 3 / 4</margin> When there was strife and discord among the Corinthians—one crying, "I am of Paul," and another, "I am of Apollos"—Paul rebuked them as follows:

"But with me it is a very small thing that I should be judged by you, or by a human court: for I do not even judge myself. For I know nothing against myself, yet I am not thereby justified: but He who judges me is the Lord. Therefore judge nothing before the time, until the Lord comes, who will both bring to light the things hidden in darkness, and will make manifest the counsels of the

<column-break/>

hearts: and then shall every man have praise from God." This I have applied to myself and to Apollos by way of an analogy for your sakes, that you might learn in us not to think of yourselves more highly than that which is written to you by me: so that no one, for someone else's sake, be puffed up one against the other; for who distinguishes you?

Those are Paul’s words, just as if he wanted to say: he who says, "I am of Paul," boasts of Paul and, in his judgment, sets him above Apollos, and thus he also sets himself above Apollos's disciples. In doing so, one attempts to judge who is more excellent and more pleasing to God: which must not be judged by men before the Day of the Lord. Therefore, although I know of no guilt within myself, yet I will not justify myself, even if I were truly righteous. For I may not judge myself, but the Lord shall do that, whose judgment has not yet come but must be awaited. Whoever judges someone as to whether they are **rejected** or **chosen** <term>rejected or chosen: Referring to the theological concepts of Reprobation and Election—who goes to hell and who goes to heaven.</term> judges concerning hidden things and the counsels of the heart. For according to the counsels of the heart, the Lord shall either reject or accept men. But those hidden things shall not be revealed before the day of the Lord’s coming; therefore, one cannot judge before that day who is chosen or rejected. Otherwise, it could have happened that the Corinthians would have rejected someone whom God had not rejected, and that they would not have rejected those whom God had indeed rejected.

<summary>In this passage, "Vaticanus" uses Paul’s letters to the Corinthians to argue that human beings lack the divine insight required to judge a person's spiritual standing or "hidden heart," and therefore should not execute those labeled as weeds (heretics).</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Tares, 1 Corinthians, judgment, Calvin, Castellio, religious tolerance, election, reprobation, magistrate, church discipline</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate between Vaticanus (the pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The discussion centers on the interpretation of the Parable of the Weeds (Matthew 13) and the limits of human judgment regarding who is "saved" or "damned," specifically in the context of religious persecution.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

Therefore, although they were endowed with some knowledge, through which they could judge other things well, nonetheless it was not so great that Paul allowed them to judge such high things, even though he far surpassed them in knowledge. Now go and compare this passage from Paul with the **Parable of the Weeds**; you will find that they are completely identical. For when the **householder** says that they should not pluck up the weeds before the time of the **Harvest**, so that they would not pull up the **Wheat** at the same time: that is the same thing Paul says, that they should not judge before the time, namely: that they should condemn no one who ought not to be condemned. Because that is what those people commonly do who wish to purify the Church with the **sword** <note>The "sword" refers to the use of state power and capital punishment to enforce religious orthodoxy.</note>. Likewise, when the householder says: "In the time of the Harvest I will say to the reapers," etc. That is the same thing Paul means with the words "Until the Lord comes," etc. That is, when He shall say to the blessed "Come," and to the cursed "Go." Therefore, to pluck up the weeds is as much as to say that someone is rejected and to cut them off from the **body of Christ** <note>The "body of Christ" is a theological term for the collective community of Christian believers.</note> for eternity: which cannot happen before the Day of the Lord. What Calvin wants to say here about the **Magistrate** <term>Magistrate: A civil government official with the power to enforce laws and carry out executions.</term> does not fit the weeds: for if he kills a murderer, he does not cast him out of the body of Christ, but out of this life. And the Magistrate often has a good hope for the soul of the one he has 

<column-break/>

killed, nor does he kill him because he is evil, but because he has committed evil deeds and done harm. But with the **heretics**, it is a different matter. For those who kill them believe that they are rejected and damned for eternity, so that they are already hated before God’s Judgment—which Paul certainly wants us to wait for. Therefore, one does not err in the killing of evildoers, as long as the evil deeds are evident: so much so that even the evildoers themselves confess to having deserved death. But in the killing of heretics, one easily errs, since many godly people have been put to death as heretics. These people may have admitted to the things they were accused of, but they nevertheless deny that those things were sinful or worthy of death. First of all, Christ did not deny what He was accused of—namely, that He had said He was the **Son of God**: for which cause He was killed as a **Blasphemer**. The same thing later happened to the **Apostles**, and many others besides, who were killed because they made a profession of the Christian religion. Since this happened to them regarding matters of religion through the wickedness of men—and since such a thing has not happened in other matters (for those who were punished at that time for wicked deeds were rightly killed, and neither the Jews nor the other **Heathens** <note>Refers to non-Christian, non-Jewish people, often Romans in this context.</note> were ever punished by God for such sentences)—we must not think

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus argues that the Parable of the Weeds forbids humans from "plucking up" heretics through execution, as only God can judge a person's eternal soul at the final harvest. He distinguishes between civil crimes, which are clear and punishable by the state, and religious dissent, which is often wrongly punished as "blasphemy."</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Weeds, judgment, execution of heretics, Magistrate, body of Christ, blasphemy, Sebastian Castellio, John Calvin</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate between Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) and Calvin regarding the Parable of the Weeds. The text argues that humans cannot accurately distinguish between true believers and "heretics" (the weeds) because both appear outwardly righteous. Therefore, capital punishment for heresy risks killing the "sons of the King" (the saved).</meta>

# Dialogue
### Page 88

...that our times are now better than those were back then, or that one cannot err as easily now as then; for it is written that the last times—that is, these times of ours—shall be the worst. Add to this: when Christ says that the sower of the good seed is the **Son of Man**, and that the sower of the **weeds** is the enemy, that is, the **Devil**, who sowed the weeds according to Christ’s teaching, it does not seem that by "weeds" he means murderers, adulterers, and similar evildoers. For the Devil did not sow such people only after the preaching of the **Gospel**, since they have existed for a very long time before that. Instead, it seems that by "weeds" we should understand those whom the Devil has sown through his false teaching after the Gospel was proclaimed. And such were the **Heretics** and **Hypocrites**, who seek to deceive people under the cloak of the **Christian Religion**, hiding among the godly like weeds among the corn until they bear fruit. Christ wants these weeds to be left alone until the time of the **Harvest**, so that by chance the good are not killed alongside the evil. For it is better that all the evil remain alive than that one good person be killed among the evil—just as a King would rather all murderers stayed alive than have one of his own sons killed along with them. To take an example: suppose the King of France had several of his sons spread throughout his kingdom...

<column-break/>

...and dressed in white garments, yet they were unknown to the people. Among these, some hypocrites had also mingled, who had also put on white garments to be thought of as sons of the King, passing themselves off as the King's sons. The King, having learned of this, forbids anyone—on pain of death—from daring to kill or seize anyone dressed in a white garment, so that none of the King's sons would be killed. Instead, he says that in a year's time, he will summon them all together and strip them, and then he will kill the hypocrites himself and save his sons, whom he will recognize by this: they will have a **Lily** <note>The lily (fleur-de-lis) is the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy; here it represents a hidden, divine mark of identity.</note> marked upon their bare bodies. Would a man not be punished by the King if he killed a man dressed in white—whether he were the King's son or not—before that time? Now, this King is **Christ**, and those dressed in white garments are his **Disciples**. But the **Hypocrites** and **Heretics** have mingled among them and put on white garments to be honored as children of God; that is, they live in **apparent virtue** <term>apparent virtue: (Dutch: *schijndeucht*) an outward show of holiness or moral behavior that hides an ungodly heart.</term> to be regarded as holy and godly people. Christ, seeing this, commands that both should be left to stand until the **Day of Judgment**, when he shall pull away the cloak of appearance and bring all the secrets of darkness to light, revealing the hidden counsels of the heart. Must we then not wait for that day? Or do we think we are so profound...

<summary>This page uses a parable about the King of France and his sons to argue that because heretics and true believers look identical outwardly, only Christ can safely separate them at the final judgment. It concludes that executing suspected heretics is a dangerous overreach of human authority that risks killing God's own children.</summary>
<keywords>Parable of the Weeds, religious tolerance, heresy, hypocrisy, Day of Judgment, King of France, fleur-de-lis, capital punishment, Sebastian Castellio, John Calvin</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (the pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The previous page used the analogy of a King's sons hidden in a crowd to argue that executing "heretics" is dangerous because one might accidentally kill a true believer. Castellio is now challenging the idea that any religious group has the absolute certainty required to execute others in God's name.</meta>

-># Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

...that we might come to know the counsels of the hearts, which, as Paul says, shall not be revealed until the day of the Lord? <note>original: "inden dage des Heeren"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 4:5.</note> Do we not act against the prohibition of Christ when we kill the hypocrites who are dressed in white garments—that is, those who have not yet committed villainous deeds? For they would not be "white" if they had committed them, since one becomes "black" through villainous acts. Could it not easily happen that some of the Godly would be killed along with them, just as it has always been seen to happen? If we say that we do not err, that is the same thing those who killed the Godly have always said. But could we not err just as well as they? Especially if we wish to do what they did, though we are not a hair better than they were. Who has ever believed that they held a false religion? The Jews erred, they who persecuted Christ and the Apostles. The Pope erred, he who persecuted the **Lutherans** and **Zwinglians**. <note>Followers of the Swiss reformer Huldrych Zwingli.</note> **Henry, King of England**, erred, he who killed the Papists, Lutherans, Zwinglians, and **Anabaptists**. <note>term: "Anabaptists" (wederdooperen); a radical Reformation group that practiced adult baptism and was widely persecuted.</note> **Luther** also erred, he who called the Zwinglians "devils" and condemned them to hell. Or shall the **Calvinists** and Zwinglians alone not err? Or shall they alone sit upon the judgment seat, and shall they alone be permitted to judge and kill the **Heretics**? <term>Heretics</term> <note>From the Greek 'hairesis' meaning 'choice'; used here to describe those whose religious opinions differed from the established church.</note> O you wicked servant, who—because your Lord delays his coming—becomes drunk and arrogant in your luxury, and beats and kills your fellow servants; the Lord shall come on a day

<column-break/>

and hour when you do not expect him, and shall cut you into pieces, and your portion he shall appoint with the hypocrites; there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth. <note>A paraphrase of the Parable of the Faithful Servant from Matthew 24.</note>

### Calvin 97.

**Excommunication** is the best remedy to cleanse the Church: if one is not permitted to touch the **weeds**, then this remedy cannot be put to work.

### Vaticanus.

That does not follow. For those whom Paul **excommunicated**, he handed over to the Devil for a time, so that through that punishment they might improve themselves and their Spirit might be saved (once they had improved) in the day of the Lord. That was not the same as rooting out the weeds, for the weed that is rooted out perishes entirely. Nor did Paul regard them as weeds; for one does not gather weeds into the barns, but Paul preserved them for the barn of Christ.

### Calvin 98.

Add to this, that the Lord there does not only speak of false teachings, but understands by it, in the gross, all the wickednesses that the Devil sows to destroy the good seed. (1) Therefore it would have to follow that the laws and Judges must stand still, if one were required to let the weeds stand until the time of the **Harvest**.

### Vaticanus.

That does not follow, as we have indicated here a little while ago. But Calvin, not understanding what is meant by those "weeds," flatly denies that one...

<meta>Signature G. Catchword: "it"</meta>

<summary>Castellio (as Vaticanus) argues that no religious group is immune to error and warns that persecuting others leads to the judgment described in the Gospels. He distinguishes between excommunication—intended to reform the sinner—and the "rooting out" (execution) of heretics, which he opposes.</summary>

It is worth noticing how swiftly

according to his foresight and

Apostles in faith.* So then it is 
no such great wonder that he 
so manfully advocated for the

against this great companion

For he has resolved (inasmuch

<summary>Castellio (Vaticanus) criticizes Calvin for calling Gamaliel "blind," noting that even Calvin's ally Bullinger praised Gamaliel's merciful advice. He argues that by Calvin's logic, if Calvin had been present at the trial of the Apostles, he would have voted to execute them.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue criticizing John Calvin’s justification for religious persecution, specifically contrasting his actions against Michael Servetus with the biblical advice of Gamaliel.</meta>

# A Conversation

Well, what do you say to that, Calvin? If someone had given the Cardinal of Tournon <note>François de Tournon (1489–1562), a powerful French cardinal and diplomat who was a fierce opponent of the Reformation.</note> Gamaliel’s advice <note>Referring to Acts 5:34-39, where the Pharisee Gamaliel advised the Sanhedrin to leave the Apostles alone, arguing that if their work was of human origin it would fail, but if it was of God, it could not be stopped.</note> regarding those five people <note>Likely referring to the "Five Martyrs of Lyon," students trained in Lausanne who were arrested and executed in France in 1553.</note> whom he had arrested in Paris for religious matters—shortly before you did the same to Servetus <note>Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian executed for heresy in Calvin's Geneva in 1553.</note>—would you also have said that he was "blind"? But what would you have advised the Cardinal then? To let the people go? By what arguments? Because they held the true religion? But the Cardinal did not believe that, and you would not have been able to force him to believe it. You would then have entered into a dispute with him. Well, he would not have agreed to that, for he is like the Scribes <term>Schriftgheleerden: scholars of religious law who often opposed new teachings</term>. Consider also: that such Disputations often turn out to be long, and have already lasted well over thirty years, while the plight of the prisoners requires immediate counsel.

Tell us then: what would you have advised the Cardinal in order to save those people’s lives? Do you not see that in such a case there can be no wiser or better counsel than Gamaliel’s advice? For he does not bring up that counsel in a matter where the Scribes were certain. If they had been deliberating whether to punish adulterers, he would have surely refrained from saying, *If it is of God, etc.* For everyone knows well that Adultery is not of God; nor does one ever deliberate over such matters. But since they were deliberating over religious matters, about which there was great disagreement at the time, even if he were a Christian (as he perhaps was),

<column-break/>

<margin>Matthew 15:13</margin> he could have given the Jews no better advice. For first, that reasoning is true: "If it is not of God, it shall perish." Just as Christ says: "Every plant which my heavenly Father has not planted shall be uprooted: let them alone," etc. Also what David said when he could have killed Saul, yet would not do so. Likewise, those examples that Gamaliel brings forward are true: for that Theudas, and Judas the Galilean, had perished. Now, that which Gamaliel feared—namely, that they might possibly be fighting against God—is exactly what happened to them later. Had they always followed Gamaliel’s advice, they would never have fought against God.

It is exactly the same even to this very day. For if the Papists <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics loyal to the Pope.</note> followed this advice, they would never kill so many innocent people. I say the same of the Zwinglians <note>Followers of the Swiss reformer Huldrych Zwingli.</note> and Calvinists, who in this regard are "apt disciples" and followers of the Papists. But now, when they hear Master John Calvin say that Gamaliel was "blind," they want to follow this leader; thus they will fall squarely against Gamaliel. And so, through Calvin’s counsel, even the blood of Calvinists themselves shall be shed. But this man is so bloodthirsty, and persecutes the deceased Servetus with such insatiable hatred, that he would rather see his own people burned by the Papists than have them saved by Gamaliel’s advice, simply so that he might not appear to have been mistaken. Furthermore, where Calvin says that Gamaliel’s advice [would overturn] all political ordinances and church disci-

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "discipline," which matches the catchword "pline" at the bottom of the page.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Gamaliel’s biblical advice of tolerance is the only practical way to prevent religious bloodshed, accusing Calvin of being so blinded by his hatred for Servetus that he rejects this wisdom, thereby endangering his own followers.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Gamaliel, Michael Servetus, religious persecution, Cardinal of Tournon, Zwinglians, Papists, tolerance, heresy</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of John Calvin’s arguments for the state’s role in punishing heresy. The speaker, Vaticanus, argues that while civil authorities are fit to judge clear crimes like murder or adultery, they are as unqualified to judge complex theological disputes as a Roman governor was to judge Jewish law.</meta>

-> Vaticanus, and Calvin. <-

[disci]pline to be knocked down, there he spares the truth. For the Discipline of the Apostles was not overturned, even though it was not supported by these Calvinist props: and civil ordinances remain firm nonetheless in such crimes that are certain, and where there is no dispute. In the Law of Moses, the seventy Elders judged over light and minor matters: but the heaviest cases they brought to Moses, and he asked God for counsel regarding them. So can it also be done today: one can take on recognizable crimes such as adultery, murder, false testimony, and the like, and judge them without danger. But regarding Heretics, there is great dispute (for if there were no dispute, one would proceed with them as one does with the others); therefore, one must ask the mouth of God for counsel there. Just as Moses then awaited God’s answer, and yet the civil ordinances were not overturned or diminished thereby, so we also ought to await the time of the judgment of our God, which shall be the surest answer regarding these points of contention. Meanwhile, God will nonetheless use the service of His own in matters that are beyond dispute.

### Calvin 102.

Claudius Lysias <note>A Roman tribune mentioned in the Book of Acts (23:26), who rescued the Apostle Paul from a mob in Jerusalem.</note> writes to the Governor Felix that he found no guilt in Paul, except that he had a dispute with the Jews only concerning the Law: not that he approved of Paul's case, but because he did not respect the Law; indeed, he could well endure that it be uprooted, laughing in his sleeve <note>original: "lachende in de vuijst" (laughing in the fist); a Dutch idiom meaning to be secretly amused or satisfied.</note> because through such bickering

<column-break/>

and twisting, he [tries to tread it] underfoot <unclear>tracht</unclear>. With such a "good advocate" as this, Servetus also tries to help himself to escape his sentence, as if these were the words of God which Luke <note>Traditional author of the Book of Acts.</note> recounts as having been spoken by such a godless man.

### Vaticanus.

Lysias was sent out to judge over crimes. But regarding the Law, he could no more judge than he could judge music. Therefore, he acted wisely in that he did not wish to judge a matter for which he had no understanding, and which was not charged to him. Had the Magistrate of Geneva been wise, they would <unclear>never</unclear> have taken it upon themselves to judge Servetus any more than music. For as for the words "Apostasy in essence" <note>original: "Apostatie wesentlycker wijse"; referring to the philosophical nature of a sin or state of being.</note> and "Connotative, in a recognizable way" <note>original: "Cognotativo, kentlijcker wijse"; likely referring to scholastic distinctions used in the trial to define heresy.</note>, and many others like them that are not found in the Bible, yet were used throughout Servetus’s case—indeed, as for this entire dark and subtle Calvinist disputation which Calvin himself does not understand—what (I say) these words mean, I am quite certain that the magistrate understands no more than Governor Lysias understood the case of Paul. This is sufficiently clear from their letters which they sent to the Swiss Ministers <term>Predikanten: Protestant clergy or ministers</term>, in which they write that they do not doubt the faithfulness of their Ministers: which is as much as to say that they judge based on other people's opinions and see through other people’s eyes.

<sig>G iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Cal-</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus argues that civil magistrates are unqualified to judge complex theological "crimes" like heresy, comparing the Geneva authorities' lack of understanding to the Roman Governor Lysias's indifference toward Jewish law. He asserts that while secular law is for clear crimes like murder, religious disputes should be left to God's ultimate judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Servetus, John Calvin, Geneva, religious tolerance, heresy, Claudius Lysias, civil law vs. divine law, scholasticism, Acts of the Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Calvin and Vaticanus regarding whether the "sword" (secular punishment or violence) should be used to defend the Christian faith. The previous page discussed whether civil magistrates are qualified to judge religious heresy, comparing them to Roman officials who stayed out of Jewish religious disputes.</meta>

# A Dialogue

<page-num>96</page-num>
**Header:** A Dialogue

->Calvin 103.<-

Lastly, they raise against us the argument that when Peter was commanded to put his sword into its sheath, the Lord thereby clearly made known how much he is displeased when the Gospel is protected by an armed hand: but what if I also replied here that Christ himself did not even demonstrate his innocence with words before Pilate, which he nonetheless could have done by good right?

->Vaticanus.<-

It seems to me that this flatly contradicts Calvin's own argument; for, if Christ did not even fight with words, how much less would he fight with the sword?

->Calvin 104.<-

If he—wishing to be obedient to the Father’s will—remained silent like a sheep before the shearer, and suspended the preaching of the Gospel until he should have cleansed sin through the sacrifice of his death, should we then wonder so much that he forbade Peter to strike?

->Vaticanus.<-

It is also the Father’s will that we must suffer with him if we wish to reign with him. <note>A reference to 2 Timothy 2:12: "If we suffer, we shall also reign with him." Vaticanus argues that Christians should accept persecution rather than use force to avoid it.</note>

->Calvin 105.<-

But I have yet another answer ready: namely, that Christ does not grant the right of the sword to his servant because he, being a private individual <note>term: "particulier persoon" refers to a citizen who holds no official government office or "magistracy."</note>, had rashly taken that same right upon himself.

->Vaticanus.<-

He was also a private individual

<column-break/>

when he killed Ananias and Sapphira. <note>Referring to Acts 5:1–11, where Peter’s words led to the immediate death of a couple who lied to the Apostles. Vaticanus uses this to challenge Calvin’s definition of a "private person."</note> But tell me now, Calvin: when you went to accuse Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish physician and theologian executed in Geneva for heresy, largely due to Calvin’s prosecution.</note>, did you then—likewise being a private individual—not also take the right of the sword upon yourself? For he who accuses someone [of a capital crime] kills him. It is true that you did not kill him with your own hands, but Herod did not kill John [the Baptist] with his own hands either.

->Calvin 106.<-

He [Christ] did not want the soldiers to be driven away by a riot—the very soldiers by whom he had to be bound—namely, so that he would not be followed by the bad reputation of raising a mob of murderers under the name of Gospel ministers.

->Vaticanus.<-

Calvin was not afraid of this bad reputation. <note>A biting sarcasm from Vaticanus, suggesting Calvin was untroubled by being seen as a "murderer" for his role in religious executions.</note>

->Calvin 107.<-

From this, one can by no means conclude that the hands of Princes are tied, preventing them from carrying out their usual office.

->Vaticanus.<-

But we deny that this (namely, the killing of heretics) is the usual and ordinary office of Princes.

->Calvin 108.<-

In short, he does not forbid Peter from attacking them with force, or intend to take the sword away from the Magistrate, or forbid the same from legally defending the Gospel. (1) But

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Calvin and Vaticanus debate whether the Bible forbids using violence to defend the faith. Vaticanus accuses Calvin of acting like a "private executioner" in the death of Michael Servetus, while Calvin argues that secular rulers still have the right to use the sword to protect the Gospel.</summary>

# Dialogue

For one should read the philosophers who have written on this matter. Truly, they begin with religion, and with that kind of worship which they held to be the best, so much so that those who did not primarily take care of religion were considered by them to be barbaric and foolish lawgivers. We should therefore be quite ashamed that they, in their darkness, saw that which remains too unknown to us in the clear light. Nor should it be considered superstition that they included the preservation of their religion in their laws.

->Vaticanus<-

A little while before this, he rejected the example of Lysias <note>Lysias was a famous ancient Greek orator known for his legal speeches.</note>, calling him an evil predecessor; and now he relies on the example of the unbelievers. Why should we be more bound to follow the unbelievers than Lysias? I do not deny that one may follow them in what is good, but I cannot easily accept that he excludes Lysias from this. Moreover, since those lawgivers established many evil things, I do not wish to follow them, even in things that are thought to be rightly established by them, unless they were proven to me with better reason. For it is very perilous to believe someone by whom one has already been deceived. They established by law that one should not accept any other foreign or new gods; it was for this reason that the philosophers of Athens rejected Christ, and Symmachus <note>Quintus Aurelius Symmachus (c. 340–402 AD) was a Roman statesman who sought to preserve the traditional pagan religions of Rome against the rise of Christianity.</note> advised the Romans to reject Christ because he was a new God. Had our ancestors followed these

<column-break/>

people whom Calvin so highly recommends to us, they would never have accepted Christ. And were we to follow them now, we would have to reject Christ and return once more to philosophy and "empty deceit" <note>original: "ydele wetenschap" – a reference to Colossians 2:8, where Paul warns against being led astray by worldly philosophy.</note> (as Paul calls it), which would mislead us and rob us of Christ. Consequently, if they had established that one should maintain religion with the sword, I would not want to follow them in that, unless better proof could be brought to me. Yet I do not know that they established any such thing. It is true that in their laws, they had their first and most important focus on religion, but not that they should confirm the same through the sword as Calvin desires. As for what Cicero says in the second book of *The Laws* <note>original: "2. Boeck vande wetten" – referring to Cicero’s *De Legibus*.</note>, where he testifies that he shall give the best and most suitable laws for a republic, he begins thus: "Approach the gods with purity, practice piety in religion, but keep riches far from it. Whoever does otherwise, God Himself will avenge it." And a little later, explaining this passage, he lays down that no human, but God Himself, is appointed as a Judge; by which it appears that religion is confirmed through the fear of present punishment <note>Vaticanus argues that Cicero meant divine punishment, not state execution.</note>. And a little before these words, he sets down a law concerning a false oath, as follows: "The punishment for a false oath is, from God’s side, his destruction; from man’s side, his loss." Do you see clearly here? That godlessness and the godless are not subject to the sword: for those lawgivers had two kinds of
