--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue between the reformer John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (the humanist Sebastian Castellio). The previous page discussed whether heresy should be punished like perjury or murder. Here, the debate shifts to the use of Old Testament precedents—specifically the decree of King Nebuchadnezzar—to justify the state's use of the sword in religious matters.</meta>

[header: Dialogue]

### Calvin 116.

<margin>Dan. 3.</margin>
Concerning this, the Spirit of God has left us a beautiful example in Nebuchadnezzar. For Daniel recounts his decree, by which he forbade anyone, upon pain of death, from blaspheming the God of Israel. It is truly no small matter for such a cruel tyrant that God sends him a prophet to record such laws of his and to place them among his prophecies. How is Nebuchadnezzar praised by the Holy Spirit and the prophet, who took it upon himself to protect the honor of the true God with power, because the holy magistrates would otherwise allow the violation of His honor to pass unnoticed? Or rather, is God here prescribing to them, under the persona of a worldly and heathen king, what their office is?

#### Vaticanus.

Since Calvin, just a little before this, rejected the counsel of Gamaliel <note>Gamaliel was a Pharisee and celebrated teacher of the Law who advised the Sanhedrin to be tolerant of the early Christians, suggesting that if their work was of God, it could not be stopped (Acts 5:34-39).</note> with such disgust, how does it happen that he praises Nebuchadnezzar’s counsel so much here? I cannot see what the cause may be, other than that Gamaliel’s counsel is somewhat gentler, but Nebuchadnezzar’s counsel is somewhat harder and more cruel. For Calvin rejects everything that is gentle and embraces everything that is cruel. As for his saying that this decree is praised by Daniel, one could also say that of Gamaliel’s counsel. For Luke recounts the one, just as Daniel recounts the other; neither of them states their opinion on whether it was good or not. But Calvin, a man like Nebuchadnezzar, exalts Nebuchadnezzar’s counsel beyond measure, and says that Gamaliel was blind.

<column-break/>

Yet it seems to me that Gamaliel’s counsel is much gentler and accords better with the New Testament. Nebuchadnezzar relied much on the sword and was a cruel tyrant, as Calvin himself calls him. 
<margin>Dan. 2:13</margin>
And it seems indeed that he had this decree proclaimed with the same impulsiveness <note>The original Dutch *lichtvaerdicheydt* implies a rash, fickle, or unstable character.</note> as when he, a little before, wanted to have all his wise men put to death because they could not interpret his dream. 
<margin>Dan. 2:46</margin>
And just as he had worshipped Daniel and commanded that food offerings and incense be offered to him. If one wants to imitate this as well, then one would also have to make offerings to our Calvin, and not to God alone. 
<margin>Acts 14</margin> <note>original: "Acto. j. 4" likely referring to the events in Lystra described in Acts 14.</note>
Such impulsiveness was also present in those people who first wanted to offer sacrifices to Paul and Barnabas, and shortly thereafter changed so much that they stoned Paul. So also this Nebuchadnezzar: after he had forbidden the blaspheming of the God of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego upon pain of death, he nevertheless did not give that same God His proper honor, as is recounted in the following chapter. Instead, he so prided himself against Him that for that reason he was changed into a beast. <note>A reference to Daniel 4, where the King suffers a period of madness and lives like a wild animal.</note> But even assuming this tyrant’s decree was good and upright—since Calvin wishes to approve the deeds of tyrants—nevertheless it is unlawful to follow such things in the New Covenant. 
<margin>Luke 9:55</margin>
One might give him this answer: 
> You do not know of what spirit you are; the Son of Man did not come to destroy, but to save.

<summary>Vaticanus critiques Calvin for favoring the harsh, impulsive decrees of the tyrant Nebuchadnezzar over the gentle, tolerant advice of Gamaliel. He argues that using Old Testament tyrants as models for Christian governance contradicts the spirit of the New Testament.</summary>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate between Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) and Calvin. On the previous page, Vaticanus criticized Calvin for preferring the harsh decree of the tyrant Nebuchadnezzar over the gentle counsel of Gamaliel. Here, Vaticanus argues that faith cannot be forced by the sword and contrasts different biblical models of leadership.</meta>

->Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

If Christ did not want his Apostles to follow the example of Elijah <note>A reference to Luke 9:54-55, where Jesus rebukes his disciples for wanting to call down fire from heaven like Elijah did.</note>, or for one private person to follow another, or one Prophet to follow another, how much less would he want Christian Magistrates to follow the command of a cruel Tyrant? But even if one granted Calvin that this Tyrant should be followed, what would the result be? That one ought to kill all those who blaspheme the God of Israel; and thus, following this Law, no one would be put to death, because they all acknowledge God. But let us apply this to Christ for a moment. In doing so, one would have to kill all the Jews who blaspheme Christ. That surely does not apply to Heretics <term>Ketters: individuals whose religious beliefs dissent from established church doctrine</term>, since they confess Christ, even though they err. For to err and to blaspheme are not the same thing: by way of example, if someone said that Calvin wore a white garment when he actually wore a red garment, this person would indeed be in error, but would not be blaspheming Calvin. If we want to follow the commands of Kings, let us follow the command of King Cyrus <note>original: "Cores"</note>,
<margin>Ezra 1</margin>
whom Ezra praises much more laudably than Daniel does Nebuchadnezzar. For that happened through the stirring of the Spirit of God, so that he commanded all the Jews who were in his kingdom that they should go up to Jerusalem, and he forced no one. Let us also force no one to godliness <term>Godsalicheydt: a state of living piously or in accordance with God's will</term>, for no one will become good against their will or through compulsion. And those who want to force people to the faith act as if someone wanted to thrust food into a sick person’s mouth

<column-break/>

against his will with a stick. When Holy Scripture speaks of teaching love to other people, it uses the words "advising," "instructing," and "exhorting," etc. But these people do not want to advise with words, but to force with the steel sword <note>original: "stale swaerde"</note>; and from this it comes that they have "steel Christians," just as their Gospel is a "steel Gospel." But let us proceed, and passing by all of Calvin’s exclamations and loud shouting, which we have sufficiently refuted, let us come to the matter itself.

### Calvin 117.

But let us now see what right God has given His Church: for after He commanded that a prophet and dreamer who had disturbed the people
<margin>Deut. 13</margin>
and led them away from the right path of godliness should be killed, it is so that He extends that same punishment over each and every person, with these words: *If your brother, your mother’s son, your daughter, or the wife in your arms, or your friend who is as your own heart, shall secretly persuade you, saying: let us go and serve other gods, whom you do not know, nor your fathers, etc. Do not consent, and do not listen to him; nor shall your eye spare him, and you shall not pity him, nor hide him; but you shall kill <note>original: "worgen", which can mean to strangle or execute.</note> him; your hand shall be the first upon him to kill him, and thereafter the hand of all the people. One shall stone him to death.*

<meta>catchword: Va-</meta>

<summary>Vaticanus argues that faith must be voluntary, comparing Calvin's coercive "steel Gospel" unfavorably to King Cyrus's religious tolerance. Calvin responds by citing Deuteronomy 13 to justify the execution of those who lead others away from the faith.</summary>

# Dialogue

<meta>catchword: Cal-</meta>

->centered text<-
Vaticanus.

This does not concern the **Heretics** <term>Ketters: individuals whose religious beliefs dissent from established church doctrine</term> in the least: For none of them says, "Let us go and serve other Gods"; but what is more, they all help to serve the one God whom we and our forefathers have known. Nor does any of them advise us to serve **Baal**, **Astaroth**, or **Neptune** <note>Baal and Astaroth are ancient deities often cited in the Old Testament as "foreign gods"; Neptune is the Roman god of the sea, here used as a general example of paganism.</note>. If Calvin wants to say that all those who misunderstand and interpret the Holy Scripture poorly serve a foreign God, then he shall speak poorly of it. For the **Sadducees** <note>A Jewish sect at the time of Jesus that denied the resurrection of the dead.</note> also misunderstood it when they denied the resurrection—which error was truly much grosser than the errors in which **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish physician and theologian burned at the stake in Calvin’s Geneva for his views on the Trinity.</note> was stuck. Likewise, when they believed that there would be fleshly marriages in the Kingdom of God; and yet this was never held against them as a capital crime. Nor did Christ ever call them servants of foreign Gods, but said that they erred by not knowing the Scriptures, and he taught them with gentleness. **Thomas** also erred, and that very heavily, when he would not believe that Christ had risen until he had seen him, even though he had heard enough of this before from Christ’s preaching, and the Apostles had collectively testified the same to him. The Apostles themselves erred when they initially thought that the Gentiles should not be called to the grace of the Gospel; which error was indeed so great that it would now exclude us from grace, and yet they were never held to be Idolaters, Godless, or Blasphemers. Finally, Calvin himself says

<column-break/>

that the **Papists** <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics, referring to their allegiance to the Pope.</note> are not only in error and are Heretics, but that they are Godless, Blasphemers, Idolaters, yes, servants of foreign Gods and Antichrists. If it is then the truth that they have a foreign God, then it is also the truth that they **Baptize** their children in the name of a foreign God. And now Calvin himself was baptized by them and holds their baptism to be valid. Therefore, if that baptism is good, then the heretical Papists must have the true God; and so it will happen that one may not count the Heretics among the idolaters. If their Baptism is bad, then Calvin must be **Re-baptized**, and is not yet a Christian. If he understands "foreign gods" to mean sins—such as greed, unchastity, and so forth—which are called gods in the New Testament (for **Paul**
<margin>Philippians 3:19; Colossians 3:5</margin>
says, "whose belly is their God," and calls greed idolatry) and means that those sins were signified by those foreign Gods of the Old Testament, then I say: that these do not concern the **Magistrate** <term>Magistraet: a civil officer or judge responsible for enforcing the law</term>. Instead, everyone must be a Magistrate for himself in this matter, as Christ teaches us:
<margin>Matthew 5:29</margin>
"If your eye, hand, or foot hinders you, cut it off." That is the true "death by stoning" <note>A reference to the Mosaic law where certain sins were punished by the community throwing stones at the offender until they died.</note> which was signified by this Law of Moses. When one has reached that point, then that Law has its end; just as the shadows of the Law have their end when the light has come. If Calvin still wants to follow the Law, then **Servetus** should not have been burned, but should have been stoned by the people; and Calvin, as the most innocent, should have thrown the first stone <note>A reference to Jesus’ words in John 8:7 regarding the woman caught in adultery: "Let him who is without sin among you be the first to throw a stone at her."</note>.

<summary>Vaticanus argues that theological errors do not constitute the worship of "foreign gods" and points out that if the Catholic Church were truly idolatrous, Calvin’s own baptism would be invalid. He advocates for a spiritual interpretation of Mosaic Law, suggesting that "stoning" refers to self-discipline rather than literal execution.</summary>
<keywords>heresy, baptism, Sadducees, Michael Servetus, idolatry, Mosaic Law, John Calvin, Sebastian Castellio, religious tolerance, stoning</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the debate between Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio) and Calvin regarding the treatment of heretics. Vaticanus argues that theological error is not the same as serving "foreign gods" and highlights the logical inconsistencies in Calvin's condemnation of the Catholic Church's baptism.</meta>

### Calvin 118.

Let them now go and accuse God of cruelty, those who think so little of falling away from the pure faith and religion as if it were a trivial and forgivable sin.

### Vaticanus.

<margin>Hebrews 10:26</margin>
Who those people are is unknown to me. As for me, I hold such apostasy <note>original: "afval"; the act of renouncing one's faith.</note> to be the greatest and most deadly sin—and so deadly that it cannot be punished by any human (since no one has power over eternal death), but only by God through eternal fire, as is proven in the letter to the Hebrews: "For if we sin willfully," it says, "after we have received the knowledge of the truth, there remains no more sacrifice for sin: but a certain fearful looking for of judgment and fiery indignation, which shall devour the adversaries." This is an apostasy and sin against the **Holy Spirit**, which not the **Magistrate** <term>Magistraet: the civil authorities or government officials responsible for enforcing laws.</term> but God Himself shall punish. And Calvin, having promised to deal with **Heresy** <term>Ketterie: beliefs or opinions contrary to orthodox religious doctrine.</term>, behaves as if that were the same thing: so that he may thus suppress all those who feel differently than he does (whom he calls Heretics), coming forward with every manner of slander. These are the tactics that the **Pharisees** and **Scribes** <note>In the New Testament, these groups were often depicted as the legalistic opponents of Jesus.</note> employed.

### Calvin 119.

Yet before we proceed further, two things should be noted. For firstly, it is the case that he

<column-break/>

does not so strictly command and confirm all religion, whatever it may be, but only that which He has instituted in His word. Secondly, He commands not to stone the foreign nations, but those who had accepted the teaching of the Law and would fall away from it. Thus, that which holds back many ignorant people is removed—when they fear that one would thereby give the **Papists** <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics, referring to their allegiance to the Pope.</note> a foothold to practice tyranny under that cover. For since God had previously revealed a certain rule of **Godliness** <term>Godsalicheydt: the quality of being pious or devoutly religious.</term> to His people before He ordained any punishment for the apostates, who shall dare to venture to advocate any doctrine with the sword and punishments which he has made up out of his own head? <note>original: "uyt sijn poot gesoghen," literally "sucked out of his paw"; an idiom meaning to fabricate or invent something without basis.</note> From this it appears that we do not give or attribute any wild and blind power to the **Authority** <note>The civil government.</note>, that they should protect and advocate without knowledge or judgment every religion that has once been accepted: but we insist that one must maintain the Law that was once presented by God, so that one may confirm the truth with punishments after it has been found.

### Vaticanus.

All **sects** <note>In this context, the various competing branches of Christianity following the Reformation.</note> defend their religion with God's word and hold it to be certain: Therefore, all sects, should they wish to follow Calvin's rule, will persecute one another. Calvin says that his religion is certain, and other sects say the same of theirs. Calvin says that those other sects err, and they say the same of him. Calvin wants to be Judge, and they do too.

<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Vaticanus argues that while apostasy is a grave sin, it is a spiritual matter for God to judge rather than a crime for the state to punish. Calvin defends the use of the "sword" by civil authorities to protect the specific "true" religion revealed by God, though Vaticanus counters that every sect claims to possess this exclusive truth.</summary>

<keywords>Calvin, Vaticanus, apostasy, heresy, civil magistrate, religious persecution, Hebrews 10:26, Pharisees, Papists, religious authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the heated dialogue between Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The previous page ended with Calvin beginning a point about the gravity of falling away from the "pure faith." Here, Vaticanus challenges Calvin's authority to act as a judge over other sects and warns that his justifications for execution will only empower tyrants and other religious groups to shed even more blood.</meta>

# Dialogue

Who will now be the **Judge**? <term>Richter: a judge or arbiter in matters of law or, in this case, religious truth.</term> Who has appointed Calvin as a **Judge** over other sects? That he alone should be allowed to kill them? With what will he demonstrate that he alone is the wisest? He has the word, one will say: well, and they have the ear. Are the matters so certain? Who is it that is assured of them? Calvin and others also think that they are assured. But why does Calvin write so many books about the truth he has found? How is it that Calvin does not write a single book to prove that murder and adultery are sins? It is because that is a matter about which no one doubts. How is it that there is no one who allows himself to be burned for the claim that everything in **Holy Scripture** is a lie? Because everyone is differently assured of that. How is it that there are people who let themselves be killed for **Anabaptism** <term>wederdoop: the practice of rebaptizing adults, which was a capital offense in many parts of 16th-century Europe.</term> and for such other things, though they still hold much from the **Holy Scripture**? Is it not because there is a dispute over it? Has Calvin now snatched all the truth right off the top <note>original: "op't hooft ghereft"; an idiom suggesting he has claimed the absolute peak of truth for himself.</note>? Why does he not also wait for the others until they also reach that point? How is it that he, by killing them, ensures that they cannot learn? There are twelve hours in a day, and someone can certainly learn at the eleventh hour. What do you know (says Paul) whether you shall not save your wife? Does Paul want one to have good hope for an unbelieving wife, who has not yet been converted through the husband's daily exhortations? Why should one not be able to have such hope for **Heretics**?

->Calvin 120.<-

Therefore the proponents 

<column-break/>

of errors need not flatter themselves <note>original: "Pluymstrijcken"; literally to stroke feathers, meaning to fawn or flatter oneself.</note> so much, those who, in the shedding of innocent blood, grant much to their foolish zeal and ignorance; so that they at least lay the burden of the **Inquisition** and the investigation upon another, so that they might thus (1) according to the verdict and judgment of other people, cruelly put to death the Holy **Martyrs** of Christ.

->**Vaticanus.**<-

With this book, you open the window to them no less than to your own. For as they think they are doing God a service when they kill the godly, and moreover are driven to it by your exhortation (for you have not yet made them realize that they are evil, for which reason they would be bound to stop the persecution, and to think that such a thing pertained only to those of **Geneva**) <note>Vaticanus is arguing that Calvin's logic for killing "heretics" can be used by any group, including his enemies, to justify killing those they disagree with.</note>, so it shall happen that they will be further inflamed to the shedding of blood, just like you, and they will kill everyone who feels differently than they do, just as you also do. So it is that no **Nero**, no **Caracalla**, no **Heliogabalus**, indeed no tyrant in the world, has shed as much blood as you or **Zwingli** <note>Huldrych Zwingli, a leader of the Reformation in Switzerland.</note> with your bloody and bloodthirsty words and writings have shed until now, and will yet shed henceforth, unless God, looking upon the human race, opens the eyes of the **Princes** and **Magistrates** whom you have enchanted and blinded: and they refuse to be at your service in the shedding of blood henceforth. (1) Just as the council of **Geneva**, according to the advice of Calvin and his accomplices and fierce associates...

<summary>Vaticanus argues that Calvin has no unique authority to judge and execute others for theological differences, noting that even "heretics" hold to Scripture and might be converted if given time. He warns that Calvin's justifications for violence give a "window" of excuse to all tyrants and religious rivals to shed blood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate regarding the execution of heretics. Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) is responding to John Calvin's argument that while one should not force faith upon those who have never known it, "apostates" who have turned away from the "true" religion must be punished by the sword. Vaticanus is currently pointing out the inconsistency and danger in Calvin's definition of who deserves death.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

...confessed judgment and put the man to death, though they could judge no better than a blind man can judge colors. <note>original: "blinde vande calueren"; likely a corruption of the Dutch idiom "blinde van de kleuren" (a blind man judging colors), meaning someone judging something they cannot understand.</note>

### Calvin 121.

Furthermore, this also answers the objection of those who ask whether Jews, Turks <note>In the 16th century, "Turks" was a common term for Muslims, specifically those of the Ottoman Empire.</note>, and the like should be forced with the sword to the Christian faith. For God does not command that the sword be drawn against all kinds of people: but He wills that those who are apostates <term>afvallighe: individuals who have abandoned their original religious faith</term> should be punished—those who have fallen away from the true religion and have also sought to lead others to apostasy.

### Vaticanus.

<margin>Psalm 2</margin>
Ask of me (says God the Father to His Son) and I shall give you the heathens for an inheritance, and the ends of the earth for a possession.

<margin>Matthew 28:18</margin>
Likewise the Son says: All power in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Since He then is King of the whole world, then all nations, none excepted, would have to be pursued with the Christians' sword—that is, if Calvin's law were truth. Now it is also true that the Turks and all the people in Asia are apostates, for the Gospel was once taught and believed there; therefore they ought to be persecuted most of all. What shall I say of the Jews, to whom it was prophesied: a Prophet like me, the

<margin>Deuteronomy 18</margin>
<margin>Acts 7</margin>
Lord shall raise up for you, etc. Whosoever will not hear him shall die. And Stephen proves that Christ is this Prophet; since the Jews have rejected this Christ, they are most deserving of punishment

<column-break/>

if Calvin considers heretics <term>Ketters: people whose religious opinions conflict with established church doctrine</term> to be apostates and believes that the Papists <term>Papisten: a derogatory term for Roman Catholics</term> are heretics, then he should first have attacked France, which has fallen away from the Light. If he says that he is not powerful enough for that, then he should be given this answer: "Fear not, little flock; one of you shall chase a hundred, and a hundred shall chase ten thousand." If he still does not dare to act on that footing, then he should at least have taken the Cardinal of Tournon <note>François de Tournon (1489–1562), a powerful French cardinal who was a fierce persecutor of Protestants.</note> prisoner, who had passed through Geneva shortly before Servetus's death (as everyone knew well) in order to burn those good people who were imprisoned in Paris, whom he indeed burned a little while later. Here, here Calvin's cook <note>This may be a satirical remark suggesting that even Calvin's servant would have been enough to challenge the Cardinal, or a reference to someone involved in preparing the "fire" of execution.</note> should have been sent, and Calvin should have accused that executioner: and those people's lives would have been saved. But no, Calvin would rather side with the Papists and join himself with the most powerful.

### Calvin 122.

Whoever still wishes to say that heretics and blasphemers are punished unjustly, willfully and knowingly involves himself in the crime of blasphemy <term>blasphemie: the act of insulting or showing contempt for God</term> or the slandering of God.

### Vaticanus

Since he has not found a single place in the whole of Holy Scripture where it is commanded to kill heretics, he craftily places them alongside blasphemers, so that anyone who would wish to [save] them from death...

<summary>Vaticanus (Castellio) critiques Calvin’s distinction between "unbelievers" and "apostates," arguing that if Calvin’s logic held, he would be obligated to wage war on the entire world, including France. He mocks Calvin for executing the lone refugee Servetus while allowing a powerful persecutor like the Cardinal of Tournon to pass through Geneva unharmed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique by Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) against John Calvin's "Defense of the Orthodox Faith." Castellio is arguing that Calvin uses labels like "heretic" and "Libertine" to unfairly group his opponents together and justify their execution under Mosaic Law. There appears to be a slight gap between the end of the previous page's translation and the start of this OCR text.</meta>

<header>Dialogue</header>

<columns>

...want to protect, he would have to bear the name of being their companion, as if he were someone who wanted to defend blasphemers and slanderers. This is a little trick <note>original: "coustken"; a clever artifice or deceptive tactic.</note> he practiced in a certain book that he wrote against the Anabaptists <term>Wederdooperen: a radical Reformation group that rejected infant baptism</term> and the Libertines, or "free spirits." In that book, in order to further burden the Anabaptists, he joins them with the Libertines, even though those two sects differ as much from each other as Calvin and Servetus do. This little trick he learned from the Jews, who crucified Christ between two murderers. Furthermore, he continues and praises with many words the diligence and zeal one ought to have for godliness. He commands one to set aside all fatherly love, and all the kindness that exists among brothers, kin, and friends; and he does all this so that he might the better bring his enemies to their deaths. For if he were as diligent in keeping the Law as he boasts, he would have begun with his own son <note>This is likely a personal jab at scandals involving Calvin’s stepchildren in Geneva; Castellio argues that if Calvin strictly followed the Law of Moses, he would have to execute his own family for their moral failings.</note> (he will well understand what I mean when he reads this). Why does he begin with heretics, who are mentioned nowhere in the Law? Why does he not begin with the adulterers, who are in Geneva in great numbers, and against whom the Law has ordained a specific punishment? Can he not obtain that from his Magistrate? What kind of Magistrate does he have then? How is it that he more easily obtains a certain judgment from them regarding the uncertain errors of Heretics, than regarding the certain sins of Adulterers? Not that I desire that one should punish adulterers according to the Law of Moses; I would

<column-break/>

rather that other punishments were also softened, rather than hardened and made more severe according to the Law of Moses. I know what kind of people we all are: if we all had to be punished with death—those of us who have deserved death according to the Law of Moses—dear God, who would be free? But since Calvin comes attacking us so cruelly with the Law of Moses, we ought not to overlook his own public failings either, especially since he has a Magistrate who is so obedient to him that through them he can put to death those whom the Law nowhere commands to be killed. Yes, what is more, in Geneva he has counterfeiters and thieves put to death against the Law, whereas the Law does not command that they be punished with death, but rather with a fine and restitution. Yes, dear God, what does this mean—that he alleges and brings forward a Law that commands one to summon and destroy the cities of idolaters, along with all their cattle and household goods? One can clearly observe here that if Calvin could once become powerful enough, he would attack France, and furthermore all other peoples whom he considers idolaters. He would go and destroy all cities and kill all the people, sparing neither women, nor the old, nor the young. All the livestock he would slaughter and kill, and having piled all the houses and household goods in a heap in the middle of the market, he would burn it all—just as he did with Servetus. Look closely at his words; you will find that they lead to this. This is where this "Jew" <note>Castellio uses "Jew" here as a polemic label to accuse Calvin of regressing into a rigid, Old Testament legalism that lacks Christian mercy.</note> seeks to lead us, he who has not [followed] Moses...

</columns>

<summary>Vaticanus accuses Calvin of using deceptive labels to group peaceful dissenters with radicals to justify their execution. He argues that Calvin inconsistently applies the harsh punishments of Mosaic Law to heretics while ignoring its application to moral sins or civil crimes like theft.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between "Vaticanus" (the pseudonym of Sebastian Castellio) and "Calvijn" (John Calvin). Castellio is critiquing Calvin’s "Defensio orthodoxae fidei," specifically Calvin's use of Old Testament Law to justify the execution of heretics. <header>vaticanus and calvin.</header>

...reads [the Law] as a single whole <note>The text suggests Calvin views the Old Testament legal code as a monolithic authority without considering the transition to the New Covenant.</note> and does not understand that Christ is the end of the Law: nor will he give any place to the word that the son shall not bear the guilt of the father. For what evil has the young child deserved if his father is an idolater? Shall we then never—casting the shadows of the Law aside—finally come to the light?

## Calvin 123.

But when the foundations of Religion are overturned, then terrible Blasphemies against God are brought to light, and souls are brought to ruin through godless and pestilential teachings: Finally, when someone seeks to instigate an apostasy from the true God and his pure doctrine, then one must take the ultimate remedy in hand, so that the deadly poison does not creep further.

## Vaticanus.

Calvin describes this monster to us, and far be it from me that I would want to advocate for such a monster; I can well tolerate the execution of those who openly dare to teach that one must turn away from the only God. But I do not believe that those people whom Calvin disagrees with, and whom he labels as heretics, are of that sort. For many Zwinglians <term>Swinglianen: followers of the Swiss Reformer Huldrych Zwingli</term>, or Lutherans, or Anabaptists <term>Doopers: radical reformers who rejected infant baptism</term>, or Papists <term>Papisten: a derogatory term for Roman Catholics</term> certainly differ in weighty

<column-break/>

matters, yet they still serve the one God and wish Him to be served: indeed, I do not believe that even Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish physician and theologian burned at the stake in Geneva for his anti-trinitarian views.</note> (whom Calvin intended to portray here) was such a man. For had he been such a man, he would not have chosen to die, but would rather have recanted everything, so that Servetus’s blasphemies and Calvin’s innocence might have been made clear. Without doubt, there must have been something in Servetus’s books that stuck in Calvin’s throat <note>A Dutch idiom, "in de krop ghesteken," meaning something that caused great offense or was difficult to accept; Castellio suggests Calvin was personally offended by Servetus's critiques.</note>. For he did not burn him out of fear that his errors would spread among the common people, given that Calvin himself holds those errors—which he considers the most terrifying—right under the nose of the whole world in this very book. My view is that Servetus brought many of Calvin’s own errors to light in that book, as is clearly enough indicated in this book by Calvin, Page 223, where Servetus speaks thus: "Oh, if I were only free to compile a register of your errors!" Therefore Calvin, fearing that he might be exposed, burned the man along with his book. But God watches him, before whose eyes he shows no reverence; and all things shall come to light in due time. Calvin will not be able to execute so many people that there will not be some left to see Calvin’s errors and bring them to light.

How he has now sought him all the more diligently and for longer

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: how</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Castellio (as Vaticanus) accuses Calvin of executing Michael Servetus not out of religious duty, but to suppress Servetus's pointed critiques of Calvin's own theological errors. Castellio argues that true heretics who abandon God are rare, and that the various Christian sects (Lutherans, Zwinglians, etc.) all aim to serve the same God despite their differences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between Sebastian Castellio (writing as Vaticanus) and John Calvin regarding the execution of heretics. Castellio is responding to Calvin’s use of Old Testament precedents—specifically the actions of Moses and the Levites—to justify state-sponsored violence in matters of faith.</meta>

->Dialogue<-

to consider how he will be hated all the more hereafter by those whom he has deceived.

### Calvin 124.

One sees how gentle a judge Moses was in punishing other crimes; but in avenging and protecting Religion, there he burns entirely. "Sanctify your hands to the Lord," he says: let no one spare his brother or kinsman: so that whoever you encounter, you shall kill them without respect of persons. <note>original: "aensien," referring to partiality or favoritism</note> Or should one think that he, being so suddenly ignited with a frenzy, would have forgotten his usual meekness, that he commanded the Holy Priests to shed blood in such a way?

### Vaticanus.

This passage flatly contradicts Calvin: for those were Levites <term>Leviten: the tribe of Israel set apart for priestly service</term>, and we have no other Levite than Christ, who is a Priest after the order of Melchizedek: and all Christians, who are a holy priesthood. If one must follow the deed of the Levites, then one ought to have followed Christ, who has come in their place and also followed their example. For just as those carnal priests, in a carnal manner, did not spare their brothers and kinsmen: so our spiritual Priest did not spare his Brothers and Kinsmen. "Who is my mother," he says, "and who are my brothers?" <note>A reference to Matthew 12:48/Mark 3:33</note> "Get behind me, Satan," he says against Peter. Likewise, whoever does not forsake father and mother, etc., and also himself, cannot be my disciple. This is the spiritual killing of kinsmen, which was signified by that deed of the Levites,

<column-break/>

and made known to us through this our eternal Levite <note>Christ</note>: whom if we wish to follow, then it is necessary that we let those Figures and shadows <term>Figuren ende schaduwen: a common Reformation term for Old Testament types that are fulfilled or replaced by Christ</term> vanish: for if we wish to keep those figures, then we shall be nothing other than pure Jews, and have fallen from grace, because we wish to stand under the law: for these two cannot be held at the same time.

### Calvin 125.

Should there be anyone who says that this manner of doing cannot be well tolerated in the kingdom of Christ and in these present times, because nowhere in the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"</note> is such a command given: then I ask them whether the Magistrate <term>Magistraet: civil authorities or government officials</term> shall therefore not be allowed to carry out their office? Since neither Christ nor his Apostles anywhere command in plain words with what punishment adulterers and thieves ought to be punished? If one gives me the answer that the criminals shall be punished with such punishments as the Law commands, because the Laws of the Polity <note>original: "Wetten der Politie," referring to civil law</note> have not been abolished by the coming of Christ: then I shall gladly accept this answer. (1) I shall not bring up here that in those days there were no Magistrates to whom the Apostles would have commanded what was becoming. But with this alone I hold myself satisfied: namely, that through the coming of Christ the civil ordinances have not been changed, nor has anything been subtracted or taken away from the office of the Authority. Furthermore, that which Paul teaches—that the sword is not borne by them in vain—must be understood as applying to more than just one kind of crime.

<summary>Castellio argues that the Old Testament violence of the Levites was a "shadow" fulfilled spiritually by Christ, meaning Christians should not literally kill; Calvin responds by arguing that civil laws and the state's right to use the "sword" remain unchanged by the New Testament.</summary>
<keywords>Mosaic Law, Levites, Priesthood of all believers, Melchizedek, civil magistrate, religious violence, New Covenant, shadows and figures</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemic against John Calvin regarding the persecution of heretics. The author, writing under the pseudonym "Vaticanus" (likely Sebastian Castellio), challenges Calvin's use of Mosaic Law to justify capital punishment for religious dissent.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

## Vaticanus

Even if we were to grant Calvin all of this, and concede to him that the entire Law of Moses must still be observed today, he still would not have gained a single hair's width. He said that he would prove that one must coerce heretics with the sword, yet nowhere does he even define what a heretic is; which is a great error, not to define the matter (especially one of such great controversy) when one takes it up for dispute, since a dispute should surely begin with a definition of the matter to be disputed. But he did this out of cunning. For had he defined what a heretic was, he would have found nowhere a single Law stating that such a person must be put to death. Therefore, he preferred to jumble things together and count heretics among blasphemers, idolaters, and apostates, so that, by grouping them together, he might execute them all at once, whereas individually he could not have touched their lives. But this deception cannot satisfy us. Let Calvin bring forward a Law that commands the killing of heretics; but what could he do? There is none. There are indeed laws against apostates and blasphemers. But what does that matter here? For the dispute is about heretics. Show us a single Law regarding heretics; or are heretics blasphemers? Define for us

<column-break/>

once and for all what a heretic is, and do so from the Holy Scripture, so that we may know for certain whether a heretic is also a blasphemer. For who will agree with you that the people considered heretics nowadays are blasphemers and ungodly men? Who would be so foolish as to agree with the Pope that the Lutherans, Zwinglians <note>followers of Huldrych Zwingli</note>, and Calvinists are blasphemers because they differ from him on the matter of the Lord’s Supper <note>the Eucharist</note>? Whom he nevertheless condemns just as strongly as they condemn the Pope. Who will agree with Zwingli that he considers the Anabaptists <note>original: "wederdooperen"</note> to be blasphemers, even though they only differ from him on the matter of Baptism? Who will allow Calvin that the Zwinglians and Lutherans are blasphemers, whom he condemns in his book over the matter of the Lord’s Supper? Likewise, Luther, Brenz <note>Johannes Brentius</note>, and all others who differ from him on the killing of heretics? Which is perhaps the greatest dispute of all, since it concerns the lives of men for whom Christ died. It is not the same thing to blaspheme and to err <term>to err (dolen): to be mistaken in doctrine without malicious intent</term>. One must make a clear distinction here, especially when lives are at stake <note>literally: "when necks hang on it"</note>, so that we do not so carelessly kill men for a crime that is unknown and nowhere named in the Law. I deny that it is commanded anywhere in the Law that those who err and are heretics should be killed; and therefore it is certain that Calvin is mocking us, in that he wants to point to a punishment in the Law that is nowhere to be found. Now he turns to Christ, to prove that the Law still barks <note>original: "blaft" — meaning the Law still has authority or "teeth"</note> under Christ, and that the same punishment in religious matters...

<summary>In this passage, "Vaticanus" (Castellio) accuses Calvin of intentionally failing to define "heresy" so he can conflate it with blasphemy and justify executions. The author argues that theological disagreement is "error" rather than "blasphemy" and should not be a capital offense.</summary>

to the spirit. That Paul says that the Law is spiritual argues very well with my view: for the meaning of the Law is spiritual, but the outward workings are fleshly. But of these circumcisions the Israelites did not have much understanding, but remained hanging onto the fleshly circumcision which is done with hands, which Paul makes known when he says that they have a veil over their heart when Moses is read <margin>2 Corinthians 3:15/13</margin>, and that they cannot see the end of the Law which must perish. But for those who have come to Christ, the veil is taken away from them and brought to nothing through Christ: and they have the spirit of the Law, which is the circumcision of the heart. For it is to this that the Law sends people, yet it does not lead them there, but remains standing before the Jordan <note>A metaphor for the Mosaic Law: just as Moses could see the Promised Land but died before crossing the Jordan River, the Law can point to salvation but cannot provide it.</note>. Since the Law consists of ceremonial and judicial or civil laws (for we do not speak of the moral law here), both of these have been changed. That the civil laws have been changed is evident from the fact that the sins, for the punishment of which those sentences were established, have changed. It was a sin for one to work on the Sabbath day, and for this a punishment was ordained—namely, physical death. Thus, the judgments of the Magistrates <term>Magistraten: civil authorities or government officials</term> have no place here, as they did in the Law of Moses: for they cannot touch the heart of the uncircumcised as they could the bodies.
<margin>Deuteronomy 18</margin>

people, shall he be deemed worthy of, who has trampled the Son of God underfoot, and has regarded the blood of the Covenant—by which he was sanctified—as an unholy thing, and has insulted the Spirit of grace? Here one clearly sees that the punishment ordained for the unbelieving and apostate <term>afvallighe: those who have turned away from their faith</term> is not commanded to the Magistrates, but is attributed to the living God. If this punishment were commanded to the Authorities, then the Jews should first of all be punished according to this Law, because they would not listen to the Prophet Christ whom God had provided for them; yet Calvin has said (above) <note>Vaticanus points out a perceived inconsistency in Calvin's logic: if Mosaic law still required the death of "false prophets," Calvin would be forced to argue for the execution of all Jews who reject Christ, which he did not do.</note> that his Magistrate may not touch them. Therefore, just as the believer in Christ is promised not temporal but eternal life, so also a death that is not temporal but eternal hangs over the neck of the unbeliever. And just as one must learn and read from the Law of Moses how to punish those who sinned against it, so too must one learn and observe from Christ's Law how those should be punished who have sinned against Christ: in which it is written: "he who does not believe shall be damned," <note>original Dutch: "verdoemt worden"; in this context, it refers specifically to divine condemnation rather than civil sentencing.</note> that is, he shall be punished by God, and not by men.

Here someone might ask / whether it would not be permitted for the Magistrate to use the Political Law of Moses? <note>The "Political Law" refers to the specific judicial and civil statutes given to the ancient Israelites, as opposed to the moral Law (the Ten Commandments).</note> To which I answer / that it is not only permitted / but that it is also much better that they use the same / than any other / yet in such a way that he does not use the same / because Moses commanded such / but because he finds that such is equitable. <note>original: "billic" — meaning fair, just, or reasonable according to natural law.</note> As for example: Moses commanded that when people build houses / they should finish the roof all around with a parapet <note>original: "schutsel" — referring to Deuteronomy 22:8, which required a guardrail on flat roofs to prevent accidents.</note> so that no one should fall off. So I understand it also regarding the Magistrate: if he punishes the criminal with a punishment that Moses ordained for it / he will do much better / than if he followed other Laws in that: since the Law of Moses, having been given by God, is the most righteous: and yet such a Magistrate shall not be subject to the Law of Moses:

<column-break/>

Yet I wish that such would happen without superstition / and that one would lean more toward mercy than toward severity / so that one, being sometimes driven too much by the zeal of Moses / does not sin against Christ's mercy. For that reason, the Law of "eye for eye / tooth for tooth" / is now nowhere maintained: Yes, not even in Geneva / where there is nevertheless a Calvinist Magistrate / and that does not displease me. <note>Geneva was the center of John Calvin's Reformation, known for its strict moral and civil discipline.</note> If I were a Magistrate / and had to judge that Adulteress / whom Christ wonderfully delivered and preserved: <note>Refers to the woman caught in adultery in John 8:1–11.</note> or of that harlot who anointed Christ's feet / I would not dare condemn them. This same I say also of the murderer who, hanging on the Cross, believed in Christ: and of the slave Onesimus: for whom Paul wrote to Philemon: and of David / when he had committed adultery and murders. There is a great difference between / forgiving a God-fearing person / who has sinned through ignorance or carelessness / or who has converted and improved himself / his crime / than if one did such to a totally abandoned, godless person. <meta>This page continues the argument that civil authorities (Magistrates) have a limited jurisdiction. # Vaticanus and Calvin. #

...would have what was right and equitable. I cannot see where that is heading: for they did not need to refrain from prescribing such things simply because there was no Christian Magistrate <note>The "Magistrate" refers to the civil government or secular authorities.</note> at that time: for since they prescribed such rules by which we should later regulate our lives, they must also have had regard for the future time, if indeed they knew that a Christian authority would come later: just as Moses, when he gave Laws to the Israelites and forbade them to make a King; nevertheless, as he knew beforehand that they would make a King against the Law, he described the right of Kings for that future time. And so it seems that Paul also does the same when he writes: the Rulers are not to be feared by those who do good, but by those who do evil. 
<margin>Rom. 13</margin>
Do you wish not to fear the power? Then do what is good, and you shall have praise from her. For it is God’s Servant <note>original: "dienerffe" — a variant of servant or minister.</note> to you for good: but if you do what is evil, then fear: for she does not bear the sword in vain. For it is God’s servant and an avenger for punishment to those who do evil. Whoever looks closely into these words shall find that the Magistrate’s Office is here prescribed silently and covertly—namely, that he must punish external crimes, and not internal ones, such as hatred, hy-

<column-break/>

-pocrisy, pride, and so forth, which he may not touch with the sword, even though they are greater than external crimes. Likewise, he may not touch Religion: For since Paul speaks of the Magistrate that then was, if he had allowed them to kill people for matters of Religion, Paul himself would first have had to be punished, because he taught a Religion for which all the Magistrates, both Jewish and Heathen, had an aversion. Therefore, when he says, "do you wish not to fear the power? Then do what is good," it is as if he were saying: I speak of such a Magistrate who punishes the Criminal, and not those who do good. For as regarding those Magistrates who persecute us Christians—who do good and are not criminals—for matters of Religion, they are not Servants of God: seeing that God has commanded His servants to protect those who do good, and not to persecute them. But those are God’s servants who protect the godly and good from all injustice. For this reason they must punish Crimes, but Religion they may not touch (for otherwise they would do nothing but turn things upside down) but they must leave the same to us (who are defenders of Religion) to be handled as Christ (who is the Father of Religion) has commanded us. Paul teaches this somewhat obscurely <note>original: "duysterlijck" — meaning unclearly, perhaps to avoid immediate political repercussions.</note>, because of the Tyranny that then had the upper hand.

<meta>This page continues the debate regarding the jurisdiction of civil authorities (magistrates) over religious matters. The previous page established that while the magistrate punishes external crimes with the "sword," they lack authority over internal spiritual states like hypocrisy or pride. Here, Calvin argues that kings are divinely appointed to protect the "pure doctrine," while Vaticanus counters using biblical prophecy to show that the Kingdom of Christ is established through peace and spiritual power, not state coercion.</meta>

->Dialogue<-

### Calvin 126.

Those people whom I am now addressing certainly acknowledge that the Judges <note>The "Judges" or "Magistrates" refers to the civil government.</note> are commanded by God to punish other crimes, but they claim that they may not lay their hands on religious matters (1), so that by remaining silent, they allow godlessness to go its way freely and openly.

### Vaticanus.

<margin>(1) 2 Cor. 10</margin>
(1) No, but rather so that godlessness might be restrained by those weapons that are spiritually powerful before God, as Paul says, through which all thoughts are taken captive to the obedience of Christ, and through which one has prepared vengeance against all disobedience. <note>Vaticanus argues that religious error should be fought with spiritual arguments and scripture rather than physical force or "the sword."</note>

### Calvin 127.

But the Holy Spirit speaks against this in countless places, saying that the Kings under the Kingdom of Christ shall be advocates and protectors of the pure Doctrine. When David exhorts all the Princes to kiss the Son <note>A reference to Psalm 2:12, interpreted here as a command for rulers to submit to and enforce Christ's authority.</note>, what else does he mean by that, than that they, through their leadership and help, must protect the pure doctrine? When it is so often stated that the time will come that they shall bring tribute to Christ, what else is understood by that, than that they shall become protectors of the pure calling and guardians of the Godly Doctrine? How shall that prophecy of Isaiah be fulfilled, <margin>Isaiah 49[:23].</margin> that they shall become nursing fathers <note>original: "voester-Heeren" — a traditional term for kings who nurture and protect the church.</note> of the Church, unless they apply their power to uphold Godliness?

<column-break/>

### Vaticanus

Nothing clearer could have been brought against Calvin. For Isaiah speaks in this place, which was brought up here by Calvin, as follows:

> The Kings shall be your nursing fathers, and the Princes your nursing mothers.

And then it follows:

> I will make peace your administration, and righteousness your overseers. In your land no cruelty shall be heard, nor destruction or misery within your borders, and you shall call your walls Salvation and your gates Praise.

And in chapter 63, Christ speaks thus:

> I tread the winepress alone, and there is no one from the people with me: I have threshed in my anger, and trodden in my fury: therefore their strength is sprinkled upon my garments, and I have stained all my raiment; for I looked around me, and there was no helper, and I was appalled, and no one sustained me: But my own arm had to help me, and my anger sustained me. Therefore I have trodden down the people in my anger, and made them drunk in my fury, and brought down their strength to the earth. <note>Vaticanus uses this to show that Christ executes judgment himself, spiritually or at the end of time, rather than delegating the "threshing" of people to earthly kings.</note>

And Chapter 65:25:

> The wolf and the lamb shall feed together, the lion shall eat straw like an ox, and the serpent shall eat dust; they shall not hurt or destroy on all my holy mountain.

And Micah 4:3:

> They shall beat their swords into plowshares, and their spears into sickles: no nation shall lift up a sword against another, and they shall learn war no more.

<meta>catchword: These</meta>

<summary>Calvin argues that the Bible mandates civil rulers to protect religious purity, while Vaticanus cites the same prophets to argue that Christ's kingdom is characterized by peace and the absence of state violence.</summary>

<meta>The previous page discussed the nature of the true Church and likely began the critique of institutional power. This page continues the argument by contrasting the spiritual victory of Christ with John Calvin’s views on secular authority.</meta>

### Vaticanus and Calvin.

These and countless other things have been written concerning the future flourishing and reigning Church; a time which Calvin, in bringing it up, flatly contradicts himself. For if there is to be righteousness for the authorities—their earthly power having been cast down and all the power of Christ having brought it to nothing—and the swords having been changed into plowshares <note>A reference to the prophecy in Isaiah 2:4 where weapons are converted into agricultural tools, symbolizing universal peace.</note>, what swords shall the authorities then use to protect the truth, which no one shall be able to oppose? But you should understand well that Christ's victory will proceed in a completely different way than Calvin intends. Christ shall fight with his word alone, of which it is written <margin>Isaiah 49:2</margin> in Isaiah 49:2: "He has made my mouth like a sharp sword." Also: "the Lord has given me a sharpened tongue." It is far from the case that the authorities should help this sharp and fiery word with their power; instead, all the Heathen <note>original: "Heydenen", referring to non-believers or nations outside the faith.</note> would set themselves against it and be overcome, and thus, being brought under subjection, would become a footstool for the feet <margin>Isaiah 60</margin> of Christ, and from that time forward would serve him and his Church for all time. In such a way, whatever Heathens and Kingdoms will not serve him would be destroyed and laid waste. Of this victory David speaks thus: "the saints shall be joyful and <margin>Psalm 149</margin> shall praise and boast in their camps; their mouth shall exalt God, and they shall have sharp swords in their hands to exercise vengeance among the Heathen, punishment among the peoples, to bind their Kings with chains, and their 

<column-break/>

Nobles with iron fetters: that they may do to them the judgment that is written; such honor shall all his saints have." Here one sees indeed that they shall combat the Kings with the sword of the Church, but not protect them; and that the Church shall so overcome them with the word and the Spirit of the mouth of the Lord that they shall fall at his feet and kiss his footsteps. Therefore, when it is written that the Kings shall be the "nursing fathers" <note>original: "voester Heeren". This refers to Isaiah 49:23, a verse often used by Reformers to justify the state’s role in enforcing religious orthodoxy.</note> of the Church, that must be understood in this sense: that those who previously held dominion shall afterwards become servants, so that they shall be compelled to feed the Church not with swords, but with their service. For the entire state of things shall be turned upside down, so that those who held dominion shall then serve those over whom they previously reigned; and those who have been captives shall have dominion over those whom they served. For he shall pull down the mighty from their <margin>Luke 1:52</margin> thrones and exalt the lowly upon them: he shall fill the hungry with good things and send the rich away empty. <margin>Isaiah 26</margin> Then we shall have a strong city whose walls and bulwarks shall be salvation, when the Lord shall strike the earth with the rod of his mouth, which shall be Christ’s iron scepter. O all you who wait for the Lord, long for this time, and do not wish to be a hindrance or in the way—neither for us nor for Him—when he shall come to bring and adorn that glorious city of Jerusalem to us upon the earth. Calvin, so that no one...

# Dialogue

(perhaps be deceived) far from it that he should think this time is already present (although he nonetheless arms his magistrate with the sword for the upbringing of his Church, as if those times had already come). He believes quite surely that those times shall never come upon the earth; for as we have proved above from his *Institutes* <note>Referring to Calvin's seminal work, *Institutes of the Christian Religion*.</note>, he nourishes a Church in which many Hypocrites and ungodly are found. And writing in his commentaries on the Prophet Isaiah about this same time of the reigning Church of which we now speak, he says that they rave beyond measure who believe that those things shall happen in this life; and that we have nothing here but a taste of that blessedness. And again in his letter concerning his Catechism, he writes thus: "Unless God comes to our aid in a miraculous way from Heaven, I see the uttermost destruction hanging over our heads." And a little after: "And oh! if God granted that our children did not realize that this word was much more a prophecy than a guess <margin><unclear>v. 1. 2</unclear></margin> had been." Therefore, we ought all the more to labor to bind together the remaining pieces and fragments of the church, of whatever kind they might be, which shall remain after our death or shall sprout forth from our writings. Since he then expects the uttermost destruction, it is clear that he does not arm the Kings here for the upbringing and protection of the church because he hopes they shall become inclined to it, but he arms himself with their help without

<column-break/>

which he sees that his word cannot do many miracles—because he would suppress his adversaries and opponents with the sword (for he cannot establish his cause with his word alone).

->Calvin 128.<-

<margin>1 Timothy 2</margin>
Hear also what Paul says, in order to clear away all obstacles. He commands that in the congregation, prayers should usually be made for Kings and all who are in high positions. To what end? So that we may lead a quiet and peaceful life. Is that enough? No; but in all Godliness and honesty. Had Paul only spoken here of Godliness and honesty, then it would seem that those people had some right who do not wish to allow that Princes lay their hand on religious matters. But since he now lays this office upon them—that they must ensure that God be well served—it is indeed a great presumption to wish to rob and deprive them of the power given to them by God. Two things are well to be noted here; for Paul does not teach here private persons that they give themselves to the service of God, but he deals here with the common office of Princes, insofar as they are placed upon the judgment seat and have received the sword to govern the human race. He does not see that their office consists only in standing up for each person's legal rights, but he teaches that they are ordained to this end: to promote Godliness.

<meta>This page continues the debate between the figure "Vaticanus" (a pseudonym often associated with the humanist Sebastian Castellio) and the reformer John Calvin. The previous page ended with Vaticanus arguing that Calvin’s reliance on the state's "sword" to protect the church contradicts the spiritual nature of Christ's kingdom.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin

### Vaticanus
A little before this, Calvin said that there were no Magistrates to whom the Apostles would have commanded that which was right and fair; but now, contradicting himself, he says that Paul describes their ministry here, and that it is divided into two parts. This is untrue, for Paul says the following: "I exhort therefore, that, first of all, supplications, prayers, intercessions, and giving of thanks, be made for all men; for kings, and for all that are in authority; that we may lead a quiet and peaceable life in all godliness and honesty." <note>Vaticanus is quoting 1 Timothy 2:1-2 to argue that prayer for rulers is intended to secure peace, not to authorize state-sponsored religious violence.</note> After that, he points out to what end he says this: for that is good and acceptable before God our Savior (he says) who desires all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth. It is as if he wanted to say: one must pray to God for all men, that He might open their understanding to come to the knowledge of the truth. If that were to happen, we would serve God in peace. But since the Kings prevent this—because they persecute us for our religion and thus hinder our tranquility—one must pray to God that He also opens their understanding (since He wants them also to be saved, just as He wants all others to be saved). This is not so they may protect Religion with the sword, but so that they might finally stop attacking Religion with the sword. For since all the Magistrates and Princes persecute the Godly—"for you shall be brought before governors and kings for my sake," says Christ—
<margin>Matthew 10</margin>
if they were once to stop their persecution, then we would

<column-break/>

lead a quiet life in all godliness. For no one will be able to hinder our peace on account of evil deeds, provided we refrain from committing them. For godliness shall protect itself well enough, as long as our bodies are not physically attacked. Thus, Paul does not intend here for Princes to uphold sound doctrine with the sword. (Indeed, against whom else would they need to defend it but themselves, since they are the only ones who fight against it with the sword?) Rather, he wants them to accept the truth and stop fighting against it. 

It is just as if merchants wished that the robbers and highwaymen would become good, orderly people, so that the merchants might carry out their trade in greater peace. This is not because those robbers and highwaymen would *provide* that peace to them, but simply because they would stop blocking and hindering it. For truly, the Princes in the time of Paul were nothing but murderers and wolves against the Christians, just as Christ intended to make known when He said, 
<margin>Matthew 10:16</margin>
"Behold, I send you as sheep in the midst of wolves." Paul also indicates this when he says that he was delivered "out of the mouth of the lion." <note>A reference to 2 Timothy 4:17, where the "lion" symbolizes the Roman Emperor or the threat of state execution.</note>

### Calvin 129.
From which it shall then follow, that the sword is given to them to defend sound doctrine (1); <note>Calvin argues that the state has a positive duty to use force to protect correct religious teaching.</note> which they cannot do if one is not permitted to keep the ungodly falsifications <note>original: "vervalschingen," meaning corruptions or distortions of religious truth.</note> in check through punishment. Let the unlearned and thoughtless people then no longer say that one may not punish the falsifiers of doctrine, unless they wish to set themselves against God.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between "Vaticanus" (a pseudonym for the humanist scholar Sebastian Castellio) and the ideology of the Genevan reformers, specifically John Calvin. The previous page discussed the role of the Magistrate and the use of the sword in matters of faith. Here, Vaticanus argues that the Reformers have redefined "sound doctrine" to mean theological agreement rather than moral character.</meta>

# Dialogue

<margin>1 Timothy 1:10</margin>
**Vaticanus.**
(1) Paul calls that which makes people healthy "sound doctrine" <note>original: "ghesont Leere"; the Greek term *hugiainousē didaskalia* implies teaching that promotes spiritual health</note>—that is, being endowed with love, sincere faith, and a good conscience. On the other hand, he calls that "unsoundness" which makes people officious, contentious, rebellious, godless, irreligious, unholy, murderers of fathers, and whatever else is contrary to sound doctrine. But these people <note>The established Reformers, such as Calvin and his followers</note> have a completely different scent in their noses than Paul did. For they consider those to be "sound" who agree with them regarding Baptism, the Lord's Supper, Predestination, etc. Such people—even if they are clearly envious, greedy, backbiting, hypocritical, lying, mockers, <term>usurers</term> <note>original: "Woerkenaers," referring to those who charge excessive interest</note>, and whatever else is against sound doctrine—are nevertheless easily tolerated. No one is put to death for such flaws, unless they have simultaneously committed murder, or theft, or similar gruesome acts, or if they are on bad terms with the Preachers. For to offend the Preachers is considered by them to be as great a sin as the sin against the <term>Holy Spirit</term>, as the proverb now goes in some places. But whoever feels differently than they do about Baptism, the Lord's Supper, Justification, faith, etc., that person is a <term>Heretic</term>, even a Devil, and may be given neither house nor home by anyone. Instead, they must be rejected by everyone as a sworn hereditary enemy of the church and a hideous falsifier of sound doctrine—even if they are otherwise perfectly healthy and upright of life, indeed, so kind-

<column-break/>

hearted, patient, charitable, merciful, generous, devout, and God-fearing, that neither friends nor enemies could find anything to rebuke in their conduct. All these virtues and the uprightness of life (which Paul did not hesitate to praise in himself) cannot help a man with these people; he must be regarded as a godless rogue and a blasphemer as long as he does not completely agree with their opinion in everything, even the smallest points. This is proven not only by the previously mentioned case of the wood-carrier, but by many other examples.

For in Zurich, Switzerland, through the counsel and actions of <term>Zwingli</term> <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the leader of the Reformation in Switzerland</note>, many people who were otherwise pious and blameless of life were put to death solely for their views on Baptism. Among others, two men born in that same city—specifically, a father and his son—whom everyone who knew them (even Zwingli's own friends) acknowledged to be so godly that few like them were to be found in the Church. Others, conversely, who had previously lived blameless lives, after they were forced to renounce their faith, led such immoral lives that they had no equal in all kinds of wickedness; yet, for this godless life, they were persecuted by no one. This first type of persecution seems to have already been in fashion during <margin>Daniel 6:5</margin> Daniel's time; for when his enemies knew nothing to say against his conduct, they decided to catch him in his religion. These people do exactly the same, and...

<summary>Vaticanus argues that the Reformers prioritize theological conformity over moral character, noting that while immoral people are tolerated if they agree on doctrine, pious individuals like the Anabaptists in Zurich were executed for their differing views.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue written by Sebastian Castellio (under the name Vaticanus), challenging John Calvin's justification for the capital punishment of heretics following the 1553 execution of Michael Servetus in Geneva. Castellio argues that Calvin relies on emotional rhetoric and false analogies rather than clear Biblical law.</meta>

### Vaticanus and Calvin.

...attacking the religion of those whose lives they cannot rebuke. In this, they truly act both cautiously and craftily; for since they themselves do not excel in the propriety of life, they turn to doctrine. Because the <term>Magistrate</term> <note>The civil government or city officials</note>—by their own admission—cannot judge doctrine (just as he is very well able to judge morals), they deceive him all the more easily. Thus, they oppress the innocent under the cloak of their beautifully adorned "sound doctrine." 

But Christ, on the other hand, will reject their doctrine on the day of judgment and demand an accounting of their lives; for when they shall say, "We have 

<margin>Luke 13</margin>

eaten and drunk with you, and you have taught in our streets," he will answer: "Depart from me, all you evildoers." Here you see clearly that one boasts in vain of doctrine unless the life corresponds with it, even if Christ himself had taught in our streets. Nevertheless, we boast of nothing so much as the Word and the <term>Sacraments</term>. 

O blind blindness of the blind! O completely incurable and cruel hypocrisy! When will you finally be exposed, so that the <term>Authority</term> <note>original: "Overheyt"; the governing power</note> may henceforth cease to shed, at your simple whim, the blood of those whom they do not even know?

Up to this point, Calvin has scraped together everything he could to justify shedding the blood of Heretics, without having been able to find a single passage of Scripture that commands Heretics to be killed. Therefore, to better deceive the reader, he nowhere describes what it means to be a "Heretic," but...

<column-break/>

...having promised to dispute the matter of Heretics, he muddles together a great heap of false Prophets, Idolaters, the godless, and blasphemers. He concludes finally that because such people ought to be put to death, Heretics therefore also ought to be put to death. 

A "beautiful" conclusion, in truth! It is just as if someone, having proven that Arsonists, Murderers, and Manslayers must be punished with death, concluded from this that Drunkards must also be punished with death—without, however, bringing forward a single law that commanded drunkards to be killed. Instead, he merely tries to prove with a heap of confused sophistries and <term>blue flowers</term> <note>original: "blauwe bloemen"; a Dutch idiom for beautiful but deceptive lies or flattery</note> that drunkards are no less harmful than those others, and therefore ought to be <term>drenched in the same sauce</term> <note>original: "met een sop overgoten"; an idiom meaning to be treated in the same manner or lumped together</note>. 

Shall we then so immediately let ourselves be convinced by a collection of deceptive and whitewashed arguments and shed human blood? Are the judges not charged to always have the Law before their eyes? Show me a single Law that commands Heretics to be killed. There is not one to be found. 

"Yes," they say, "but one finds a law concerning false Prophets." 

What have I to do with false Prophets? (a good and God-fearing judge will answer). You accuse a Heretic; bring forth a Law that commands Heretics to be killed. 

"Yes, but Heretics are false Prophets." 

Prove that, says the Judge; I do not believe you simply on your word. For Heretics do not command anyone to honor strange Gods, nor do they predict any future...

<summary>Castellio argues that Calvin's call to execute heretics will lead to a bloody cycle of mutual slaughter among different Christian sects, as each group considers the others heretical. He cites Calvin's own definition of heresy and schism to show how easily any dissenting group could be targeted for execution.</summary>
