--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue, where Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) critiques Calvin’s views on heresy. The previous page ended with a warning that if Calvin’s rules were followed, all denominations (Lutherans, Papists, etc.) would end up executing each other.</meta>

Now indeed these Churches must be held together by two bonds: namely, by agreement in sound doctrine, and by brotherly love. Since there is a very great dispute between those Churches—which Calvin alone considers to be Churches—not only in doctrine but also in spirit (which can easily be proven), and since all they (according to Calvin’s view) who break these bonds are to be held as Heretics: thus the Calvinists must necessarily fall upon the others, and the others in turn upon the Calvinists, and holding each other for Heretics, bite for so long until they have consumed one another. And if people want to follow Calvin’s advice, there will not be a single Sect that will not condemn and persecute all the others (for every Sect thinks itself to be the best), so that in this way the Ammonites, Moabites, and Edomites <note>original: "Esabiten", referring to the descendants of Esau</note> bring each other down, and that the true Jews then come along and, without a blow or a stroke, divide the others' plunder among themselves. Since we have now sufficiently refuted Calvin’s saying, it remains for us to point out (which he has not done):

## What a Heretic is, and how
## he ought to be treated.

It often happens that words from other languages, when not understood, give birth to great and dangerous errors. We see this in the name *Ecclesia* <note>original: "Ecclesia," Greek for "assembly"</note>, Church, which word—although it signifies a community

<column-break/>

or assembly—has nonetheless been so far corrupted <note>literally "betrayed"</note> that in common speech it is taken for a Temple or house of prayer. I say the same of the word *Idolum* <note>original: "Idolum," Latin/Greek for "image"</note>, which signifies an image; and yet those who now possess images think that they are free and pure from "Idols." But if the thing were expressed in each people’s own language, these errors would find no place. It is the same with the name *Haereticus* <note>original: "Haereticus"</note> or Heretic, which is Greek; and whenever it is named by those who are inexperienced in the Greek tongue, they immediately think that a Heretic and a Blasphemer, or a sorcerer, or a godless person, or an idolater, or similar terrifying monster, are all one and the same; and they would more easily forgive murderers, traitors, and killers, than a person whom one calls a Heretic. This error of the common people is well known to the firebrands <note>provocateurs or agitators</note>; and therefore, when they wish to suppress someone who feels differently than they do, they call him a Heretic, the hatred of which name Christ himself would not be able to escape. But if they called such a person, instead of *Haereticus* <note>original: "Haereticus"</note> Heretic, *Pertinax* <note>Latin for "obstinate" or "stubborn"</note> or headstrong, the mist of this error would easily clear up; and the Judges would be able to judge a stubborn person much more justly than a Heretic, because the first is a known crime, and the second an unknown one. We then shall explain the word thus: *Haeresis* <note>original: "Haeresis"</note> is a little Greek word, and signifies a sect, that is a following or consequence, or also an *opinio* <note>Latin for "opinion"</note>, that is a meaning or feeling.

<summary>Castellio warns that Calvin's definition of heresy will lead different Christian sects to destroy one another. He further argues that the term "Heretic" is a misunderstood Greek word used by agitators to incite hatred against those who simply hold different opinions.</summary>
<keywords>Heresy, Calvinism, sectarianism, etymology, Ecclesia, Idolum, religious tolerance, Sebastian Castellio, linguistic error</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of a heretic, moving from a general categorization of religious groups to a specific explanation of how "stubbornness" or "persistence" defines a true heretic compared to someone who is simply misguided or poorly taught.</meta>

# Dialogue

<columns>

Now there are three kinds of sects in the world: some are praiseworthy / as in Acts 24 and 28, where the sect of the Nazarenes is mentioned / that is / Christians. 
<margin>2 Peter 2</margin>
Others are entirely godless / of whom Peter speaks / where there were also false prophets among the people / just as there shall be false teachers among you / who shall introduce destructive sects, even denying the Lord who bought them / and bringing swift destruction upon themselves / and many shall follow their destruction / through whom the way of truth shall be blasphemed / and they shall through greed with fabricated words seek gain from you. Finally, other sects go between the two / and cannot in themselves be called godless / such as those who maintain religion / and believe the Holy Scripture / but understand it wrongly / such were the Scribes / Pharisees / Essenes / and Sadducees. Those who now stubbornly adhere to such evil sects / and do not want to be brought to a sounder doctrine / even though they are truly and properly exhorted to it / these are called in Greek <term>Heretics</term> <note>original: "Hæretici"</note>, by the Latins <term>the Persistent</term> <note>original: "Pertinaces"</note>, and by us the stubborn / or Heretics. I say that they must truly be exhorted / for he is not to be held as a Heretic who is falsely exhorted / that is / someone whom one seeks to bring to a false or untrue opinion. Just as if a Sadducee / had exhorted a Pharisee / to desist from his opinion on the resurrection / he would wrongly hold the Pharisee

<column-break/>

to be stubborn or heretical / and hold himself for steadfast and orthodox: indeed he himself would rather be stubborn or heretical. I say that they must properly be exhorted / for even if the Pharisees / who were themselves greedy / had exhorted someone to desist from his greed (for in morals there also lies stubbornness / or Heresy) / and he nonetheless had not desisted / he would not therefore immediately be stubborn / although he nonetheless ought to have desisted: but such should rather be blamed as a fault of the Pharisees / who, because they themselves were greedy / had used dead and powerless reasons. For the speech / which flows only from the lips / and not from the heart / (as is that of hypocrites or stage-actors <note>The original "kamerspeelders" refers to amateur actors or those performing in "chambers," implying a lack of sincerity</note>) is entirely powerless. But if a greedy person / having been exhorted by Christ / nevertheless continued in his greed / he would truly be stubborn.

Let us now come to our own century. There are today three kinds of sects: Godly / godless / and the middle-ground. Godly are they / who in this world live holily, temperately, and righteously,
<margin>Titus 2</margin>
expecting the blessed hope and appearance of the glory of the great God, whom they serve in the Spirit and truth. Godless are all despisers of God / and blasphemers / enemies and mockers of all religion / who believe the Holy books no more (to say nothing of less) / than the unholy / greedy / unchaste / and self-pleasure seeking people. These are for the most part Apostates / or defectors who in

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the discernment of true faith and godliness, moving from the state of those who fall away from the Gospel to a practical method for identifying true Christians among various religious factions.</meta>

->### Vaticanus and Calvin.<-

first believed the Gospel, but afterwards did not persevere, having become godless, and to whom it has happened as Christ says: *The evil spirit has returned to them, having with him seven other spirits more evil than himself, and thus the last state of those men has become worse than the first.* <note>A reference to the Parable of the Returning Unclean Spirit found in Matthew 12:43-45 and Luke 11:24-26.</note> Peter prophesied about such people in the passage cited above. Middle-ground are those who truly fear God and believe the Scripture, but do not understand it. Now, the godless and entirely abandoned people can easily be judged, as they reveal themselves enough not only through their morals but also through the impurity of their words, not even caring for godliness. But regarding who the true Christians and children of God are, there is great dispute; for among so many sects, there is not one that would not gladly claim those honorable names for themselves. If a dispute arose as to who was the best of many physicians, and the people could not judge by their learning—since they all disputed plausibly and cited Hippocrates and Galen <note>Hippocrates and Galen (Claudius Galenus) were the primary authorities on medicine from antiquity through the Renaissance.</note> equally strongly—then there would be no closer or clearer way for the people to judge than to set aside all their logical arguments and to judge by the fruit; that is, to give the prize to the one who had healed the most sick people. So also, when speaking of the medicines of souls, and the common people cannot judge the disputes of the learned, it would be best and surest to judge from the fruits; that is, to consider as the best soul-healers those who [the sick]...

<column-break/>

nesses of the soul—namely sins—heal best, and make the most sober people out of drunkards, the most temperate out of the intemperate, the most generous out of the greedy, and the most merciful out of the cruel and fierce. Paul taught us this way of judging; <margin>Galat. 5.</margin> *the works of the flesh* (he says) *are manifest, namely: adultery, fornication, impurity, lewdness, idolatry, sorcery, enmity, strife, jealousy, anger, quarreling, discord, sects, hatred, murder, drunkenness, gluttony, and the like;* <note>original: "wercken des vleeschs... overspel, hoererye, onreynicheydt..."</note> *But the fruits of the spirit are love, joy, peace, kindness, friendliness, faith, gentleness, temperance.* From these fruits, one can easily judge which sect is the best, namely the sect of those who believe in Christ, are obedient to him, and follow his life; whether they be called Papists <note>A term, often derogatory, used for Roman Catholics.</note>, or Lutherans, Zwinglians <note>Followers of the Swiss reformer Huldrych Zwingli.</note>, or Mennonites <note>Followers of Menno Simons, an Anabaptist leader.</note>; it matters not: for the truth lies not in the name, but in the deed. Those who are such, and for whom it is not enough to say *"Lord, Lord,"* but who furthermore do His will—they are truly godly people and children of God, who can judge heretics and other men. And whoever is not obedient to the warnings of such people may rightly be called stubborn and a heretic. Just as a disease that cannot be cured by a good physician who has cured many other diseases may justly be called a stubborn disease.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio’s views) and Calvin. The previous page used the analogy of a physician to argue that the "best" religion should be judged by its ability to heal souls and produce good fruits, rather than by complex theological arguments.</meta>

# Dialogue

But if a sick person could not be healed by a bad physician <note>original: "Medecijnmeester"</note>, one must not immediately call them incurable; for if the physician were good, they might perhaps be healed. So it also goes with morals; if a bad person could not be healed by another bad person, one must not therefore consider him obstinate, for he might perhaps be improved if he could obtain a good Master. Just as it happened long ago that a person was improved in a single hour by a God-fearing man, who for many years could not be improved by those like him. But nowadays, people set aside morals and holiness of life, and they proceed to judge based on doctrine, killing those who differ from them in opinion in some matter, even if they are otherwise upright in life. One ought to do the opposite and forgive a man his misdeed—even if he had clearly erred—if the rest of his life were innocent. Just as
<margin>2 Chronicles 19</margin>
**Jehu** said to **Jehoshaphat**: "You deserved for the Lord to be angry with you" (otherwise: "you should be punished by the Lord for this deed of yours"), "nevertheless, some good is found in you" (otherwise: "were it not that some things in you were praiseworthy"), "namely, that you have swept the groves <note>The "groves" refer to the Asherah poles, centers of pagan worship in the Old Testament.</note> out of the land, and have directed your heart to seek God."

Because people then want to judge and kill, not out of and according to morals, but out of and according to doctrine—and it has not yet been judged,

<column-break/>

indeed, before the coming of the supreme Judge <note>A reference to the Second Coming of Christ and the Last Judgment.</note> it cannot be judged, which of all is the best *Sect*—how then shall we conduct ourselves in the meantime with the obstinate? If you advise that they should be killed, then people of all *Sects* will be killed by those who belong to another sect. And so it shall happen that the **Calvinists** shall be killed in **France**, the **Papists** <note>A term used by Protestants for Roman Catholics.</note> in **Geneva**, the **Lutherans** by the **Zwinglians** <note>Followers of the Swiss reformer Huldrych Zwingli.</note>, the **Mennonites** <note>Anabaptist followers of Menno Simons, often persecuted by both Catholics and other Protestants.</note> by all others, and all others in turn by the **Mennonites**; and there shall be neither end nor measure to the killing, until we have all helped one another off the face of the earth.

He who thinks that the Church is built up through such a frenzy of killing makes a **Satan** out of **Christ**; for in that way—namely with blood and not with love—**Satan’s** Church is usually built. On the other hand, if you wish to leave the heretics unpunished, one will immediately think of those godless people and blasphemers: should they also not be punished? And therefore I believe that one ought to distinguish the matter; that is, to separate the godless from those who err. And because **Calvin**, when disputing about heretics, mixes and confuses all things together—now raking over the blasphemers, now the idolaters, now again the false prophets—he has burdened the heretics with the hatred of these names. Therefore, I shall prove that those held to be heretics are not such abandoned people.

<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

<summary>This page argues against the execution of religious dissenters, suggesting that moral conduct is a better measure of a person than doctrinal purity. The author warns that if every sect kills those it deems "heretical," it will lead to an endless cycle of mutual destruction.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio’s views) and Calvin. The previous page argued that a person's life and fruits are better indicators of their faith than strict adherence to a specific doctrine, using the example of King Jehoshaphat.</meta>

### Vaticanus and Calvin.

**Left Column**

Those godless ones, and despisers of Scripture, and blasphemers, I do not include under the name of Heretics <note>In the 16th century, "Heretic" was a legal category for those who held "incorrect" religious beliefs, often punishable by death.</note>, but I hold that they must be dealt with as people who have forgotten God. If they deny God, or blaspheme, if they publicly curse the doctrine of Christians, or loathe the holy life of the godly, I leave them to the authorities to be punished—not for their religion (for they have none) but (if I may speak so) for their irreligion. Yet, if any authority held such people prisoner to see if they might perhaps improve (for God's mercy is infinite), such an authority would seem to me not at all far removed from Christian kindness.

But when one deals with Heretics, they are judged according to the common feeling of the people; and therefore we must see who today are held to be Heretics. For Heretics today, one counts the Papists <note>A period term for Roman Catholics.</note>, the Waldensians, the Lutherans, the Zwinglians, the Mennonites (or Anabaptists), the Schwenckfeldians <note>Followers of Caspar Schwenckfeld, who emphasized the "inner word" of the Spirit over outward rituals.</note>, and others like them. I say that none of all these sects in themselves may be called godless, even if they all err, for they all believe in the same God and in one Lord and Savior Jesus Christ. I know well with how many and how absurd errors the Pope has filled the world, so that all the other aforementioned sects have rightly fallen away from him. I know also that in the others there are still errors which could be set right through a greater light, and that there are many masters among them who are not a hair

<column-break/>

**Right Column**

better than the Pope; but I am speaking here of such people who are, each in their own heart, religious. Take a good Papist—that is, one who fears God, who would not want to commit adultery, give false witness, nor do to another what he would not want done to himself—I say that he must not be called godless nor be killed for any reason. "Yes," you might say, "but he nevertheless honors images." What of it? He honors them out of error, not out of malice, just as we also once honored them. If you now want to insist on the literal words of the Law, then we all should have been killed. But it is not so, for God, who is merciful, wants the erring to be instructed and not killed. You will say, "Yes, but he persists in his error." 

What then? The Apostles themselves could
<margin>Matthew 17:15</margin>
not heal a certain lunatic <note>original: "maensiecken" - literally "moon-sick," a term used in the 16th century for epilepsy or those believed to be affected by the phases of the moon.</note> or possessed person, so stubborn was that guest; but did they have to kill him for that? Should they not rather have blamed their own unbelief, which was the cause of it, as the Lord reproached them? For such a kind of demon could not be cast out except by fasting and prayer. And if we now, along with the Apostles, do not have enough faith, do not fast enough from sins, or do not pray diligently enough, must we therefore kill those whom we cannot heal because of our own unbelief and fault? Woe to us if we do it! Is our faith, uprightness, and temperance so great that we exalt ourselves above the Apostles? Why do we not blame our own unbelief, which is the reason that we cannot cast out the spirit of error?

<sig>I iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while blasphemers might be punished for irreligion, "heretics" (including Catholics and various Protestant sects) should not be executed because they all believe in God and Christ. He suggests that if a person cannot be "cured" of their religious error, it may be due to the lack of faith and prayer in their teachers rather than the stubbornness of the student.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that various Christian sects—despite their disagreements on points like the Lord's Supper or Predestination—should not put one another to death, as they all acknowledge the same God and Christ.</meta>

# A Conversation

**Left Column**

What I say here regarding the **Papists**, I also want to be understood regarding all other sects. For if the **Papists**, who err so grossly and in so many matters, nevertheless may not be killed for their errors, how much less the others, who have for a large part moved away from their errors? Therefore, neither **Luther**, **Calvin**, nor **Zwingli** (provided that **Calvin** and **Zwingli** share the same view) should put the other to death—even if one considers the other a heretic in the matter of the Lord’s Supper <note>original: "Avontmael"; referring to the fierce debates over whether the bread and wine were the literal or symbolic body and blood of Christ.</note>, yes, even a devil and a reprobate. Nor should **Zwingli** kill **Luther**, even if he considers him blasphemous; nor **Calvin** kill **Melanchthon**, even if the latter feels differently than he does about Predestination <note>The doctrine that God has foreordained who will be saved and who will be damned.</note>; nor should any of them kill **Osiander**, because he differs from them in the matter of Justification <note>original: "Rechtveerdighmakinge"; the process by which a sinner is made righteous before God.</note>. To be brief, all who acknowledge one God and one Lord may not wrong <note>original: "voordelen"; here meaning to harm, disadvantage, or judge unfairly.</note> one another, which is a far cry from killing. For otherwise, whoever does such a thing cannot in any way escape being called that evil servant who—because his Lord delays his coming—beats his fellow servants.
<margin>Matthew 24:48.</margin>

And so that I may conclude this entire treatment of persecution, I shall now more broadly explain the passage of Scripture I just pointed out, speaking of the persecution of these last times. From this, the whole matter shall be understood, provided one is willing to open their eyes. Thus speaks the Lord: "Who then is a faithful and wise servant, whom his Lord has set over his household to give them food in due season? Blessed is the

<column-break/>

servant whom his Lord finds so doing when he comes. Truly I say to you, he shall set him over all his goods." He speaks here of a good Shepherd, who does not kill the sheep but feeds them with his teaching. "But if that evil servant says in his heart, 'My Lord delays his coming,' and begins to beat his fellow servants, and to eat and drink with the drunkards, then the Lord of that servant will come on a day when he does not expect him, and at an hour he does not know, and will cut him in pieces, and appoint him his portion with the hypocrites <note>original: "Gheveynſde"</note> (that is, he shall consider him equal to the pretenders); there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth." He speaks of an evil Shepherd, who does not pasture the sheep, but kills them. And when he speaks of the delay of the Lord's coming, he indicates thereby that he speaks of the last times, when **Christ** shall seem to delay his coming for a long time. For the ancients, such as **Augustine**, **Jerome**, **Hilary**, and others, did not persecute their fellow servants in this way, because they feared the Lord's coming; but these latest evil servants behave as if he will not come at all. What follows—"He began to beat his fellow servants"—signifies that he is not speaking here of the persecution of those who are not our fellow servants; that is, those who do not know the Lord **Christ**, such as **Turks** <note>In the 16th century, "Turks" was a common term for Muslims.</note>, **Jews**, and the like. For they do not persecute us for **Christ's** sake, nor we them. But he speaks of those who share one...

<meta>This page continues the author's application of the Parable of the Faithful and Evil Servant (Matthew 24) to the contemporary religious landscape. The previous page established that all Christian sects, despite their differences, should not persecute one another because they serve the same Master. ### Vaticanus and Calvin

**Left Column**

...confess one and the same Christ, such as are all the sects mentioned above, except for the godless who do not know Christ and therefore have no persecution to suffer. The fact that He says *he began* <note>Referring to Matthew 24:49: "And shall begin to smite his fellow-servants."</note> indicates that this persecution shall not be like that of the **Pagans**, who not only began the persecution but also brought it to an end without being hindered in it. For this persecution shall not last as long as the evil servant desires, but he shall be caught by surprise by the sudden coming of the Lord. The fact that He says *to eat and to drink with the drunkards*, He says this of the prosperous and the rich, who, being drunk with prosperity, play the master and rage against their fellow servants—which they did not do before, when they were not yet rich and powerful. For **Zwingli**, **Calvin**, and other persecutors, before they had obtained power and become strong, bore themselves humbly and meekly, condemning the persecutors. But now, having become fattened <note>original: "ghemest"; literally "stall-fed" or "fattened up," implying they have grown spiritually or materially arrogant through worldly success.</note> and strong, they pride themselves on their riches, and they rage and strike their weak fellow servants. Just as God foretold long ago 
<margin>Ezekiel 34:20</margin> 
with these words:
> Behold, I will judge between the fat and the lean sheep, because you have pushed with your sides and shoulders, and have thrust at all the weak with your horns, until you have scattered them abroad. And I will help my flock, so that they shall no longer be a prey, and I will judge between sheep and sheep, and I will raise up for them a single

<column-break/>

> Shepherd who shall feed them, namely my servant David; he shall feed them and be their shepherd. <note>The author uses this Old Testament prophecy to illustrate how powerful religious leaders (fat sheep) mistreat the humble (lean sheep).</note>

Furthermore, when Christ says that the Lord of this servant shall come on a day when he does not expect Him, and at an hour that he does not know, He indicates that the carelessness <note>original: "sorghloosheydt"; here meaning a state of spiritual complacency or false security.</note> shall then be as great as it was in the days of Noah. This carelessness is caused both by the hardened wickedness of men and by the flattering teachings of the hypocrites. When He says He *shall appoint him his portion with the hypocrites*, He means to say that the authors of this persecution shall be hypocrites—that is, people who seek to appear better than they actually are, and who therefore try by every means to defend their name and reputation, most of all by persecuting those by whom they are exposed.
For (to use **Calvin's** own words here) there is nothing so bloodthirsty as the **Hypocrite**. And therefore, whatever people bearing the Christian name inflict persecution upon other people who acknowledge the same God and Christ for the sake of Religion, they are without doubt that evil servant who strikes his fellow servants. **Consider, O Reader**, the whole matter; open your eyes and love the Truth, and you shall find that it is so.

<meta>This page continues the critique of the leaders of the Reformation, shifting from a general discussion of the "evil servant" in the Parable of the Faithful and Evil Servant to a specific accusation against John Calvin. It focuses on Calvin’s role in the arrest and execution of Michael Servetus, a Spanish physician and theologian who was burned for heresy. The text argues that Calvin’s actions reveal a hypocritical "double-mindedness."</meta>

->**Dialogue**<-

Let us now come to Calvin himself, who, setting aside the general persecution, turns his attention to Servetus <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a scholar and physician executed in Geneva for his non-trinitarian views.</note>, so that we may see what kind of "iron warrior of Christ" <note>original: "yfere Kryssman Christi"; a term used to describe a militant or uncompromising defender of the faith.</note> he is, and examine his deeds to the end that the simple-minded might not be deceived by this double-minded man. 

First, he pushes the matter of which he is accused away from his neck <note>An idiomatic way of saying he denies responsibility.</note>, as if it were not through his doing that Servetus was imprisoned in **Vienne** in the **Dauphiné** <note>Vienne is a city in southeastern France where Servetus was first arrested by Catholic authorities before escaping to Geneva.</note>. He says it is unbelievable that he should be on such good terms and so familiar with the **Papists** <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics.</note>—as if we did not know that a messenger was later sent from Geneva to Vienne to fetch the sentence that had been passed there against Servetus. Or as if such great intimacy were required to ensure that Servetus was imprisoned by the Papists, he who had written so vehemently against the **Pope**, and in that very book (which was printed in Vienne at the time) proved with many arguments that the Pope was the **Antichrist**. 

Or as if peace could not easily be made between **Herod** and **Pilate** <note>A biblical reference to Luke 23:12, where two enemies become friends through their shared opposition to Christ.</note> against someone who is an enemy to them both. Truly, Calvin denies this matter in such a way that he almost admits it, for he writes as follows:

### Calvin 130.

Nor would I, if they cast this against me with truth, think I had any cause to deny it; I, who do not deny that he was seized in this city through my doing, and was made

<column-break/>

to answer for his case.

### Vaticanus.

As if he meant to say: "Had I caused him to be arrested in Vienne, I would have done well, since I have done so in Geneva; I have done a good deed therein." 

If, then, he was able to have him arrested in Geneva, he must also have been able to do it there [in Vienne], since he admits that such a thing is well done. Now, he could have done that very easily; for what is easier to do than to ensure that a man is imprisoned in France upon the slightest identifying mark—a man who had said (to say nothing of such violent things) anything (which is less than writing) against the Pope? 

Thus, I no longer doubt whether Calvin did it, and moreover also wrote, or at least caused to be written, the words he recounts here; namely, that Servetus denies it, "but we have his own hand [handwriting], which shall be shown to you, if you wish." This was written from **Geneva** to **Lyon** to the Papists. 

Now, let one consider what kind of confession this is from Calvin. "Had I caused" (he says) "Servetus to be imprisoned in France, I would have done well, since, having done it in Geneva, I have done well." If Calvin would have done well in that, then the Papists also (according to Calvin’s judgment) would have done well to imprison and kill Servetus.

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

And thus it is also permitted for the **Papists** <note>A term used by reformers to refer to Roman Catholics.</note>, just as much as for those of **Geneva**, to kill heretics. Now **Calvin** has previously said that this is allowed to no one but a **godly government**: therefore the Papist government is godly, and is just as capable of judging in matters of **religion** <note>original: "religie"</note> as that of Geneva. If that government is godly, and this is permitted to them, then they may, with this godly judgment of theirs, kill those whom they judge to be heretics; and consequently, they may also kill **Calvin** and all **Calvinists**, since they hold them to be heretics. 

Does **Calvin** wish to say that such a thing was permitted to them in the person of **Servetus**, but not in others? Who shall allow this exception and accept it as valid currency? For they (the Papists) shall say: "We do not kill **Servetus** as **Servetus**, but as a heretic; and from this we conclude (according to your own teaching, Master **John Calvin**) that it is permitted for us to kill those whom we hold to be like **Servetus**—that is, to be heretics." For it must be one of two things: either we must not be allowed to kill heretics at all, or if we are, we must be allowed to judge whom we hold to be heretics or not. For since we are **Magistrates** <note>Civil authorities or city officials.</note>, we follow our own judgment and not that of other people. If you wish to take from us the judgment of **religious** matters, then take from us at the same time the power to judge **Servetus**. If you grant us such judgment, then grant us also the right to judge you, whom we hold to be just as much a heretic as **Servetus**. 

Just as we would also kill you, if you advised us to kill all murderers, and if

<column-break/>

we held you to be a murderer. For we hold that we may freely kill only our enemies, and not our own people.

->**Calvin** 131.<-

### That the ill-willed, or also
criminal men, may hate as long as they please, I gladly admit—and even better <note>original: "moy mede"; an idiomatic expression suggesting "all the better" or "so be it."</note> (since according to the laws of this city (1) the man could not otherwise be legally addressed) that the informer or accuser was appointed by me (2). I also do not deny that by my counsel a **form** <note>original: "formulier"; a legal document or formal set of charges.</note> (3) was written to make an entrance into the case.

->**Vaticanus.**<-

(1) In **Geneva** there is a law that one may not arrest anyone on a Sunday, unless it concerns capital matters <note>Issues of life and death.</note>. Since **Calvin** appointed the accuser on a Sunday, he either accused **Servetus** of a capital crime, or he acted against the city's Law. 

(2) In the Law of **Moses** <note>The biblical legal code found in the Old Testament.</note>, the accusers are witnesses, and such as can say "I have seen it" or "I was there," and they threw the first stone. Now, whether **Calvin’s** proxy <note>original: "hoc"; likely referring to the servant or associate Calvin used as a formal accuser.</note> could have judged **Servetus’s** errors regarding **Fate** <note>original: "Fatum"</note>—that is, **Predestination** <note>original: "Predestinatie"; the doctrine that God has foreordained all that will happen, especially with regard to the salvation of some and not others.</note>—the **Trinity** <note>original: "dry-eenigheyt"; literally "three-unity."</note>, and similar obscure questions (which the Church has sweated over for so many hundreds of years), and whether he was entitled to throw the first stone, or whether he himself, as a false witness, ought to be punished with the same penalty—that I leave to be judged by those who know his ignorance. Furthermore, whether it was permitted for a **Shepherd** <note>A pastor or minister.</note> to be an accuser against a...

<meta>This page continues the debate between "Vaticanus" (the pseudonym of Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the trial and execution of Michael Servetus in Geneva. The previous page discussed the legal implications of the Magistrate’s power in religious matters; this page focuses on the procedural manipulation Calvin used to bring Servetus to trial while avoiding personal legal risk.</meta>

# Dialogue with Calvin 133.

...that one is called from the body <note>Likely referring to someone of lowly status or a servant.</note> to be fetched from his kitchen; those people may say that who know the nature and customs of the Apostles. This cook is no accuser, but a mute character, and simultaneously an agent <note>original: "mits-aen-zocht"</note> for an accuser, made up to deceive the **Magistrate**, and through this to bring it about that the real accuser, **Calvin**, might push through the entire case of **Servetus**. This he would not have been able to do if he had been required, according to the City Statutes, to stand his ground <note>original: "voet by stuck," an idiom meaning to stand firm or face the consequences directly.</note> and go into custody himself. (3) What kind of formula this might have been, I do not know; but from the outcome, I suspect it was thus: "I say that this man is a heretic." This accusation **Calvin** concludes, and then says: "Heretics ought to be killed. Therefore <note>original: "Ergo"</note> (I say following that) this man must be killed, on the condition nonetheless that if I do not prove him to be guilty, I am content to die in his place, according to the law of retribution <note>The *lex talionis*, which required an accuser to suffer the same penalty as the accused if the charges were not proven.</note>." Behold, this is **Calvin’s** accusation. Let him now answer once before **God**, who sees through all hearts, whether he would also want people to leap upon him in the same way as he has done with **Servetus**.

### Calvin 132.

But what my intention then was, that has since become apparent from the progress of the proceedings.

### Vaticanus

It became apparent from the fact that he was imprisoned on a Sunday, and further from the outcome—that is, from the man's death.

<column-break/>

For as soon as I and my fellow brothers were called, it did not depend on us <note>original: "en heeft het aë ös niet getogé," likely meaning "it was not for lack of effort on our part."</note> that he should not have been able to deal freely and friendly with us concerning his doctrines.

### Vaticanus.

A poor and all too subservient freedom of disputation <note>A formal academic or theological debate.</note>, when the **Executioner** must provide the conclusion, unless one agrees with **Calvin’s** opinion. This is something **Calvin** has drawn from somewhere other than the **Gospel**. If he wanted to dispute, then he should not have accused (for no disputation fits an accusation, only a defense), but rather should have invited the man as a guest in a friendly manner. He did not need to fear that **Servetus**, having the freedom to dispute, would have run away, for he had perhaps come to **Geneva** for that very purpose. Indeed, even if he had feared his running away, he still should not have brought the man into such a heap of trouble for that reason; for one must not dispute with the unwilling.

### Calvin 134:

Indeed, we went to him as if we ourselves had to give an account of our doctrine from within the bonds <note>Referring to the chains of imprisonment.</note>, and if he had wanted to object to anything, we testified that we were ready to answer him.

### Vaticanus.

He must be very gullible who will believe that. He who pleads his case from bonds fears the punishment. But how can such **Preachers** be feared, since the authorities in **religious** <note>original: "religiös"</note> matters do not pass judgment except according to their prescription and opinion?

<meta>catchword: Cal-</meta>

<summary>This page critiques John Calvin for using his servant as a proxy accuser to bypass the "law of retribution" and argues that the theological debate offered to Servetus was a sham because it took place under the threat of execution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemic between "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the 1553 trial and execution of Michael Servetus. Castellio uses this dialogue to highlight what he perceives as Calvin's hypocrisy and the psychological pressure placed upon the accused.</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

### Calvin 135.

Although he repeatedly spat out many insults at me with a full mouth, so that the Judges themselves were annoyed—yes, even made to blush—nevertheless, I refrained from insulting him in return.

### Vaticanus

<margin>Acts 23:3</margin> Truly, I do not praise Servetus for not restraining his tongue; yet one must pay close attention to the heart from which the words are spoken. Had Servetus spoken to Calvin with such a heart as Paul said to the High Priest—*God shall strike you, you whitewashed wall* <note>original: "ghewitte wandt"; a reference to Acts 23:3, where Paul insults the High Priest Ananias for hypocrisy.</note>—I would not think that one ought to rebuke such a thing lightly, for it would have been true. But if Servetus threw out anything too immoderate, having to deal with the most immoderate man in the world, then Calvin has met his match; and therefore he should not rebuke him. Since he himself is so overflowing in insults that some say one could gather a dictionary or word-book of such material from his writings. That he then says he refrained from insulting Servetus is hard for me to believe, seeing as he was accustomed to publicly exposing the man daily in his sermons, and now still exposes him in a printed book to the whole world, calling him a "foul dog," an "unbridled horse," and what not. How shall one then believe that in the struggle itself he refrained from insulting the man? No; either he did not refrain from it, or if

<column-break/>

indeed he did, then he wanted to gain a reputation for modesty with the **Magistrate** <note>The civil government of Geneva.</note> and with all the world, so that afterward he might mock and slander the man according to his own mere pleasure. For if it had been genuine modesty, he would not have exposed the man in his absence, nor now that he is dead, to the whole world.

### Calvin 136.

Add to this that there was no danger of any heavy punishment, if he had been in any way curable. <note>In this context, "curable" refers to Servetus being willing to recant his "heretical" views on the Trinity.</note>

### Vaticanus.

Those who are sick in body while in prison are sometimes released in order to be cured. Calvin considers Servetus "incurable"; yet even if he had been healthy, he might have become sick—or being already sick, become incurable—through such great injustice. Or one must say that chains weigh more heavily on the sicknesses of the body than on those of the soul.

### Calvin 137.

But it was so far from the case that he would request to be handled more gently, that being full of boastfulness and arrogance, he willfully rejected all healthy and useful advice.

### Vaticanus

Exactly so; then Servetus would have been "healthy" if he had believed that Calvin’s advice was healthy for him. If Calvin were thus imprisoned in Spain <note>Referring to the Spanish Inquisition, from which Calvin, as a Protestant, would have faced the same fate as Servetus.</note>, he would certainly realize how "healthy" the advice of his enemies was to him.

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the original page is "Cal-", indicating the start of the next section "Calvijn 138".</meta>

<summary>Castellio (as Vaticanus) accuses Calvin of hypocrisy, arguing that Calvin’s claims of modesty are false given his history of verbal abuse, and mocks the idea that Servetus was "incurable" for refusing Calvin's theological corrections.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between John Calvin and "Vaticanus" (a pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio). The debate centers on whether Michael Servetus was executed for his theological beliefs (heresy) or his supposedly abrasive and "unruly" behavior during the trial, as well as the role of predestination in his fate.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
->Dialogue<-

# Calvin 138.

But how many absurd and cursed blasphemies escaped him in his speech will perhaps be more conveniently related elsewhere: I only wish to testify for now that I was not such a deadly enemy to him that he could not have saved his life simply through modesty <note>original: "sedigheyde"; meaning temperate or restrained behavior.</note> (unless he had been robbed of his senses).

# Vaticanus.

So the man was not killed for Heresy, but for his unruliness <note>original: "onghestuimigheyt"; meaning turbulence or tempestuousness.</note>. In that case, Heretics must not be killed unless they are also unruly. Why did Calvin not point out this rule earlier when he was disputing the killing of Heretics? Furthermore, if modesty can benefit the lives of Heretics, why not also the lives of murderers? For it is a much more serious matter in Geneva to be a Heretic than a murderer: as can be traced from the severity of the punishment: for Heretics are burned alive there, while murderers are merely executed with the sword. Truly, one who has eyes sees clearly enough that Calvin himself admits that Servetus died out of Calvin’s hate and envy.

# Calvin 139.

But I know not what to say, except that he must have been possessed with a fatal or predestined madness to throw himself down from above.

# Vaticanus.

Some Calvinists, not being able

<column-break/>

to justify Calvin’s deed in this regard, have finally fled to the argument that Servetus necessarily had to be burned in Geneva. Or as the brothers of Leeuwarden <note>A city in the northern Netherlands (Friesland) where reformed debates were common.</note> write, his trap was set for him there. By doing this, one will be able to excuse everything that happens, by saying *So it was in the Fates* <note>original: "Sic Erat in Fatis"</note>; the end had to be so. Have I murdered the man? *So it was in the Fates*; I was predestined for it. Nevertheless, Calvin does not want to accept the name of Fate or necessity, as one may see in his *Institutes* Chapter 14, section 40; but he holds the matter itself so firmly that he simultaneously rejects the name of chance or fortune.

# Calvin 140.

To this, someone will perhaps object: why did I go beyond Paul’s command, who only orders his Titus to shun a heretical person? <note>Referring to Titus 3:10: "A man that is an heretick after the first and second admonition reject."</note> But I have my answer ready, namely that it was enough for me to shun a man (in whose godlessness I knew there was no hope) and to warn others to beware of him: but that neither I, nor anyone else, was hindered by this caution of shunning the man from delivering the same heretic—when the opportunity arose—to the godly Magistrate to be punished.

# Vaticanus.

In this way the masters of the Sorbonne <note>The prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris, often used here as a comparison to strict Catholic inquisitorial methods.</note> justify themselves: they accuse and reject a heretic, while meanwhile commanding the Government—if it may be done in any way—to keep the same person alive; but oft-

<meta>catchword: then</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Vaticanus critiques Calvin's claim that Servetus could have saved himself through "modesty," arguing that Geneva punishes heresy more severely than murder. The discussion also touches upon Calvinist predestination as a justification for the execution and Calvin's interpretation of the biblical command to shun heretics.</summary>

### Dialogue

that he afterwards writes of him to the Corinthians thus. <margin>2 Cor 2:6</margin> It is enough for such a person that he was punished by many, so that you ought much more to forgive him and comfort him, so that he might not somehow perish in excessive sorrow. Therefore I pray you that you show love toward him. See, Paul fears that the man might perish from grief because of the shame of his <term>excommunication</term> <note>original: "afsnijdinghe," literally "cutting off," referring to being expelled from the church community</note>; how much more would he have feared that from the Executioner? Not to mention how unjust it would have been to strike the man with two rods: once from Paul and again from the Magistrate. Why did Calvin not fear the same for Servetus, who in vain humbly begged the Magistrate for the sword, so as not to be brought to the ultimate despair by the unbearable pain of the fire? Where then was the love of the Calvinists? Why did Calvin not hand him over to Satan, that he might learn not to blaspheme? <note>Referring to 1 Timothy 1:20, where Paul "hands over" sinners to Satan for spiritual discipline rather than physical execution.</note> He handed him over to the Executioner; the Executioner was his Satan. How shall Servetus now learn not to blaspheme and <margin>Psalm 6</margin> praise God? Since it stands written there: the dead shall not praise you, Lord. Or how would Hymenaeus and Alexander have learned not to blaspheme if they had fallen into the Executioner's hands through Paul's accusation? Does the Executioner also usually un-teach people how to blaspheme? (2) I am so far removed from Calvin’s opinion in this matter that I do not doubt that Paul would have prayed the Magistrate to be merciful to them and to forgive the guilt, so that they might learn not to blaspheme. For that

<column-break/>

accords with Paul’s kindness; and he did the same for Philemon, for whom he so friendlily prays on behalf of Onesimus, his formerly useless servant: <margin>Philemon 18</margin> *If he has done you any wrong, or owes you anything, charge that to me; I, Paul, have written it with my hand, I will pay it.* This differs as much from Calvin’s opinion as Heaven does from the earth. Calvin thinks that Paul was a Calvinist; and because he cannot say that Paul actually did such things (for all his teaching and life, as long as he was in Christ, straightly contradicts it), he says instead that Paul *would* have done it, had he had a godly Magistrate on his side. Yes, certainly, if he had been the man he was before he became Paul, he would have done it; and our Calvin makes him into such a man, for he prefers **Saul** over **Paul**. <note>A reference to Paul's life before his conversion, when as Saul he was a violent persecutor of Christians.</note> But dear friend, if Paul would have done that to the Heretics, why did he not do it to the evildoers? He surely had the Magistrate at hand to punish the murderers, adulterers, false witnesses, and the like, and he teaches himself in his letters that the Magistrate is God’s servant in that regard. Why then did he never accuse any evildoer, when he certainly could have? Or why would he pick out only the Heretics to accuse, since Calvin himself reprimands such foolish "picking and choosing" of one thing over another among the Papists? There it is commanded in the Law (he says on page 389 of his first *Institutes*) to slaughter the calf, to offer its fat—

<meta>This page continues the polemic between "Vaticanus" (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the execution of Michael Servetus in 1553. The previous page discussed how the Apostle Paul preferred spiritual discipline over physical execution, contrasting this with Calvin's reliance on the state's "sword."</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

...[slaughtering] rams, and burning them, with countless more other institutions; all of which being set aside, it makes me wonder why only the anointing with oil <note>Original: "salvinghe met oly." This likely refers to the selective application of Old Testament laws by the Reformers, specifically regarding the authority of the state.</note> should please them above all else. But with greater right it makes me wonder why so many different kinds of evildoers whom the Law commands to be killed are passed over by Calvin, and it only pleases him to accuse the **Heretics**, of whom in the entire Law not a single **Tittle** <note>A "tittle" refers to the smallest stroke in writing; Vaticanus is arguing that the Mosaic Law does not explicitly mandate death for the specific kind of "heresy" Calvin is prosecuting.</note> is to be found.

### Calvin 142.

For that which is commanded to me and all ministers of the word does not bind the hands of the Judges, so that they might not therefore carry out the office laid upon them by God. Indeed, it does not even prevent me (1) from commending the truth of God, when it is appropriate, to their protection.

### Vaticanus.

That is: it does not prevent me from denouncing the Heretics and saying: "See, my Lords, the faith stands under your protection; if you let this one go, you shall abandon the faith and your bounden duty." This is the same as what he claimed Paul would have done. Which, what else is it to say than: "You just go ahead, Pilate; it is not permitted for us to kill anyone; but to denounce, and to threaten the authorities if they refuse to serve us, that is our task." And here Calvin contradicts himself, for above he said that he had not uttered a single word regarding Servetus’s death after he was convicted: and here he writes that this is exactly the Office of

<column-break/>

a minister of the word, and that Paul would have done it. If Paul ought to have done it, then Calvin surely must have done it too; and if he did it, then he lies when he denies it; if he did it not, then he has not fulfilled his Office. That which Calvin further writes of Servetus, and those three questions, I pass by here—not because there is not enough to rebuke in them, but because there would never be an end to refuting Calvin’s errors, and also because Servetus has passed away. Whereby that which Calvin has lied about Servetus must rest upon Calvin’s word: to whom how much faith one may and ought to give can be sufficiently gathered from what we have related above; it is easy to slander a dead man. But Servetus shall one day rise again with us, and his cause is not yet so suppressed by Calvin that it shall not (if he has lied) be able to refute him before God, who shall judge very differently than Calvin. Let us now come to Servetus’s death, which Calvin describes in such a manner that he simultaneously portrays his own mercy. For thus he speaks:

### Calvin 143.

But so that the rogues, who know no counsel for their idle hands, might not boast of the stubbornness of the senseless man as if he were a martyr: in his death there appeared a beastly stupidity <note>original: "beestelijcke domheyt." Calvin often described Servetus's final refusal to recant not as courage, but as a lack of human reason.</note> from which one might judge that religion had never been a matter of earnest to him, or had never gone to his heart.

<meta>This page continues a dialogue, likely between the character Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio) and an opponent, discussing John Calvin’s account of the execution of Michael Servetus in 1553. The previous context likely involved accusations of Servetus's cowardice or "beastly" behavior during his final moments.</meta>

# Dialogue

<margin>Vaticanus.</margin>
<margin>This is a small specimen of Calvin’s modesty <note>original: "seedbaerheydt," meaning modesty, decorum, or civility</note>.</margin>
<margin>Calvin, p. 144.</margin>
Now that death had been announced to him, he sometimes stood as if struck senseless; sometimes he let out very deep sighs; sometimes he howled as if he were possessed.

<margin>Vaticanus</margin>
Although I believe that all of this is said by Calvin in the most hateful way and in bad faith—more out of a desire to find fault <note>original: "wroegs-wise"</note> than out of truth—I nonetheless do not perceive here any "beastly dullness" <note>original: "beestelijcke doriheydt." This refers to a state of being unfeeling or insensible, like an animal.</note> at all. We see that [dullness] in "war-lions" <note>professional soldiers or mercenaries hardened by combat</note> and other inhuman men who are no more disturbed by the fear of death than beasts are. But for a man to be terrified by it—that is human, and not beastly. Hezekiah <note>A King of Judah mentioned in Isaiah 38</note> also let out deep sighs when it was announced to him that he would die—and that by a far gentler death than Servetus faced; he moaned like a dove or a crane. Job himself, whom one is accustomed to present as a mirror of patience, remained as if defeated for seven days long, even though his friends did not bring him such evil news as Servetus received from his enemies. Yes, Christ himself sweated blood from anxiety, and his soul was sorrowful even unto death; and hanging on the cross, as one abandoned, he cried out: "My God, my God, why have you

<column-break/>

forsaken me?" All this Calvin could mock with similar skill, and as those prone to suspicion are wont to do, twist it backwards and interpret it in the worst light—which he nonetheless writes is not proper to do in his first *Institutes* <note>John Calvin’s seminal work, *Institutes of the Christian Religion*</note>, as we have shown above. But this is the true nature of the Sophists <note>Here used as a pejorative for those who use clever but fallacious arguments</note>: that they twist everything, and can now praise and then blame the very same thing. If Servetus is disturbed by death, they say it is a "beastly dullness"; if an Anabaptist <note>A radical reformist group often persecuted for their beliefs on adult baptism</note> dies steadfastly (as people indeed say of those folk, that they are unbelievably steadfast in enduring death), then they say it is "stubbornness" and happens through the power of the devil. In this, they are truly followers of the Jews <note>A common 16th-century rhetorical device comparing opponents to the biblical Pharisees</note>, whom neither the severity of John [the Baptist] nor the friendliness of Christ satisfied. Conversely, if someone among their own disciples dies steadfastly, they exalt him to Heaven; if he is troubled before death, they know how to excuse him immediately. Which I also do not condemn: for love <note>Christian charity/Caritas</note> wishes that one interprets all these things for the best. But that they do not judge others in the same way—that I cannot approve. One must in every way look into the heart, not at the face or the gestures of the body. For it can happen that a godly man will weep in his death and a godless man will sing; and conversely, a godly man may sing and a godless man may weep. And therefore these are no certain signs of the soul's state. That Servetus was troubled does not surprise me. Calvin himself, who now with the...

<meta>catchword the</meta>

<summary>In this dialogue, the author (likely Castellio) defends Michael Servetus's emotional reaction to his death sentence against John Calvin's mockery, arguing that fear of death is a human trait shared by biblical figures like Hezekiah, Job, and Christ. He accuses Calvin and his followers of "Sophistry" for inconsistently judging the deaths of their enemies as beastly or demonic while praising similar behaviors in their own martyrs.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Michael Servetus, martyrdom, Hezekiah, Job, Anabaptists, Sophistry, religious tolerance, fear of death, Institutes of the Christian Religion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the 1553 execution of Michael Servetus in Geneva. The previous page ended with Vaticanus comparing Servetus’s cries to the suffering of Christ on the cross.</meta>

vaticanus and Calvin.
->K ii<-
->pray<-

is so arrogantly mocking; if he were in [Servetus's] shoes, I do not know how he would behave, since he is often accustomed—whenever someone speaks to him a bit too boldly—to immediately call it his "migraine" <note>original Latin: "sic-hemicrania"</note> or, as they say in the Zeeland dialect, a "splitting headache" <note>original: "schelen hooft-sweer"</note>; so poorly can the man keep his anger in check. It works insidiously like this: the more courageously someone behaves when he is far from pain, the weaker he is when the "straitjacket" <note>original: "nyprock," a metaphor for the final agony or the constraints of execution</note> must be put on. Believe me, reader, it is one thing to talk under the shade of an elderberry tree, and quite another to see oneself being smeared with sulfur by the executioner and to have the fire set around one's face. O Calvin, Calvin, see to it that they do not deal with you in such a way, either in this present life (which would be better for you) or in the life to come.

### Calvin 145.

...which howling at the end became so great that he shouted nothing else but, in his Spanish: "Mercy, mercy! <note>original Latin: "Misericordia, misericordia"</note> Grace, grace!"

### Vaticanus.

He truly had good reason to call upon the mercy of God, since he had fallen into the hands of men who were so unmerciful that, despite all his praying and pleading, he could not even obtain the sword <note>meaning a quick death by beheading rather than the slow death of burning at the stake</note>, which he surely would have received even among the cannibals or man-eaters.

<column-break/>

### Calvin 146.

When he arrived at the place where he was to die, our best brother and fellow-minister Farel <note>William Farel, a key leader of the Reformation in Geneva</note> could hardly pluck enough speech from his mouth to get him to ask the people to pray collectively with him. I do not know how he could still take this upon his conscience, for he had written with his own hand that here (in Geneva) the "faith of devils" was in fashion: that we had no Church, indeed no God, and that by baptizing children, we were forsaking Christ. How then did he bring himself to pray with a people whose fellowship was to be shunned? Is it not a desecration of holy unity to profess the same God and faith with a godless and unholy assembly?

### Vaticanus.

Luther <note>original: "Lupher," likely a printer's error for Martin Luther</note> also called those of Zurich "soul-murderers," but testified that he spoke such things of their Teachers. The Lord Christ himself prayed for the common people. Servetus may likewise have meant the Teachers with those words, and yet did not mean the same for the people, or at least not for all of them. He who believes a congregation is deceived and misled does not therefore curse the entire congregation. Christ and the Apostles themselves did not fail to pray in the Temple.

<summary>Vaticanus defends Servetus’s final cries for mercy as a natural human reaction to a cruel execution and argues that praying with the Genevans was an act of Christian charity rather than a contradiction of his previous criticisms.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Michael Servetus, William Farel, Geneva, execution, religious tolerance, Martin Luther, mercy, prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin regarding the 1553 execution of Michael Servetus in Geneva. The previous page ended with Calvin describing Servetus’s desperate cries for mercy as he faced the stake.</meta>

# Dialogue

to pray and to be present at the divine service; nevertheless, Christ testified that <margin>Luke 19</margin> the Jews had made the Temple into a den of murderers <note>original Dutch: "Moort-kuyl." While often translated as "den of thieves" in English Bibles, the author uses a stronger term here to emphasize the violence of the state.</note>. How does he then go about praying together with the murderers? Calvin himself has a congregation in which there are many hypocrites and godless people, as we have proven above from his writings. With what conscience, then, does he allow himself to call upon the same God alongside hypocrites and the godless?

->Calvin 147.<-

Farel <note>Guillaume Farel (1489–1565), a leading French reformer and associate of Calvin who accompanied Servetus to his execution.</note> indeed exhorted that one should pray for him, but he explicitly mocked him at the same time, [praying] so that God would take pity on the lost man and would bring him from his cursed errors <note>original: "dolingē," referring to theological mistakes or heresies.</note> to a better state.

->Vaticanus<-

So Servetus is then accused because of his errors; but the Law kills no one for errors, even if someone had killed his own father out of error. The Jews who killed Christ erred far more grossly than Servetus, and although

<column-break/>

they were worthy of death for the sake of the murder <note>original: "bootslags," referring to the act of killing.</note>, they were nevertheless not worthy of it for the sake of their errors. Thomas <note>The Apostle "Doubting" Thomas.</note> also erred very heavily when he could not be moved to believe that Christ had risen—neither through Holy Scripture, nor through Christ's prophecy, nor through the harmonious testimony of so many apostles and disciples. Such a great error might well be called blasphemy by Calvin; but should he have been killed for it? Servetus believed that Christ had risen even though he had not seen it with his own eyes; why then did they not keep him—even if he erred in other things—and give him time to repent? Had Thomas been killed then, would the door to his salvation not have been closed? Why do we set a time for the Lord to help, and define it so narrowly that if He does not convert this man from his errors before the pyre <note>original: "bier," likely referring to the wooden structure of the funeral pile.</note> is lit, the man must burn this day? And if the Lord wishes to come only tomorrow, shall we set Him a limit? Oh! If God wanted to kill all erring people, who would go free? But they will say that these errors were blasphemies; therefore Musculus <note>Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563), a German Reformed theologian.</note> also speaks thus of Servetus in the epitaph which is said to have been written by him:

<summary>Vaticanus argues that theological errors should not be punished by death, using the Apostle Thomas's doubt as a precedent for why the erring should be given time to repent rather than being executed.</summary>
<keywords>Servetus, Calvin, Farel, Apostle Thomas, Musculus, religious tolerance, heresy vs. error, capital punishment, blasphemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Vaticanus (representing Sebastian Castellio) and John Calvin. The discussion focuses on the legal and moral justification for the execution of Michael Servetus in 1553. The previous page discussed whether it was right to pray with those one considers "godless" or "erroneous."</meta>

# Vaticanus and Calvin.

->Servetus, a Spaniard,<-
->Who was entirely unworthy<-
->To live here for so long<-
->Within the true Christian realm,<-
->Has, out of hatred and spite,<-
->Falsely alleged of us,<-
->That we a three-headed Beast<-
->And devilish phantom or spirit,<-
->Which Satan first devised,<-
->Have held to be the upright God,<-
->Even until [the end]<-
->Remaining blind of sight.<-
->Not for his heresy<-
->Or errors was he<-
->burned in Geneva:<-
->But for this wicked blasphemy,<-
->And his godless tongue,<-
->Was he put to death.<-

Do you see clearly what he calls blasphemy here? It is not that Servetus had mocked [God], saying that God was a three-headed beast or a Devil (as the people have been falsely led to believe), but rather that he had mocked them <note>The Reformers, specifically Calvin and his associates.</note> for not knowing what God was, and for serving a fictional God instead of the true God. Now, this should not be called blasphemy, but an error <note>The Dutch word "dolinghe" implies a wandering or mistake in judgment rather than a malicious attack on the divine.</note>. But just as sycophants <term>Pluymstrijckers: literally "feather-strokers," meaning flatterers or "yes-men" who smooth over the faults of their superiors.</term> are accustomed to minimizing the faults of those they flatter—calling one who is angry "strict," a miser "thrifty," and a spendthrift "generous"—so too do the false-speakers or slanderers tend to aggravate those same faults and

<column-break/>

to call an error "blasphemy," thus interpreting other people’s words incorrectly and in the worst possible way. Had Servetus called God a Devil, that would indeed be blasphemy, and I would rejoice that he had been put to death. But in saying that they <note>Calvin and his followers.</note> did not know what God was, and consequently served a false God, he did not speak against God, but against men. Otherwise, the Jews might also have accused Christ when he said that they <margin>Luke 19</margin> had made a den of murderers <note>original: "Moort-kuyl." Referring to Jesus cleansing the Temple.</note> out of the house of God. Likewise, when he said that they were of their father the Devil, even though he himself had acknowledged that they were children of Abraham.

K iij
<meta>Catchword: Sp (referring to the next page's starting word)</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the distinction between "error" and "blasphemy" in the trial of Michael Servetus. Vaticanus argues that Servetus did not insult God, but rather criticized the Reformers' understanding of God, which should not have been a capital offense.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Calvin's account of Michael Servetus’s trial. <page-num>147</page-num>
<header>VATICANUS AND CALVIN.</header>

I wish to present a sample here, which (far from being, as he might perceive it, contradictory to the others) actually confirms all the others and makes them credible when it is examined.

->vaticanus.<-

Calvin proves that Servetus’s teaching was condemned by those people with a unanimous voice, and that they wrote as if from a single mouth—which you, Reader, should carefully note, as it pertains to what we shall point out later. Furthermore, in place of all the others, Calvin provides the letter from the Swiss Church, in which they condemn Servetus as a heretic and blasphemer, and thereafter craftily advise the Council of Geneva to put the man to death with these words:

### Those of Zurich 151.

We therefore judge that great faithfulness and diligence must be exercised against this man, especially because our churches have a bad reputation among foreigners <note>original: "uptlanders," referring here to those outside the Reformed faith, specifically Catholics</note> (1), as if they were heretical and supported heretics.

->vaticanus<-

<margin>(1) By "foreigners" they here mean the Papists <note>A term used by Reformers for Roman Catholics</note>: among whom they do not wish to have a bad reputation. And yet they hold the Pope to be a Heretic, even the Antichrist: thus they wish to gain favor and a good name from the Antichrist. Just look at what sort of Christians they are!</margin>

### Those of Zurich 152.

Now God's holy providence has brought an opportunity to clear both you and us

<column-break/>

from the suspicion of this evil, provided that you are watchful and diligently see to it that the contagion of this poison does not crawl any further through this man: which we do not doubt will be done by you.

->vaticanus.<-

<margin>Galatians 1:10</margin>

*If I pleased men, I should not be a servant of Christ,* says Paul. These people, to please the Papists (whom they regard as enemies of Christ), advise those of Geneva to kill the man, so that they might be cleansed through Servetus’s blood. Even so, they do not say, "kill the man," but indicate as much with a crafty detour of words, so that the deed follows from it, while they—should they be accused—can still justify themselves. But who does not understand that they advise those of Geneva to treat Servetus just as, or even more harshly than, they themselves treated the Anabaptists? <note>The Anabaptists practiced adult baptism and were frequently executed by drowning or burning by other Protestant groups.</note> For since they have killed a great number of them for the sake of "re-baptism," who can doubt that they would want to deal even more severely with Servetus, since they condemn in him not only that same point—namely, "re-baptism"—but also many other and far greater points? Thus, those of Geneva—especially since they took Master Calvin as their interpreter—could easily have understood their meaning. O bloody and Pharisaic counsel! Christ, to wipe away our shame and death, gave himself up to shame and death: these men have [taken] the shame, with which they [were held] by the

<meta>This page continues the dialogue ("Te samen sprekinge") between "Vaticanus" (the pseudonym for Sebastian Castellio) and the positions held by John Calvin and the Swiss Reformed churches regarding the execution of Michael Servetus in 1553. who were burdened by the Papists, now wish to purify themselves with someone else’s blood. Woe to you, you hypocritical murderers! If you truly wish to wipe out this shame of yours, then kill yourselves, for (as you yourselves write) the Papists hold you to be heretics. Woe to you when men praise you, for their fathers did the same to the false prophets. Woe to you who followed the advice of the one who said that it was better <margin>John 11:50.</margin> for one man to die for the people, so that the whole nation should not perish <note>Vaticanus is comparing the Reformers' logic to that of Caiaphas, the High Priest who argued for the execution of Jesus to save the Jewish nation from Roman intervention.</note>. In this way, you wanted Servetus to be killed so that henceforth you would not bear a bad name among the Papists. Go forth then and make a covenant with your Pope; keep your bloody concord hidden no longer.

### Those of Zurich 153
May the Lord Jesus grant your Honorable Lordships <note>original: "E. E." standing for "Edelen Erfentfeste," a formal address to the Council of Geneva</note> wisdom and strength, along with a way, a means, and a righteous manner to do His will, to the honor of His name, and the preservation of the upright faith and of the Church.

### Vaticanus.
Jesus is a Savior, come to save and not to destroy. His wisdom is to wisely heal the sick, not to kill them. The way is to bear the cross, not to crucify others. The means and righteous manner to do His will is to let the weeds stand until the harvest <note>A reference to the Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13, a common argument for religious toleration</note>, and not to pull them out. The honor of His name is to follow His loving-kindness and His commandments, not to kill a human being. The preservation of the faith and of the Church is to follow Christ submissively through faith,

<column-break/>

and to protect the truth not with the (sword, but) with the weapons prescribed by the Apostle <note>A reference to the "Armor of God" in Ephesians 6, implying spiritual rather than physical warfare</note>. Take the sword away from these people, and they will no more remain standing than a vineyard that is not propped up.

### Those of Zurich 154.
Devoted to your Reverence, the Shepherds <note>Pastors</note> and Lecturers <note>Teachers/Professors</note> of the Church of Zurich.

### Vaticanus
If this letter does not differ at all from the others (as Calvin has written), then it must be understood that the Shepherds and Servants of the other three churches also wrote and advised exactly the same. This is a sentiment that Calvin also secretly leaves in the hearts of the readers, but he does so so evasively <note>original: "loselijck," meaning loosely or craftily</note> that, if he were accused, he could deny it. Since they say "the Shepherds and Lecturers," it seems that this must be understood as applying to them all, since Calvin said that they all wrote unanimously. Let us then think that the Shepherds and Lecturers of all the other churches also wrote in this way, and let us wonder at this unity and harmony of theirs in the killing of a man, while they are nonetheless so discordant in many other things.

That which Calvin disputes further against Servetus I shall pass by, since I do not have Servetus’s books. For one cannot judge his teaching from the accuser’s books any better than one can judge the Gospel from the writings of the Jews. An enemy can easily [distort] the passages of...

<summary>Vaticanus (Castellio) condemns the Swiss churches for sacrificing Servetus's life to gain favor with Catholics, arguing that true Christian "preservation of faith" involves spiritual weapons rather than the executioner's sword. He highlights the irony of Protestant churches being unified only in their desire to kill a "heretic" while remaining divided on most other doctrines.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the concluding arguments of the author (likely Sebastian Castellio writing under the pseudonym Vaticanus) regarding the case of Michael Servetus and the authority of church leaders.</meta>

### Vaticanus and Calvin.

to disparage someone’s books) and render them hateful / and it is an old proverb / <note>original: "Audi alteram partem"</note> **Hear the other side**. Hear also the other party. Therefore I suspend my judgment on the teaching of **Servetus** <note>Michael Servetus (1511–1553), a Spanish theologian executed in Geneva for heresy</note> / until his books come to light / if indeed they shall ever appear / for I am so minded / that I wish neither to protect his errors / nor to lightly believe the accusations of others against him.

To make an end / I have not intended in this my writing to take away from the **good Shepherds** <note>A common metaphor for ministers or church leaders</note> their authority or their dignity <note>original: "onsienlijckheyt," referring to the respect and status they command</note> / but rather I hold good shepherds in such esteem / that I believe / that whoever does not obey them / cannot be saved.

<column-break/>

**Good Shepherds** I call those / who follow the supreme Shepherd, **Christ** / to whom Christ / when he sends them out / speaks thus: *He who hears you, hears me.* Such as these offer their souls for their sheep / and do their utmost to save / not to destroy. These, I say, one must obey just as Christ himself. But that one should—I say—obey the evil and bloody shepherds, who do not tend the sheep / but feed themselves with the fat of the sheep / and thirst for blood / especially in the shedding of blood / that I deny: and pray the **Lord of the Harvest** / that he send out **Laborers** into his **Vineyard**. Amen.

<summary>In this concluding section, the author refuses to judge Michael Servetus without reading his original works and argues that while true, Christ-like ministers deserve absolute obedience, "bloody" leaders who advocate for execution should be resisted.</summary>

## ->Those of Zurich 153<- <note>Refers to section 153 of Calvin's "Defensio," which contained the official response from the Zurich ministers.</note>

### ->Vaticanus<-

Jesus is a Savior, come to save, not to destroy. His wisdom is to heal the sick wisely, not to kill them. The way is to carry the cross, not to crucify others. The means and righteous manner to do His will is to let the weeds stand until the harvest, and not to uproot them. <note>A reference to the Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13.</note> The honor of His name is to follow His kindness and commandments, not to kill a man.
