## ->Those of Zurich 154<-

Devoted to your Reverence, the Shepherds and Lecturers <note>original: "Leesmeesters," meaning academic teachers of theology</note> of the Church of Zurich.

### ->Vaticanus<-

If this letter does not differ at all from the others (as **Calvin** has written), then one must understand that the Shepherds and Servants of the other three churches <note>Bern, Basel, and Schaffhausen</note> have all written and advised the same. This sentiment Calvin also secretly leaves in the hearts of the readers, but he does it so slyly <note>original: "loselijck," meaning loosely or craftily</note> that, if accused, he could deny it. Since they say "the Shepherds and Lecturers," that seems intended to mean all of them; since Calvin said they all wrote in unity, let us then assume that the Shepherds and Lecturers of all other churches also wrote so. Let us marvel at this unity and agreement of theirs in the killing of a man, while they are nonetheless so discordant in many other things.

That which Calvin disputes further against Servetus I pass by, since I do not have Servetus’s books. There is an old proverb: <note>original: "Audi alteram partem"</note> *Hear also the other side.* For that reason, I suspend my judgment of Servetus's teaching until his books come to light—if indeed they ever shall—for I am of such a mind that I neither wish to protect his errors nor lightly believe the accusations made by others against him.

And I pray to the Lord of the Harvest that He sends workers into His vineyard. **Amen.**

### A short account of the Death of Michael Servetus, Spaniard, alias de Renes <note>Likely a variation of Michael de Villeneuve, the alias Servetus used while living in France.</note>, of Aragon, who was burned alive in Geneva for his Faith: In the Year 1553, on the 27th of October.

While Servetus was busy having his books regarding the Trinity <note>Servetus published "Christianismi Restitutio" (The Restoration of Christianity), which challenged the traditional doctrine of the Trinity.</note> printed at Vienne in the Dauphiné <note>A former province in southeastern France.</note>, it happened that a man from Paris, living in Geneva, wrote a letter to a certain friend of his who was living in Paris, in which letter he wrote among other things the following:

> We keep no Heretics among us, but you people tolerate among you Michael Servetus, the very greatest Heretic, who has books printed that are full of errors. He is now in Vienne in such a House, etc.

Those who have seen this letter believe that Calvin wrote it, because the style is very similar to his own; and because that man from Paris did not possess such eloquence that he could have been able to write such a clever Letter. The man says indeed that he wrote it: but others who have seen the Letter tell us that the Letter was written out of cunning, so that it would come into the hands of the Magistrate, and so forward into the hands of the Cardinal of Tournon <note>François de Tournon, a powerful French cardinal and diplomat known for his efforts to suppress heresy.</note>.

<column-break/>

There are also those <gloss>n</gloss> <note>The <gloss>n</gloss> indicates a missing letter or abbreviation mark in the original Dutch.</note> who say that Calvin himself allegedly wrote to the Cardinal in this manner:

> If religion were as dear to your heart as you pretend it is, you would not tolerate Servetus among you, etc.

However it may be or not be, that letter was no sooner read than Servetus was imprisoned at Vienne, along with the Book-printer whom he had commissioned to print his book. But since he later escaped <gloss>n</gloss> <note>The <gloss>n</gloss> marks an abbreviation for "from prison."</note> from the prison, he came <gloss>n</gloss> <note>The <gloss>n</gloss> marks an abbreviation for "came."</note> to Geneva and on the same day, being Sunday, heard the Sermon there in the afternoon: And as he went to sit in the Church among the other people, a little before they began to Preach, he was recognized by some who immediately brought the news to Calvin. Calvin immediately reported the man to the Magistrate as a criminal, or at least orchestrated and brought this about <gloss>n</gloss> <note>The <gloss>n</gloss> marks an abbreviation for "brought this about."</note> through others, desiring that Servetus should be thrown into prison for his Heresy. Upon which the Magistrate gave as an <gloss>n</gloss> <note>The <gloss>n</gloss> marks an abbreviation for "answer."</note> answer that...

<header>A short account</header>

it was not permitted to arrest a man in a free city <note>Geneva was a "free city," meaning it governed itself independently.</note> unless there was someone to accuse him and—as the saying goes—"put foot to piece" <note>original: "voet by stuck stellen." This refers to the *lex talionis* or "law of retaliation," a legal custom where an accuser had to submit to the same imprisonment as the accused to ensure the charge was not frivolous.</note>; that is, to allow himself to be placed in prison alongside the accused. Calvin prompted his servant to be the accuser of Servetus. This servant had formerly been the servant of a distinguished nobleman named Falesius <note>Jacques de Bourgogne, Lord of Falais, a former friend of Calvin who later fell out with him.</note>. Calvin once held this Falesius in such high esteem because of Religion that he praised him exceedingly in a certain letter. But after this Falesius appeared to be a supporter of a certain doctor named Hieronymus <note>Hieronymus Bolsec, a physician who publicly challenged Calvin’s doctrine of predestination in 1551.</note> (who was imprisoned because of the doctrine of Predestination, and because he had changed his views on that point), he was condemned as a Heretic by Calvin in a public assembly. Calvin prompted this servant and sent him to go and accuse Servetus. And Servetus, having been called out of the sermon and having made his name known, was thrown into prison, as was Calvin’s servant, who was released on bail shortly thereafter. Servetus was so guarded and locked up that no one (unless he was of great standing) was allowed to visit him, or he had to be a special friend of Calvin. While Servetus was imprisoned, the Magistrate sent a messenger to Vienne <note>Vienne, France, where Servetus had previously been condemned in absentia.</note> to fetch the sentence which those of Vienne had pronounced against Servetus. Those of Vienne gave the messenger the sentence, further adding this: that Servetus, through the initiative and arrangement of the chief Preacher of Geneva <note>Referring to John Calvin.</note>,

<column-break/>

had come into the hands of those of Vienne. Once this sentence had arrived, the messenger was sent to the Swiss Churches in Bern, Zurich, Schaffhausen, and Basel, carrying Servetus's book <note>The *Christianismi Restitutio*.</note> as well as the accompanying accusations of the Preachers and some letters from the Magistrate of Geneva to the Preachers of the aforementioned Churches, or to the Magistrates there, in order to thus gather a collection of Opinions regarding Servetus’s case.

In the meantime, a certain Thomas, who was a servant of Robertus Stephanus <note>Robert Estienne, the famous Parisian printer who fled to Geneva.</note>, was sent to Frankfurt; he burned Servetus’s books, which had been bought there at the Fair <note>The Frankfurt Book Fair, the most important trade fair for books in early modern Europe.</note>, so that they would not come among the people. The aforementioned messenger brought back Letters from the Preachers of the prominent Churches in which Servetus was judged to be a Heretic. Upon this, the Magistrate of Geneva was immediately made to assemble regarding Servetus's case. Amedeus Rozrius <note>Ami Perrin (also known as Amedeus), the Captain-General and a leader of the faction opposed to Calvin.</note>, the Captain, and another who was then the oldest and most prominent burgomaster, when they saw that the majority of the council was inclined to take the man's life, did not wish to enter the judgment and flatly refused to give their votes to shed his blood. Some others did the same, but the rest all condemned him together, though one in this way and another in that: some voting that he should be banished, some that he should be put in perpetual imprisonment; however, the majority of the votes carried the day, namely, that
<meta>catchword: men (they)</meta>

### Concerning the Death of Servetus.

he was to be burned, on the condition that if he did not wish to recant everything and retract his words <note>original: "door sijnen hals halen," literally "to pull through his throat," an idiom meaning to take back one's words or recant.</note>.

Many say that Cellarius <note>Martin Borrhaus (1499–1564), also known as Cellarius, a professor of theology at Basel who was known for his more moderate views on heresy compared to other reformers.</note>, the most prominent professor of Holy Theology of that city, never wished to vote for the death of Servetus or any other heretics. The same sentiment was also held by some assistant ministers of the same city, who for that reason were not requested to state their judgment concerning Servetus. Thus, he was brought before the Court <term>Vierschare: a historical term for a tribunal or court of justice, named for the four-sided enclosure where judges sat.</term> and was condemned there to be burned to ashes. Servetus, having heard this sentence, earnestly pleaded with the Magistrate that he might be killed by the sword, so that he might not be brought to hopelessness and desperation by the great and terrible pains, and thus risk losing his soul. He stated that he had sinned unknowingly, if indeed he had strayed in anything, and that his intention and will had never been anything other than to promote the Glory of God. This plea was presented more broadly and clearly to the Magistrate by Farel <note>Guillaume Farel (1489–1565), a key figure in the Reformation in Geneva and a close associate of Calvin.</note> (a Minister in Geneva); but Servetus did not obtain his request from the Magistrate, and was thus led away to the place of execution, occasionally crying out:

> O God, preserve my soul! O Jesus, son of the eternal God! Have mercy on me.

Having arrived at the place where he was to die, he knelt down, praying to God, and lay flat on the ground for a short time. Meanwhile, Farel 

<column-break/>

addressed the people as follows:

> You see how great a power Satan has when he has taken possession of someone: this is a very learned man, and perhaps he intended to do well, but now the devil has overcome and possessed him, which could happen to all of you as well.

Servetus having stood up in the meantime, Farel requested of him that he should say something. But he, sighing heavily, cried out, **"O God, O God"**; upon which Farel asked if he had nothing else to say, and he answered: "What else should I speak of now, but of God?" Farel exhorted him, if he had a wife or children and wished to make any Will, that a Notary Public was at hand; but he answered nothing to this. And so he was brought to the wood, which consisted of oaken faggots <term>Mutsaerden: bundles of firewood or brushwood used for a stake.</term> that were still green and full of leaves, with small logs on top. Servetus was placed on a block that lay on the ground, yet in such a way that his feet touched the earth. Above his head, some straw or leaves were placed, which were sprinkled with gunpowder. His body was fastened to a stake with an iron chain, and his neck with a four or five-fold cord, though somewhat loose. He begged the executioner that he would not inflict a long death and pain upon him. Immediately, the executioner lit the fire first in front of him, and then all around. As soon as the man saw the fire, he cried out so fearfully that all the people were terrified by it

# Dialogue

...and since the fire for a long time burned very weakly, the people themselves began to throw much wood onto it; he cried out most piteously:
> Jesus, son of the eternal God, have mercy on me! <note>original: "Iesu sone des eeuwigen Gods ontfermt u mijnder." This phrasing was highly significant; Servetus was executed for denying the eternal divinity of Christ, so he called him the "Son of the eternal God" rather than the "eternal Son of God," maintaining his theological position even at the stake.</note>
After he had been in the fire for about half an hour, he finally gave up his spirit.

There are some who say for certain that Calvin, hiding somewhat under his cloak, grinned when he saw Servetus being led to his churchyard <note>original: "Kerckhof," here likely referring to the burial ground or the outskirts of the city where the execution took place.</note>. This entire proceeding has horrified many godly people and has given a scandal above all scandals, because the godly rebuke it in many points.

**Firstly:** That a man was killed in Geneva for the sake of Religion; for they deny that one may kill anyone for matters of Religion. And when people argue against them and bring forth from the Old Testament that one must kill false Prophets, they then bring against it from the New Testament that one must not uproot the weeds before the Harvest <note>A reference to the Parable of the Tares (Matthew 13:24–30), a common argument for religious toleration at the time.</note>. When he says that the Swiss Churches voted for Servetus’s death, they answer in return that those Churches could not be Judges since they were the accusers; for Servetus had accused them (the Church of Geneva). Furthermore, they wonder that Calvin went to complain to those Churches about another man’s death—churches whose teachings he himself had previously condemned: for in a certain French booklet on the Lord’s Supper <term>Avontmael: The Lord's Supper or Eucharist.</term>, he openly con-

<column-break/>

-demned Zwingli <note>original: "Swinglius." Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the Swiss Reformer. Calvin disagreed with Zwingli's symbolic interpretation of the Eucharist.</note>. If they then could err regarding the Lord’s Supper, they could also certainly err in the killing of heretics.

**Secondly:** That Servetus was brought to his end through Calvin’s management; for in order to do away with his enemy, he set up an accuser from his own kitchen <note>The "accuser from the kitchen" refers to Nicolaas de la Fontaine, Calvin’s personal servant, who was used to bring the formal charges against Servetus so Calvin could avoid the legal risks of the "lex talionis" (the law of retaliation).</note>, who was nevertheless a man who had not the slightest understanding of the disputes that occurred between Servetus and others. They say this is entirely contrary to the true Christian nature, and differs as much as Heaven does from the earth, since Christ came not to destroy, but to save.

**Thirdly:** That he was put to death so cruelly, even though he himself had pleaded for the sword. This unheard-of cruelty would make anyone think that those of Geneva were seeking the favor of the Pope again, and wished to show by deed that they are not as poorly minded toward him in their hearts as they loudly proclaim with their mouths.

**Fourthly:** That the Evangelicals <note>Meaning the Protestant Reformers.</note> conspired with the Papists <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics.</note> to kill this man, from which some believe that a certain friendship was made, just as was made between Pilate and Herod through the passion of Christ <note>A biblical reference to Luke 23:12, where former enemies Pilate and Herod became friends during the trial of Jesus.</note>.

**Fifthly:** That they went and burned Servetus’s books, which (like many other things) they seem to have learned from the Pope. Indeed, what is more,

# On the Death of Servetus

If Calvin’s doctrine of Predestination and election <note>The belief that God has pre-determined who will be saved and who will be damned before they are even born.</note> is true, then they should not have had any fear that Servetus would have led anyone astray—if it is indeed true that the elect cannot be deceived. And what does it matter what the reprobate <note>term: reprobate (the "verworpenen" or those not chosen by God for salvation)</note> believe, since they must be deceived and damned regardless? Furthermore, if sins occur by necessity through God’s own driving force, then it is certain that Servetus could not have failed to do what he did. And if the Calvinists had been cheated and led astray, that too would have had to happen; if they were not led astray, that also could not have happened otherwise.

Sixthly, that the deceased Servetus was, moreover, condemned and damned to

## Here follow some accounts and actions which John Calvin performed in Geneva.

In the beginning, when William Farel <note>original: "Guylhelmus Farellus"</note> was preaching the Gospel in Geneva, along with Pierre Viret <note>original: "Petrus Viretus"</note>, John Calvin came to them from France. Having been accepted by Farel as a fellow minister, he also began to preach. In the meantime, a dispute arose between the preachers and the citizenry; the citizens wanted the Lord’s Supper distributed with unleavened bread <note>Bread made without yeast, similar to the Catholic practice or the Reformed style in Bern.</note>, in order to be in agreement with those of Bern. The preachers wanted to distribute it with leavened bread. The city was divided into two factions; one was against

<column-break/>

hell in public sermons; indeed, he was so condemned that the good Farel was heard thundering about it, saying that people trembled and shook because of it.

Seventhly, that Calvin now appears to write against the dead man, in which it seems he follows the Jews, who, when Christ was dead, asked Pilate (calling Christ a deceiver) that his body should be guarded. Likewise, some say that Calvin does not fear that Servetus’s body will be taken away (for through Calvin’s cautious planning, precautions have already been taken), but he fears that Servetus’s ashes will begin to speak. For otherwise, had he wanted to write against him, he should have done so while the man was still alive, so that he might have been able to defend himself—something which is granted even to a murderer.

the preachers, under the leadership of one Jan Philippe; the other was with the preachers, under the leadership of one Balthasar. But as the faction against the preachers was the strongest, it was resolved in the council that the preachers should be expelled from the city, unless they were willing to compromise. And so, a number of them were put out of the city, and Calvin went to Strasbourg <note>original: "Straesburg"</note>, where he became a Professor of Holy Theology. In the meantime, Jan Philippe was beheaded for a manslaughter that he had committed. Because of this, the other faction gained the upper hand.

<meta>This page continues the historical account of John Calvin's career in Geneva. The previous page ended with a conflict over the type of bread used in the Lord's Supper, which led to the temporary exile of the preachers. This page picks up with the city inviting Calvin back from Strasbourg and his subsequent consolidation of power through religious and civil reforms.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<columns>2</columns>
<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>A short account</header>

...received; and they immediately sent messengers to Strasbourg to call Calvin back again. He finally, after much pleading and praying, came; and he immediately began to put everything on a new footing. First, he went about making a new form for Baptisms and prayers, rejecting the others that had been kept there before. After that, he provided and introduced a new Catechism <note>A book of instruction in the form of questions and answers used to teach Christian doctrine.</note>. Finally, he wrote certain rules and statutes for the church of Geneva, and had them approved and confirmed by the Authorities or Councils, and thus established a Consistory <note>term: Consistory (a church court composed of ministers and elders intended to monitor and punish the moral conduct of the citizens)</note>. The preachers whom he found there, along with two schoolmasters, he drove out one after another—sometimes for this reason, sometimes for that—excepting only his friend Viret <note>Pierre Viret (1511–1571), a close associate of Calvin and a prominent Reformer.</note>. He also introduced the singing of Psalms in the Church, and even established certain Psalms that were to be sung on specific days. He turned Wednesday afternoon into a Holy Day, so that no shops could be opened before the sermon, upon pain of a certain fine. He abolished Christmas Day, so that it would no longer be a Holy Day. He wrote several books which he sent through France and other foreign lands, through which he lured many people to Geneva, teaching that they could not live under the Pope with a good conscience. He always took the Magistracy <note>The civil government and city officials.</note> firmly under his arm, and always sought the friendship of the wealthiest and most powerful,

<column-break/>

relying upon whom he kept his enemies under the whip—now dragging one before the court, then shamelessly mocking another from the pulpit <note>original: "door den Mostaert sleyende" (literally "beating through the mustard," an idiom for harsh public rebuking or mockery)</note>. Some he caused to be banished, and others to be imprisoned; indeed, he forced many to come and beg his forgiveness. He finally brought the matter so far that, because there were some who had murmured against him, a gallows was erected the next day for the *Gervasians* (a part of the city so named) <note>Refers to the Saint-Gervais district, a neighborhood in Geneva known for its opposition to Calvin's strict rules.</note>, as a threat to punish all those who would once dare to speak out or stir. As if to say: whoever is not now in Calvin's favor must know that it will cost him his neck. But the worst of all is that Calvin so easily believes lies, and cannot bear for anyone to refute them. Whoever disturbs the man is a godless rogue, an Atheist, and an Epicurean <note>term: Epicurean (at this time, a slur for someone perceived as having no soul or being interested only in earthly pleasures, used against Calvin's critics)</note>. Whoever differs from him in opinion in any matter is a Heretic, and is painted so black that even someone who merely speaks to him once is considered no Christian. All those who arrive there, and especially the French, are immediately warned that they must be wary of the Heretics—that is, of those who are of the party against Calvin, such as Falesius, Hieronimus Medicus, Andreas Sebedeus, Marandus, Marcolfus, Castalio, Calius, and all the others besides <note>This list includes several scholars and reformers, most notably Sebastian Castellio, who broke with Calvin over the execution of Servetus and the issue of religious freedom.</note>. Thus, it is a mortal sin to hold speech with those people. And all these things happen under the title and cloak of Christ. But furthermore, he is a very industrious man, clever of mind (were it not for his bitter...

I have wished to portray the man in this way so that—since he boasts of having such excellent knowledge of the Holy Scriptures and thereby deceives many—people might look around and consider whether it is believable that God would have poured and revealed His holy secrets into such a soul. For God has always been accustomed to reveal the same to the humble and the small, and to hide them from the wise of this world.

### On the words of Paul
#### 2 Corinthians 10:3.

> The weapons of our warfare are not fleshly, but mighty through God, to the casting down of strongholds.

THE war that Paul waged consisted in this: that he had to fight and cast down the thoughts of men. That is, to speak and teach so concisely and powerfully that men, being convinced in their hearts by the truth, had to think to themselves: "The man speaks the truth, and his reasons are irrefutable." For this spiritual struggle, Paul used no fleshly, but only spiritual weapons. For just as he would be insane <note>original: *uytzinnigh*</note> who would think to withstand and overcome a sword or other weapon with his thoughts, so too must he be insane who thinks to overcome thoughts with the sword. Now, the power of a Christian Teacher <note>term: Teacher (in this context, a minister or theologian responsible for doctrine)</note> lies in this: that he must overcome thoughts. It follows, therefore, that he may use no other weapon than that by which the thoughts of men can be overcome. We have a fine example of this in the Emperor Nero, who, traveling through Greece, called for many musical contests of singers and players, against whom he tested and showcased his own skill at his own expense. When he surpassed other artists, he would...

# A Brief Account

->L iij<-

he was crowned. And that was the true nature of a lawful contest <note>The original Dutch *werpspel* refers to a game of chance or a competition of skill; here it signifies a fair debate or trial of ideas.</note>. But when he was defeated by them, he had them killed. This was surely not a craftsman being defeated, but a human being deprived of life; and through this foolish injustice, he brought it about that people, with a feigned mouth in his presence, praised him steadfastly as a victor. Even the other musicians, in order not to outshine him, from then on intentionally sang and played poorly. But although he was the most powerful prince in the world, he nevertheless did not conquer the thoughts of men, who at that time did nothing but regard him in silence as a fool and a senseless man, as we still do today.

On the other hand, the other craftsmen who surpassed him in knowledge and skill did not only conquer the thoughts of other people, but also the thoughts of Nero himself—so that his mind, even against his will, was forced to say: "These people sing and speak better than I." 

And it is much the same with religion. Many points are disputed there, and if we overcome someone <note>i.e., through better argument or scriptural proof</note>, then we are also crowned; that is a lawful contest. But if there is someone whom we cannot overcome, he is called a heretic and put to death. Now this is surely no heretic being conquered, but a human being deprived of life. And through this foolish injustice, we certainly achieve this: namely, that we are then called victors by people with a feigned voice, and that other people hereafter, in order not to also

<column-break/>

die, do not learn as well as they otherwise would. But however powerful we may also be, we nevertheless do not conquer the thoughts of men. Instead, we are held for fools by those living then who are not blind, as well as by posterity; and they pronounce such a judgment upon us as we do upon Nero and similar followers. For nothing is so hidden that it will not be discovered. 

God has promised such great power to those who would obey Him, that one of them shall put a hundred, and a hundred of them shall put ten thousand enemies to flight. And thus Paul fought, who with his divine weapons cast down not only a hundred, but the thoughts of all men. But we, alas, are busy with hundreds of us to deprive a single man of his life, and we defend Christ <note>original: *Christum*</note> with carnal weapons <note>Physical force or violence, as opposed to "spiritual weapons" like prayer and persuasion.</note>—so that people might well understand that we are stripped of spiritual weapons and have a carnal Christ. 

For since the Apostles, who planted the Church, were so successful with their spiritual weapons, it certainly appears that those who put carnal weapons to work do not sufficiently trust the spiritual ones; which they would nevertheless trust if they had them. Since the spiritual weapons are almighty, as shall appear in the future coming of the Lord, who shall overcome the Antichrist <note>The ultimate enemy of Christ in Christian eschatology.</note>, His greatest enemy, with no other weapons than with the spirit of His mouth. 

We read in Exodus 25 that the Tabernacle was made of the willing and spontaneous gifts and offerings of the people, without anyone being forced...

# On the Death of Servetus.
->I iiij<-

...nothing was given toward it. This must be understood concerning the Church, as appears first from the 110th Psalm, where Christ’s soldiers are said to be willing <note>Psalm 110:3 in many early translations describes the people of the Messiah as "volunteers" or "willing" in the day of his power.</note>: and furthermore from the Law, where it is commanded that the fearful shall depart, so as not to make the hearts of others faint in the battle <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 20:8, where those who are afraid are sent home so they do not discourage their fellow soldiers.</note>. In this way also must the willing soldiers of Christ fight, and be compelled by nothing else than God’s word. Those who serve otherwise, serve someone other than Christ. Therefore also the outcomes and the unseemly behaviors give enough to know what fruits such forced service brings forth.

### On the words of Paul
->Gal. 4:29–30.<-

Just as at that time, he who was born after the flesh persecuted him who was born after the Spirit, so it is also now. But what says the scripture? Cast out the bondwoman and her son: for the son of the bondwoman shall not inherit with the son of the free woman.

Consider these words of Paul, good reader! Ishmael, who was born after the flesh, persecuted Isaac, who was born after the Spirit. In the same way, carnal men now persecute spiritual men. It cannot be otherwise. For just as Isaac—because he was younger, and therefore also weaker—could not persecute Ishmael: so also now the Christians, because they were born after the pseudo-Christians <note>The original Dutch *weder-Christenen* refers to those who claim the name of Christ but lack His spirit; the author argues that the true, spiritual church is always the "younger" or "weaker" party in the eyes of the world.</note> and are weaker, cannot persecute the pseudo-Christians. And this same thing has been signified by many other examples. For neither did Abel persecute Cain, nor Noah the Giants, nor Lot the

<column-break/>

Sodomites, nor David Saul, nor the true Prophets the false ones, nor Christ and the Apostles the Scribes and Pharisees; but on the contrary. Indeed, even if there had been some who could have done so, they nevertheless did not do it: as specifically David. Saul pursued him like a flea, or a partridge in the mountains: David, having caught him once or twice when he was alone, could have killed him, but did not wish to. 

Thus today the godly are just like a flea, and are persecuted by the ungodly: and if it sometimes happens that they could harm the ungodly, they are so far from doing such a thing that they would rather be of benefit to them and seek their salvation; for they have learned that from their David. Just as, on the other hand, the others have learned from their father Saul to harm even those from whom they have received benefits. Here you have the signs of the godly and the ungodly. 

The ungodly are noble, rich, learned, powerful, mockers, persecutors, cruel, proud, famous, and ambitious: such were Cain, the giants, the Sodomites, Saul, the Egyptian sorcerers, the false prophets, the Scribes, the Pharisees, Herod, Pilate, Annas, Caiaphas, and the philosophers <note>In this context, the author uses "philosophers" to mean those who rely on worldly wisdom and human logic to justify persecution.</note>.

The godly are ignoble, poor, unlearned, weak, mocked, tormented, peaceful, gentle, humble, lowly, unknown, base and despised, nameless, the offscouring and refuse of the world <note>original: *uptvaegsel en afsnijdtsel* — echoing 1 Corinthians 4:13.</note>, despisers of their own honor, but seekers of the honor of God; they repay evil with good, bless those who curse them, and follow the Lamb...

# A short account
### [of the life and death of Servetus]

Jesus Christ through the way of the cross and of virtues. Now, place such a people before your eyes, Christian Reader, and consider if it is even possible that such a people, being scattered throughout the whole world, could persecute the ungodly—that is, the Electors <note>The original Dutch uses 'Keusen,' likely referring to the Electors of the Holy Roman Empire—the high-ranking princes who held temporal power.</note> and Lords of the world banding together against them. Since this is impossible (as it surely is impossible), then do not doubt for a moment whether those who persecute others for their faith's sake are the true Ishmaelites; for they follow the nature of their father, Ishmael <note>In the Book of Genesis, Ishmael was the son of the bondwoman Hagar, who was said to mock or persecute Isaac, the son of the free woman Sarah.</note>. But what says the Scripture? *Cast out the bondwoman and her son, for the son of the bondwoman shall not inherit with the son of the free woman.* What is the bondwoman? It is the Law, as Paul himself declares. The children of the bondwoman are those who wish to be under the Law—that is, under the letter—and therefore they persecute those who wish to live under the Spirit, just as Ishmael did to Isaac. And so that it may be understood that they are indeed true Ishmaelites, they appeal to the letter of the law, which commands that false prophets be put to death. They interpret "false prophets" as "heretics," and they call "heretics" all those who differ from them in the slightest degree. In this, they are the children of those who cried out: *We have a Law, and according to the Law he must die, because he made himself a son of God.* <note>A reference to the trial of Jesus in John 19:7.</note> Do you see how the children of the Law persecute the children of the Spirit, and to that end invoke the Law? What shall Sarah—that is, the free and spiritual Church—do now? She herself does not cast out the son of the bondwoman—that is, the servants of the letter—

<column-break/>

nor does Isaac cast out Ishmael, for he does not have the power. The godly do not cast out the ungodly, because they cannot. But Sarah prays to her husband—that is, the Church prays to God—that He should cast out the persecuting Law—that is, the letter—and its sons, the persecutors, so that they are not heirs of the Heavenly Kingdom. Thus it does not belong to the Church to cast the ungodly out of the heavenly inheritance, but to her husband, Christ, who shall cast them out with the rod of his mouth <note>original: "roede sijns mōts"</note>, when he shall say: *Depart, you cursed ones, into eternal fire.* To pass this sentence belongs neither to Sarah nor to Isaac, but to Christ alone. Let those who condemn heretics take heed of what they do; for heretics are not usually treated the same way as murderers. For even regarding murderers, those who have them hanged often still have good hope for them, and pray to God for them, not thinking that they are necessarily damned to hell. But those who kill heretics hold that they are simultaneously rejected by God and condemned to eternal fire. By judging in this way, they climb onto the judgment seat of Christ and pronounce that terrible sentence: *Depart, you cursed ones, into eternal fire.* This may be spoken by no one other than Christ, and not before the Day of Judgment. For this reason it is called the Day of Judgment, because then—and not before—shall one be able to judge who is chosen or rejected. Just as Paul taught when he said: *Judge nothing before the time, until the Lord comes, who will bring to light the hidden things of darkness...*

<summary>The author argues that persecutors are "children of the Law" (Ishmaelites) who usurp Christ's authority by condemning "heretics" to hell before the Day of Judgment. He asserts that the true Church (Sarah/Isaac) suffers persecution but never inflicts it, leaving final judgment to God alone.</summary>

# Regarding Servetus’ Death.

...will bring to light, and will make manifest the counsels of the hearts <note>This is a continuation of a biblical reference to 1 Corinthians 4:5.</note>. Reflecting on these words myself, I can say nothing else than to warn all people: when one speaks of condemning someone as a **Heretic**—that is, as one sentenced to eternal fire and eternally rejected by God (for that is how people view heretics)—that they should then (I say) reflect seven times on what they are undertaking. And...

### The Translator
->in conclusion.<-

Do not be surprised, Christian reader, that although **Calvin** is portrayed so unfavorably here, I have nevertheless allowed this to be printed, as if it were contrary to Christian love, which covers a multitude of sins. For know that this was not done because of **Calvin's** person (for he is dead), but to weaken his authority (which we freely admit); that is to say, that authority which some overly fervent zealots and ambitious people exalt and lift too high. They seek to place him like a saint upon the altar, to be honored—even worshipped—by every man, as if he were the Church itself, and his opinion the faith of the Church. Indeed, they act as if no one could reach the knowledge of the truth or the understanding of Holy Scripture, or come to God, unless they suckled his breasts, read through his books, and had, as it were, consumed them. In short: as if he were both the sun and the moon by which the day and night are governed and from which they draw their light; and as if all men must walk in darkness who did not believe his teachings

<column-break/>

...therefore, for this reason, the aforementioned proverb (so that it may be read profitably) shall be set forth here once more in large letters:

->1 Corinthians 4:5<-
> Judge nothing before the time, until the Lord comes, who will bring to light what is hidden in darkness, and will reveal the counsel of the hearts.

...or who did not live according to his rules and statutes. No, it is not so; one finds in **Holland** and elsewhere enough people who need not yield to **Calvin** in either learning or godliness. That he was a learned man, I gladly acknowledge; but it would be a pity if "his rooster alone were king" <note>A Dutch idiom meaning he should not be the sole authority or the only voice heard.</note>. Indeed, it were to be wished that he had "shot the parrot" <note>A Dutch idiom derived from archery competitions; it means to have won the top prize or reached perfection.</note>, for then there would be some appearance of reason for exalting him so; but now (as is shown every day) he has made very gross blunders. His piety was also not so great that it should be forced upon everyone as a rule for their conduct, or that anyone should be moved and drawn to an upright Christian life through it. I will gladly give him the honor that is his due—namely, that in the beginning he helped much toward the **Reformation**—but what of it? He later committed dishonorable acts in that, through certain strange doctrines, bitter words, and cruel deeds, he greatly damaged the good progress of the **Gospel**. Therefore it was...

# A Short Account

it would have been a desirable thing if he had possessed as much of the spirit of Christ as he, in many places, accurately captured the true meaning of Christ. It is a bad thing that people hold such a "Saints' Day" for that man; and some **saint-gnawers** <term>santeknagers: a derogatory term for people perceived as hypocritically or excessively pious</term> have been on their feet all too early to place this man at the head of the table, without considering whether the host would recognize that guest as worthy enough. I fear that a voice shall come, saying: *give up this seat.* But you will say: let the dead bones rest, and do not judge the dead. Quite right; yet do not praise him too much, nor set him too high in heaven to the detriment of the truth and all lovers of the same. We would gladly remain silent, even help to smother his failings. But if you wish to **canonize** the dead man into a saint—who in his life was also a man just as we are—we shall strike him from the muster. If you wish to set him upon the altar, we shall cast him down from it. Is it permitted for you to rake up and pile on the failings of the Apostles, Prophets, and all holy men, so that they might serve as a **cloak** for your own coarse and clumsy sins? And further, to prove how sinful they were, in order to conclude that it is impossible for you to perfectly serve God according to His will revealed in the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelio"</note>? If so, do not be offended that we now pour that same sauce over you, to prove the great imperfection that existed in **John Calvin**, and further, that he is not such a great saint as you would gladly have us believe.

<column-break/>

Does your conduct not then conflict with love? As for ours, very little is at stake if we, in doing this, damage Calvin’s authority. But it matters exceedingly much that you so violate the authority and dignity of the Apostles, holding and portraying them as such abominable sinners. Do not say that this is a **lampoon** <note>original: "Pasquils," a form of anonymous satire</note> because we withhold our names. For that is not done out of shame, as we consider ourselves honorable, and if it came to it, we would certainly dare to reveal ourselves and stand by our words. But believe this: we withhold our names out of fear, worrying that our **heretic-murderers** would also shun us as heretics. Yes, we fear that the preachers of **Leeuwarden** <note>A major city in the northern Netherlands, then a center of strict Reformed orthodoxy</note> would pass a sentence upon us, declaring that we are the greatest plague in the Church of God and true instruments of the devil for the uprooting of the congregation. Whereupon they might send the **Executioner** to our house to "dispute" with us—one who would quickly **lay our little head in the rushes** <note>A Dutch idiom for being beheaded</note>. If you will renounce the killing of heretics, and condemn and strike out this sentence, and guarantee us **safe conduct** (not like the one **Jan Hus** had) <note>Jan Hus was a reformer promised safe passage to the Council of Constance in 1415, only to be arrested and burned at the stake</note>, then we shall reveal our names. Otherwise, we shall follow the good lesson of our Lord Jesus when He says in Matthew 7:6: *Do not give what is holy to the dogs; nor cast your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet, and turn and tear you in pieces.* And then you may dispute against the matter with reasons, and not with the Executioner against the person.
->THE END.<-
